<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of dioptrics, likely from Johannes Kepler's 1611 work. It focuses on how the curvature of lenses (the "size of the circle") and their relative distances affect the magnification and clarity of an image, building upon previous propositions regarding the optics of telescopes.</meta>

<page-num>61</page-num>
<sig>H 3</sig>

### If a concave lens is placed near the eye, convex lenses formed from a large circle require a long distance from the concave lens and the eye, while those from a small circle require a short distance.

<note>original: "magno circulo... parvo". In 17th-century optics, a "large circle" refers to a lens with a large radius of curvature, meaning it is flatter. A "small circle" refers to a more sharply curved lens.</note>

For according to Proposition 109, the eye is near the <term>point of convergence: the focal point where light rays meet</term>, and according to Proposition 111, the concave lens—being of the same type throughout—remains at the same interval from the convergence point of all convex lenses. However, these convergence points are at different distances from their respective convex lenses: for they are far from lenses made from a large circle, and closer for those from a small circle, according to Proposition 39. Since subtracting equal amounts from unequal ones leaves unequal results, and the interval between the concave and convex lenses—given that the distance from the concave lens to the convergence point is always the same—is less than the interval between the convex lens and that same convergence point: therefore, the concave lens (along with the eye) will be further away from a convex lens of a larger circle than from one of a smaller circle.

## PROPOSITION CXIII.

Given a convex lens, a concave lens of a smaller circle represents visible objects as larger, while one of a larger circle represents them as smaller.

For according to Proposition 110, concave lenses of a small circle must be moved further away from the convex lens to achieve the necessary distance for clear vision for the eye they are placed against. But according to Proposition 22, the further the eye recedes from the convex lens toward the point of convergence, the larger the objects appear. Therefore, an eye using a concave lens of a smaller circle sees objects distinctly and as larger than it does with a concave lens of a larger circle.

->A diagram showing two sets of converging light rays. On the left, rays pass through an opening labeled D-E and converge to a point F. On the right, rays originate from a line A-B, pass through an opening labeled I-K, and converge to a point C. The points G and H are also marked near I-K.<-

## PROPOSITION CXIV.

A concave lens, when moved just a very short distance further away from the convex lens, greatly increases the size of visible objects.

For according to Proposition 109, in the following diagram, G H and I K are very close to points C and F. Indeed, once such a very short distance as I F is traversed and the eye at I K is placed at F, the magnitude of a single point, from which all rays [flow] into D E... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "influxunt" (flow into) indicates the continuation on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page explains how the curvature of lenses determines the physical length of a telescope and the resulting magnification, noting that sharper curves and specific distances significantly increase the size of the observed object.</summary>
<keywords>optics, magnification, convex lens, concave lens, radius of curvature, focal point, dioptrics, Kepler</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof regarding the magnification of lenses, specifically comparing how the curvature (the "circle") of a lens affects the size of the image produced when paired with a concave eyepiece.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<sig>Nans</sig>

*...flow in, it increases to a certain size, indeed equal to the whole lens D E, so that a single point is seen at the angle D F E—this represents infinite magnification. Thus, with a very slight adjustment, a great effect is produced.* <note>The author is describing how a small change in the position or curvature of a lens can lead to a massive increase in magnification.</note>

## PROPOSITION 115.

When a concave lens <note>a "lente cava" is a diverging lens, like those used today for nearsightedness</note> is placed very close to the eye, then among convex lenses <note>magnifying lenses</note>, the one curved from a smaller circle represents visible objects as smaller, and the one from a larger circle represents them as larger.

Let there be a convex lens A B, with a larger radius A C, and a lens D E with a smaller radius D F. Therefore, C and F will be the points of convergence <note>original: "puncta concursus"; we now call these the focal points</note> according to Proposition 39. On both sides, let there be one concave lens, G H or I K, which, when placed close to the eye, causes a certain divergence of parallel rays through its specific portion. Since the concave lens is the same in both cases, G H and I K will be in the same position relative to the focal points C and F, according to Proposition 111. The eye is indeed very close to the lens on both sides, by our assumption. 

Therefore, when the equal segments G C and I F are subtracted from the unequal lengths A C and D F, the remaining lengths A G and D I will be in a greater proportion to one another. Thus, the convex lens A B is removed further from the concave lens G H and the eye, in its own proportion, than the convex lens D E is from the concave lens I K and the eye. And G H (with the eye) is closer to point C in the proportion of A B C than I K (with the eye) is to point F in the proportion of D E F. Therefore, larger images are represented through A B and G H than through D E and I K, according to Proposition 32. And indeed, with a very slight change in proportion, the images become much larger, according to Proposition 114.

This principal Proposition was once highly obscure. The reason for this is that if the ratio of A C to C G had been the same as that of D F to F I, then the longer distance A G would have done nothing to increase the size of the visible objects. Everything would have been equal on both sides according to Proposition 83.

## PROBLEM 116.

To represent visible objects as large as one desires.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical analysis of optical instruments, specifically focusing on the relationship between lens curvature, tube length, and image clarity. It refers back to previous principles (Propositions 113 and 115) to explain how to manipulate telescope magnification.</meta>

For it is clear from Propositions 113 and 115 that by increasing the proportion of the circles of the cavity <note>the concavity of the lens</note> and the convexity, the visible objects are enlarged.

## 117. PROBLEM.
**To represent visible objects with an equal increase in magnitude using unequal distances between the lenses—that is, using tubes of different lengths.**

According to 113 and 115, ensure that there is the same proportion between the cavities themselves, the convexities, and the distances between the lenses, even if the convex lenses themselves are dissimilar to one another.

## 118. PROBLEM.
**To represent objects as larger using shorter tubes.**

If the convex lens is smaller, but the proportion between the convexity and the cavity <note>the concavity</note> is greater than in a longer instrument, then larger objects will be represented by the shorter instrument, according to 113 and 115. <note>This explains how a short telescope can have higher magnification than a long one if the lenses are curved more aggressively.</note>

## 119. PROPOSITION.
**When a concave lens is in place, visible objects are represented more clearly or more strongly with a larger or wider convex lens than with a smaller one.**

For more light is spread from a single point (as seen in the nearby diagram) through a larger width AB than through a smaller width DE. All that light is indeed gathered into a single point, C or F. Therefore, the image <note>original: *pictura*</note> is stronger in C than in F; likewise, the eye in the space GH receives more rays than in IK. 

By the "size" of the convex lens, you should understand this according to Proposition 30 as referring to the physical body of the lens <note>its diameter or aperture</note>, not its geometric shape <note>the degree of curvature</note>.

## 120. PROPOSITION.
**When a convex lens is in place, visible objects are represented more clearly or more strongly through the cavity <note>concavity</note> of a larger circle than through the cavity of a smaller one.**

<summary>This page provides technical instructions for adjusting telescope magnification and image brightness by varying lens curvature and diameter. It establishes that a larger lens diameter (aperture) produces a brighter "picture" by gathering more light rays into a single point.</summary>
<keywords>optics, telescope construction, lens magnification, convex lens, concave lens, light refraction, image clarity, aperture</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of optical refraction through lenses, specifically focusing on how the size and curvature of a lens affect the clarity and strength of the image perceived by the human eye.</meta>

64

For a small lens placed very close to the eye causes a proper divergence of rays with only a small part of itself. *Even if many rays from a single point shine upon it and radiate from a large portion of the convex lens, most of them, however, wander away from the eye due to the excessive refraction caused by the sides or the rim of the concave lens (as in the diagram for Proposition 100 <note>The text uses the Roman numeral "C" for 100.</note>, where FG deviates from HI, the width of the pupil). Indeed, only a few radiations enter the eye, and those are entirely close to the perpendicular <note>In this context, "perpendicular" refers to the central axis of the lens or the direct line of sight.</note>, thus arriving from a small portion of the convex lens. Therefore, according to Proposition 119, vision through a lens with a small circle of concavity is weak. The same occurs if a narrow portion is taken from the concavity of even a large circle and is smaller than the pupil.*

### PROPOSITION CXXI.

**Of a portion of a hemisphere viewed through lenses, the middle part and the part closest to the perpendicular are seen more clearly and strongly than the surrounding rim.**

The cause is evident to the eye in the diagram for Proposition 70, in which the width of the pupil is marked QG. For the eye placed at QG—whether naked or with a concave lens held before it—intercepts all the radiations of the middle point E between EAQ and EBG. However, it does not intercept all the rays of point C, but only a part of the pencil of light <term>pencil of light (penicilli): a bundle of light rays originating from a single point and diverging or converging together</term> CAFBC (specifically, that which is between CA and ICHG). That which is now between CHG and CBF wanders away from the pupil QG. Therefore, since E is seen through AB, and C through AH, then according to Proposition 119, E will be seen more strongly and clearly than C.

### CXXII.

**With a narrow portion of a convex lens, other things being equal, visible objects are represented more distinctly; with a wide portion, more confusedly.**

For those rays which radiate into the eye through a large portion of the convexity, they radiate more strongly according to Proposition 119; by this strength, first the colors of the rainbow <note>original: "Iridis colores"; this is an early description of chromatic aberration, where light passing through the edges of a lens splits into colors.</note> and then clouds <note>original: "nebulæ"; refers to blurring or a hazy "halo" effect around an image.</note> are excited. For the concave and net-like tunic <note>original: "retiformis tunica"; the retina, which was described in early anatomy as a net-like web.</note> of the eye is full of spirit <note>original: "spiritu"; in 17th-century physiology, "animal spirits" were thought to be the subtle fluids that transmitted sensations through the nerves.</note>, and although it may be touched by only a point, nevertheless, if that point is immoderately bright from the meeting of many rays, the spirit in some [part]...

<summary>This page explains why the center of a lens provides a clearer image than the edges and how wider convex lenses can cause visual distortions like color fringing (chromatic aberration) and blurring.</summary>
<keywords>refraction, convex lens, concave lens, pupil, retinal tunic, visual spirits, chromatic aberration, Proposition 121, Proposition 122</keywords>

# JOHANNES KEPLER’S
## MATHEMATICIAN to His Sacred Imperial Majesty <note>Kepler served as the Imperial Mathematician to Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II.</note>
# DIOPTRICS <note>The title *Dioptrice* refers to the study of the refraction of light through lenses, as opposed to "Catoptrics," the study of reflections from mirrors.</note>

->A demonstration of those things which happen to sight and visible objects<-
->because of the lenses <note>original: "conspicilla"</note> invented<-
->not so long ago.<-

<page-num>86</page-num>
<meta>catchword: Having placed,</meta>

For the <term>pencils of light</term> <note>original: "penicilli"; these are bundles of light rays originating from a single point</note> at the sides of the concave lens are refracted entirely and obliquely: toward the left on the left side, and toward the right on the right side. For suppose, in the diagram for proposition C, that the line A B K F is the center line of one pencil of light coming into the center of the pupil; that line is refracted outward to the left through points B and K, because B K is the left part of the lens. Therefore, if the eye is moved from the middle of the hollow lens to the left side K, the point A, seen through the straight line F K M, will be thought to be at position M, which is further to the right, according to Proposition 19.

## 124. PROBLEM.

To skillfully estimate the size of an enlarged image.

Let the left eye be directed freely at a visible object without an instrument, while the right eye views the same object through the lenses. Since the left eye is directed at the visible object, and the right eye—by its own nature—always remains parallel to the left (according to Proposition 57), it will remain so even if it is covered as it now reads through the instrument; for the parallel direction of the eyes is natural. Therefore, the right eye will be directed as if toward the visible object itself, whether the <term>instrumental image</term> <note>the image of the object as seen through the telescope</note> appears lower or higher than the actual thing seen by the left eye. For according to Proposition 62, the right eye will indeed see the enlarged image of that object toward which it is directed through its association with the left eye, but it will not, for that reason, always see it with the same part of itself that the left eye uses to see the object.

Therefore, when the images appear to differ in their position, by moving the convex lens of the instrument here and there, and sometimes by applying the concave lens to the eye in a slightly different way, it can easily be brought about that both visible images of the same thing match each other in their position. Then, by overlapping the images, the degree to which one exceeds the other will become apparent.

->Thus far concerning the simple instrument: the **blending** follows.<- 
<note>original Greek: "κρυσις" (krasis), referring here to the combination or mixing of different types of lenses.</note>

# TO THE MOST REVEREND AND 
# MOST SERENE PRINCE, THE LORD,
### Lord Ernest, Archbishop of Cologne, Elector of the Holy Roman 
### Empire and Archchancellor throughout Italy, Bishop 
### of Liège, Administrator of the Monasteries of Hildesheim and 
### Freising, Prince in Stavelot, Count Palatine of the Rhine, Duke 
### of Upper and Lower Bavaria, Westphalia, and Angria, etc., Marquis 
-> of Franchimont, my most merciful <-
-> Lord. <-

---

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'R' containing floral and leaf patterns, beginning the main body of the dedication text.</image-desc>

Most Reverend and Most Serene Prince-Elector, most merciful Lord: Since in recent years the <term>dioptric reed</term> <note>original: "Arundo dioptrica"; a poetic or technical term for the telescope, referring to its tube-like shape and the use of lenses (dioptrics)</note> was added to the great heap of discoveries of this past century—an instrument by no means to be counted among common inventions—and while concerning it some contend for the prize of its first discovery, and others boast further of the perfection of the instrument (claiming that in the former case <term>Chance</term> <note>original: "casus"; suggesting the telescope was discovered by accident by spectacle-makers</note> played the greatest part, while in the latter <term>Reason</term> <note>original: "Ratio"; referring to the scientific refinement of the device</note> prevails); and while Galileo truly celebrates a most brilliant triumph over its revealed use in searching out the secrets of Astronomy; as one for whom diligence supplied the plan, and fortune did not deny success: I, led by a certain honorable emulation <note>A sense of scholarly rivalry or the desire to match the achievements of others</note>, have opened a new field for Mathematicians to exercise the power of their intellect—that is, the demonstrating by geometric law of the causes upon which such longed-for and manifold effects, in their delightful variety, depend. Since

<meta>catchword: for (enim)</meta>

<summary>This page is the formal dedication of Kepler's *Dioptrice* to Ernest of Bavaria, the Archbishop-Elector of Cologne. Kepler frames his work as a theoretical, geometric foundation for the telescope, distinguishing his "Reasoned" approach from the accidental discovery of the device and Galileo's observational triumphs.</summary>

<keywords>Ernest of Bavaria, dedication, Elector of Cologne, telescope, Galileo, dioptrics, geometry, discovery, astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Ernest of Bavaria. The previous page ended mid-sentence while discussing Galileo’s "triumph" with the telescope. This page completes the transition from Kepler's earlier work on optics to his current focus on the "Dioptrics" of the telescope.</meta>

...ph, <note>original: "triumphum"; completing the word "triumph" from the previous page.</note> for when six years ago I had published the *Optical Part of Astronomy* <note>original: "Opticam Astronomiae partem"; referring to Kepler’s 1604 work *Ad Vitellionem paralipomena*.</note>, in which I discussed the nature of vision by a new method and, as far as I know, was the first of all to argue such points about spectacles <note>original: "perspicillis"; used here to mean lenses or eyeglasses.</note> that remain unshaken to this day: it was fitting that I should show that those same foundations, upon which I had built the theory of vision and the effects of simple spectacles, were also sufficient to support the combination of different transparent lenses into a single <term>tube</term> <note>original: "arundinem"; literally "reed," a poetic or technical term for the telescope tube.</note>. And indeed, it could not be otherwise (which is the proof of truth) than that this demonstration be carried out by any principles other than those I have used. And since Euclid made <term>Catoptrics</term> <note>original: "Catoptricen"; from the Greek word for "mirror." The study of light reflection.</note> a branch of Optics, which deals with the reflected ray—a name derived from the primary instrument of this kind, the Mirror; and their wonderful and pleasing variety—after this example, the name **Dioptrics** <note>original: "Dioptrice"; from the Greek *dioptra*, meaning "to see through." The study of light refraction.</note> was born for my little book. For it deals primarily with the ray refracted by dense transparent media, both natural ones in the human eye and artificial ones in the variety of lenses; by this subject it is distinguished from Catoptrics, as one species from another. Yet Dioptrics is primary and Catoptrics secondary; because Catoptrics deals with images which cannot be understood at all without the knowledge of the eye that must be sought from Dioptrics.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For which reason I also repeated the nature of vision and of simple <note>original: "sim-[plicium]"; the catchword "plicium" completes the word "simplicium" (simple) at the start of the next page.</note>

<summary>Kepler defines the term "Dioptrics" as the study of refracted light through lenses and the eye, distinguishing it from "Catoptrics" (reflection). He asserts that his previous optical theories provide the only true scientific foundation for understanding how the telescope works.</summary>
<keywords>Dioptrics, Catoptrics, Euclid, refraction, reflection, lenses, human eye, telescope tube, Optical Part of Astronomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dedication to Ernest of Bavaria, Archbishop-Elector of Cologne. Kepler is explaining the pedagogical goals of his new work, "Dioptrice," specifically addressing the difficulty readers had with his previous book on optics.</meta>

)( 3

...ous <note>original: "plicium"; completing the word "simplicium" (simple) from the previous page's "varietatum."</note> varieties of simple **lenses** <note>original: "perspicillorum"; in this context, Kepler refers specifically to convex and concave lenses used in spectacles.</note>; both so that **Dioptrics** might be in some way complete, and because the principles of the Instrument <note>the telescope</note> are tied to the human eye, and the instrument itself is composed of simple lenses: so that one cannot be explained without the other. Finally, because some judged that I had treated these matters too obscurely in the **Optics** <note>referring to his 1604 work *Ad Vitellionem paralipomena*.</note>; so that for many, it was not a dullness of mind, but the fault of the teacher that acted as a barrier, preventing them from perceiving what was written and demonstrated. 

Therefore, to look out for them, I have presented some things here more briefly, others more extensively, and conceived some in different words; I have reviewed the definitions of terms, which I use with <term>geometrical freedom</term> <note>original: "geometrica libertate"; the practice of a mathematician defining terms specifically for the scope of their own proofs.</note>, in continuous numbering among the propositions at appropriate places; I have added more diagrams (which are the genuine letters of <term>Geometers</term> <note>original: "Geometrarum"; meaning mathematicians who specialize in geometry.</note>). By which effort, if I have not removed every obscurity, I hope that students of Philosophy will grant some indulgence to my weakness and will take this effort in good part.

Furthermore, I devoted myself to this concern at that time above all when my mind, numbed by a certain lamentable cold <note>Kepler may be referring to personal grief or the literal winter of 1610-1611, during which his wife and son fell ill.</note>, was warmed by the most generous Sun of Your Most Reverend and Serene Highness's presence, and His most merciful conversations and frequent encouragements, like some *Mercury* <note>the messenger of the gods, often associated with intellectual stimulation.</note>, roused me from sleep. Finally, [I was inspired by] the most delightful and simultaneously most ingenious **hand-operated machines** <note>original: "machinamenta manuaria"; specialized lathes or tools for grinding glass.</note> of His Noble Mathematician and Chamberlain, Lord **Johannes Zuckmesser**, and the most skillful polishing of glasses, by which [Your] Reverend Serene Highness...

->Tuam.<- <note>Catchword: "Your"</note>

<summary>Kepler explains that he has rewritten his optical theories to be more accessible, using more diagrams and clearer definitions to explain how lenses work together in a telescope. He credits the encouragement of his patron and the technical skill of the craftsman Johannes Zuckmesser for reviving his spirits during a difficult time.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedication to Ernest of Bavaria, Archbishop-Elector of Cologne. Kepler continues to explain why he is dedicating this specific work on optics to the Prince, shifting from the technical needs of the reader to the personal intellectual qualities of his patron.</meta>

...I saw that Your Highness was wonderfully delighted, they provoked me to an emulation of the same duty. Even if these singular causes did not impel me to dedicate this **Dioptrics** <note>original: "Dioptricen"; the study of the refraction of light, particularly through lenses.</note> of mine to Your Most Reverend and Serene Highness: then this one reason alone would suffice in general—that mathematical treatises, being remote from the common grasp and therefore held in contempt, are offered to no one more rightly than to those who are able to judge them; those whom Nature has equipped with a sharp intellect, and whom a love of **philosophy** <note>original: "philosophiæ"; in this era, this referred to "natural philosophy," what we now call science.</note> and meditation have led to a perfect knowledge of these matters. In which knowledge, whether you have any equal among the leading men of this time is unknown to me; certainly, among the professors of the Universities, those who are equal to this judgment are found to be fewer than is common.

Indeed, if in the very frequent dedications of books, no praises of Patrons were more artificial than these, I believe the trust that dedications have almost exhausted regarding the virtues of Patrons would soon be restored. And for this very purpose, I refrain from recounting the rest of Your Most Reverend and Serene Highness’s virtues (as is usually done in dedications); **lest the cobbler seem to wish to be wise beyond his bench** <note>original: "ne sutor ultra crepidam"; a classical proverb suggesting one should stay within their own area of expertise. Kepler, as a mathematician, modestly limits his praise to the Prince's mathematical talents.</note>.

For the rest, I hang no other **ivy** <note>original: "hederam"; a reference to the proverb "good wine needs no ivy bush," meaning a good product needs no advertisement. Kepler suggests the Prince's approval is the only "sign" or advertisement the book needs.</note> for the reader than to indicate to him that this little book has been approved by such a prince and ordered to be published. And now I most submissively commend myself to Your Most Reverend and Serene Highness. Farewell: The Kalends of January <note>original: "Cal. Ianuarijs"; January 1st, 1611.</note>.

<summary>Kepler concludes his dedication by praising the Elector’s rare mathematical competency and stating that the Prince’s endorsement is the only advertisement the book requires.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Ernest of Bavaria, patronage, Dioptrice, mathematics, natural philosophy, scientific publishing, 1611</keywords>

<meta>The previous page provided in the prompt appears to be from a completely different document—an ecclesiastical record of a church visitation from 1652. # JOHANNES KEPLER’S
## PREFACE TO DIOPTRICS, ON THE USE AND EX-
### cellence of the recently invented telescope: and concerning the new celestial
### wonders detected through it.

*I am presenting a mathematical booklet, friend reader, that is not so easy to grasp: it is one that requires not only intelligence in the reader, but also a particular focus of mind, and an incredible desire to understand the causes of things.*

*While I consider this, it seemed appropriate to comment on the excellence of Dioptrics or Telescopes <note>original: "Perspicillorum"</note>, and their wonderful effect in extending the boundaries of philosophy: so that talented youths and other cultivators of Mathematics, spurred on by this incentive of usefulness, might be encouraged to grasp the principles of the instrument from this booklet.*

*Many and great are the things which Jean Pena of France <note>original: "Ioannes Pena Gallus," a 16th-century mathematician who challenged the idea that the heavens were made of solid spheres.</note>, formerly Royal Mathematician, wrote in his preface concerning the use of the whole of Optics in his edition of Euclid’s Optics and Catoptrics, which he himself translated: yet, however great those things may be, compared to those which have been revealed in this past two-year period by the benefit of telescopes <note>original: "dioptrarum"</note>, they can be considered plainly childish.*

*And because I recommend that preface to the reader by this mention, come, let us examine its principal points briefly; lest I should seem to have knowingly and deliberately concealed the doubtful and false things which I cannot deny are interspersed there among the true and famous things. When I have completed this, then finally I will add those new things which the discipline of the Telescope <note>original: "Perspicillaria disciplina"</note> has detected in this time.*

*First, I establish with Pena a dogma concerning the Heavens, solidly demonstrated from Optics: namely, that the physicists—and even some theologians—are vehemently deceived when they think there are nine or ten transparent spheres embracing this Elementary world, just as the white of an egg usually embraces the yolk, or as the layers of an onion enclose one another. For since the eccentric paths of the planets are established by necessary reasoning, it is correctly gath-* <note>The text ends mid-word on the Latin "colligit," meaning "gathered" or "inferred."</note>

<summary>This is the opening page of Johannes Kepler’s "Dioptrice," where he introduces the telescope as a revolutionary tool that has rendered previous optical theories "childish" and begins to dismantle the ancient idea of solid celestial spheres.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Dioptrice, telescope, optics, Jean Pena, celestial spheres, astronomy, scientific revolution</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface of Johannes Kepler’s 1011 work, *Dioptrice*. In the preceding section, Kepler began discussing Jean Pena’s theories regarding the nature of the heavens and the absence of solid celestial spheres. The catchword "tate" from the previous page likely completed a word such as "falsitate" (falsity), leading into the conclusion of the optical argument here.</meta>

[He] concludes as an optical scientist that starlight descending obliquely <note>At an angle.</note> through these vast volumes—since the Earth is situated away from the centers of certain spheres—would be refracted according to optical laws. If this were granted, all certainty of astronomical observations would be destroyed; yet, experience itself provides testimony to their reliability. This same conclusion follows from the perceptible proportion of the body of the Earth relative to the sphere of the moon. For even if we ignore eccentric orbs <note>Planetary paths that do not share the same center as the Earth.</note> and place the Earth at the very center of all the spheres, the surface of the Earth itself still stands at a significant distance from the center of the lunar sphere (which the Earth occupies with its own center). Consequently, the rays of the stars descending to the surface of the Earth where we live would cut through the lunar sphere at an angle, and it would happen that those refracted rays would disturb the certainty of our sight.

Before Pena has even exited the entryway of this most beautiful demonstration, he stumbles far too improvidently, removing the distinction not only between the spheres themselves, but even between the air and the ether <note>The "ether" was historically considered the pure, thin substance of the heavens, distinct from the "air" of our lower atmosphere.</note>. While he makes the material of the ether the same as the material of this air we breathe, he demonstrates by his very error how much it matters for one walking in the palace of Philosophy to have the "eyes of Optics" wide open. For by the very same argument that removes the distinction between the celestial spheres, the distinction is instead firmly established between this air and the ether which succeeds it just above the mountain peaks.

For even if astronomical observations are not disturbed by some complex system of refractions tangled among themselves—as the distinction and solidity of spheres would require, if they existed—they are nonetheless disturbed by a uniform pattern of refraction whenever the stars approach the horizon. These refractions can come from nowhere else but the surface of this air we breathe; so much so, indeed, that in the optical branch of astronomy I have even been able to investigate the height of that atmospheric surface from the surface of the Earth. Pena appeals to experience, bringing in Gemma Frisius <note>Reiner Gemma Frisius (1508–1555), a Dutch mathematician and instrument maker.</note> as an eyewitness with his astronomical staff <note>term: *baculo astronomico*—A "cross-staff" used for measuring angles between celestial bodies.</note>, who denied that any refractions had been detected by him. Clearly, the wonderful diligence of the great Master Tycho Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), whose high-precision observations were essential to Kepler's work.</note> was not yet known to Pena; Tycho, partly through a multitude of assistants, and partly through the great size and precision...

<summary>Kepler critiques Jean Pena’s optical theories, arguing that while solid celestial spheres do not exist, there is a clear boundary between the Earth's atmosphere and the celestial ether. He cites atmospheric refraction near the horizon and the precision of Tycho Brahe's observations as proof of this distinction.</summary>
<keywords>refraction, astronomy, atmosphere, ether, Tycho Brahe, Gemma Frisius, Jean Pena, optics, celestial spheres</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s discussion of Jean Pena’s optical theories. The previous page ended with a discussion of how the rays of stars are refracted as they enter the atmosphere, and how Pena sought to remove the distinction between the "air" and the "ether." The catchword from the previous page, "perple-," is completed here as "perplexitate" (perplexity).</meta>

[By this] perplexity he achieved that small result which had escaped the crude instrument of Gemma <note>Gemma Frisius (1508–1555), a Dutch physician, mathematician, and master instrument maker.</note> and the attention of a single, solitary man. And I myself, in the optical part of my astronomy, brought forth witnesses for refractions as reserves for Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), the preeminent observer whose data Kepler used to derive the laws of planetary motion.</note>, drawn from antiquity, and therefore complete and untainted.

I hear that Doctor Helisæus Röslin <note>Helisaeus Röslin (1545–1616) was a German physician and astronomer who often engaged in friendly but sharp debate with Kepler.</note> has proposed a problem for me to solve concerning the sun being seen by the Dutch in the northern lands fourteen days earlier than it should have been <note>Original: "à Batavis in septentrionali Terra viso." This refers to the "Nova Zembla phenomenon" of 1597, where atmospheric refraction caused the sun to appear above the horizon nearly two weeks early during a polar expedition.</note>. I have not seen his book because of these disturbances <note>A reference to the political and religious unrest in the Holy Roman Empire during the early 17th century.</note>. However, I advise that this question was resolved by me through the refractions of the air in *The Optical Part of Astronomy*, Chapter 4, Number 9, page 138.

Pena gave the second place to the doctrine concerning the truly eccentric paths of the planets <note>Orbits that are "off-center" or do not share a common center with the Earth.</note>; and he did so rightly. Optics possesses the firmest arguments for these. Only this must be guarded against: lest that which happened to the ancients happen to us—that while trusting too securely in one eye (that of Optics) for the perception of this planetary orbit, we close the other eye (that of Physics). Thus, by attributing solely to optics what ought to have been granted equally to the reasoning of both Optics and Physics, we might again miss the mark. On this matter, see my *Optical Part of Astronomy* and my *Commentaries on the Motions of Mars* <note>Kepler’s *Astronomia Nova* (1609), in which he published his first two laws of planetary motion.</note>.

In the third place, Pena examines the question of the order of the planets from the perspective of Optics. He does not reason poorly based on Aristotle: if the Earth indeed stands fixed in its place, it is not likely that the Sun, Venus, and Mercury—in three distinct orbits of unequal size—would travel around in an equal period of time. Rather, it is more consistent—as pleased Martianus Capella, Campanus, and Brahe, and as Galileo demonstrates most evidently below—that if the Sun is being carried, those others are carried in a single orbit, and the Sun (like the axle of wheels) is surrounded by the epicycles <note>Small circles whose centers move along the circumference of a larger circle.</note> of Venus and Mercury as if by the rims of wheels. Indeed, it is most probable—as Copernicus held, and as that most ancient Samian philosophy <note>Referring to the school of Pythagoras of Samos, who were thought by the Renaissance to have held heliocentric views.</note> held so many centuries ago—that the Sun stands fixed in the center; and around it, not only Mercury and Venus at their own times, but even the Earth itself with the Moon as its companion, travel in an annual motion, along with the other three planets in their respective periods.

Here again, however, Pena [extricates] himself from the briars, though with some loss of truth...

<summary>Kepler defends the use of both Optics and Physics in determining planetary orbits and discusses various models of the solar system, ultimately favoring the Copernican view over geocentric alternatives. He also mentions his previous work on atmospheric refraction as a solution to the solar phenomenon observed by Dutch explorers in the Arctic.</summary>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s critique of Jean Pena’s (1528–1558) work on optics. In the previous page, Kepler discussed atmospheric refraction and Pena’s attempt to simplify celestial physics by treating air and ether as the same medium. Here, he specifically addresses Pena’s theories regarding the motion of the Earth and the fixed stars.</meta>

...clears up the [confusion] of tangled reasonings. For indeed, this argument, bound by no such evident necessity, bears witness only to probability. Therefore, **Pena**, distrusting this doubtful argument, timidly let go of the Earth's mobility as **Copernicus** <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543), who proposed the heliocentric model where the Earth revolves around the Sun.</note> teaches it; on the other hand, with a mere wink of his "Optical Eye," he conceived a confidence in some other investigated, very slow motion of the Earth. If this were granted, he thinks it follows that the fixed stars would seem to take on an unequal motion—a motion of the fixed stars to which the differing consensus of the centuries bears witness. 

But oh **Pena**, this is not to commend the excellence of **Optics**—to strain its powers on impossible things. **Bucephalus** <note>The famous horse of Alexander the Great.</note> was entirely noble, even if he could not imitate the wings of **Pegasus** <note>The mythical winged horse.</note>. And if someone who claimed to have seen **Bucephalus** flying were proven wrong, the glory of **Bucephalus** would not therefore fall. Too much, oh **Pena**, has this reasoning of yours departed from optical principles; too many things stand between your optical assumption and that which you conclude from it. 

First, you were not touched by that concern regarding the truth of the **Observations**, which we send away today from that profound antiquity <note>Kepler is skeptical of the accuracy of ancient astronomical data compared to modern measurements.</note>. Next, you allege the motion of the fixed stars as a thing seen by the eyes. And yet, it is far too distant from the sight of the eyes: only by a composition of three of the most subtle reasonings into one—and that not a very strict one—does an **Astronomer** finally dare to pronounce in which place of the **Zodiac** in any given century a certain fixed star stands. 

Finally, what you call the motion of a fixed star from the point of the **equinox** is, on the contrary, most truly the recession of the equinoctial point from the fixed star <note>Today known as the "precession of the equinoxes," caused by the Earth's axial wobble.</note>; where the equinoctial point wanders very far from **Pena's** conception. For what else is the equinoctial point than the imaginary intersection of two imaginary circles? One of these is understood to be continued by the sun through the orbit of the **Earth** <term>Tellus</term> <note>This refers to the ecliptic, the apparent path of the Sun against the background stars.</note> all the way into the highest ether; the other is likewise understood to be continued from the center of the **Earth** <term>Terra</term> through the terrestrial equinoctial <note>The celestial equator.</note> all the way beneath the fixed stars—and that not in every position of the Earth, but only then, when the Earth is in the equinoctial points. 

But this matter is taught in astronomy, and in my *Commentaries on Mars* <note>Original: "meis de Marte Commentarijs." This refers to Kepler's landmark work, *Astronomia Nova* (1609).</note>. In vain, therefore, from such uncertainties, the ill-informed **Pena** attributed some new motion to the **Earth**, and a very slow one at that, by which motion it might be exiled from the center of the world:

<summary>Kepler critiques Jean Pena for using optical arguments to propose an alternative "slow motion" of the Earth to explain the movement of fixed stars. Kepler argues that Pena misunderstands the nature of the equinoctial points and relies on unreliable ancient observations.</summary>

<keywords>Jean Pena, Nicolaus Copernicus, Optics, precession of the equinoxes, Astronomia Nova, Earth's motion, fixed stars, Zodiac, Bucephalus metaphor</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s critique of Jean Pena, focusing on the defense of the Copernican system and the rejection of the Aristotelian "sphere of fire." Kepler is highlighting how optical observations—specifically the apparent size of the Moon and the refraction of light—can be used to disprove long-held physical and astronomical theories.</meta>

page 5
a 3

...it would have been better to retain the Earth’s motion, which the most excellent masters <note>Referring to astronomers like Copernicus.</note> introduced; this motion certainly carries the Earth around the center of the planetary world at such an interval as is thought to be the semi-diameter of the **Sun’s sphere** <note>This refers to the Earth's orbital radius around the Sun, or 1 Astronomical Unit.</note>.

I cannot pass over, however, without also eliminating this error of **Pena’s** from his own preface, where he accuses **Copernicus’s** critique of the **Ptolemaic** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the proponent of the geocentric model whose lunar model had significant mathematical flaws regarding distance.</note> lunar hypothesis of being false. For by this accusation, the reputation of such a great artist is greatly harmed among the unlearned. **Copernicus** refuted **Ptolemy**, whose assumptions show the Moon when <term>bisected: the first or last quarter moon phase, when only half the disk is illuminated</term> to be nearly twice as close to the Earth as when it is full. **Copernicus** took an **Optical** argument, and indeed the best one: that it ought to appear nearly twice as wide in body when bisected as when full; whereas experience testifies to a diameter that is constant and varies by only a few minutes. Here **Pena**, abusing the subtlety of the optical axiom cited by **Copernicus** (which is repeated in these propositions at Number 67), dismisses the argument irrelevantly. For what then, if the most apparent diameters of the moon are not precisely in the inverse proportion of their distances? If they are nevertheless nearly so, did **Copernicus** therefore say nothing? A general of an army says he cannot capture a city in which there are ten thousand garrisoned soldiers, except with fifty thousand. What then if he lacks just one from this number, will he therefore hesitate over the capture?

But I return to the number of dogmas which **Pena** most truly proves from **Optics**; of which this is the fourth: that it is most correctly argued from optics that there is **no sphere of fire** above us. If this foundation is undermined, it cannot be obscure to any Philosopher of this time how great a ruin of **Aristotelian Meteorology** <note>Aristotle’s *Meteorologica* explained atmospheric and celestial phenomena based on the arrangement of the four elements; the "sphere of fire" was thought to exist between the air and the moon.</note> follows. For if there were fire beneath the heaven—whether visible or invisible—a very large **refraction** of rays would occur. For fire seeks higher places because it is of a thinner substance than air. Just as an inflated bladder emerges from the depths of the water, pushed upward by the weight of the water, so also the fiery substance finds the cause of its ascent from its own thinness, for it is driven by the thicker body of the surrounding air.

<meta>catchword: Cum</meta>

<summary>Kepler defends Copernicus against Jean Pena's criticisms regarding the Moon's apparent size and uses optical principles to argue against the existence of an Aristotelian "sphere of fire" in the upper atmosphere.</summary>
<keywords>Nicolaus Copernicus, Claudius Ptolemy, Jean Pena, lunar theory, atmospheric refraction, sphere of fire, Aristotelian physics, optics, planetary motion</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s critique of Jean Pena’s optical theories. Kepler is using the physics of light refraction to argue against the existence of the Aristotelian "sphere of fire," a region of fire supposed to exist between the Earth's atmosphere and the heavens.</meta>

Since, therefore, **physicians** <note>In this context, *physici* refers to natural philosophers or scientists who study the physical world.</note> say that a transparent substance, thinner than this air of ours, is spread above our heads, they cannot deny that the rays of visible objects are refracted when passing through the boundaries where the surfaces of the thick air and the thinner fire meet, wherever they pass through obliquely. Moreover, all rays except for one <note>The ray coming from the zenith, directly overhead, is the only one that strikes the atmosphere perpendicularly and thus does not bend.</note> pass obliquely to the observer’s location. Therefore, great refractions of rays would occur from every direction.

The force of this argument can be explained to the eye, as it were, by an experiment. Let the **Sun** shine against a wall. Let a **censer** <term>thuribulum: a vessel used for burning incense or coals</term> with burning coals be placed in between. If the air is calm, a certain stream of fiery substance will rise straight up from the censer, with no smoke mixed in; but if a slight breeze blows through, that stream will deflect a little to the side, yielding to the wind as it rises, yet bubbling with its own wavy motion. You will not perceive this stream of fire with your eyes, as it is completely colorless and transparent. But if you look at the opposite wall, you will see the shadows of things placed in the sun beyond the burning coal **tremble**—those shadows which are projected through this flow of fire.

Indeed, this **trembling** is a type of motion. Thus, the rays of the sun, which define the shadow, tremble because they are refracted as they pass through that **fiery bubbling**. They do so differently according to the changing surfaces of that fiery flow. From this inconsistent bending of the rays at the surface of that bubbling, there results an inconsistent landing of the bent or refracted rays upon the wall, and an inconsistent—that is, trembling—projection of the shadow. Therefore, it is established by this experiment that rays of light are sensibly refracted at the surface of a fiery substance, no matter how invisible it may be. 

Consequently, no such **fiery substance** is spread out under the expanse of the heavens, hanging over our heads—neither a fluctuating one nor a calm one—because **observers of the stars** detect no trembling refraction or change of position in the stars, nor even a constant one that would be proportional to the shape of a **sphere of fire**; in short, they find no refraction other than that which occurs at the surface of the air.

This most solid argument, **Pena** again handles carelessly:

<summary>Kepler uses an experiment with a censer and sunlight to demonstrate that if a "sphere of fire" existed above the atmosphere, it would cause visible refraction and distortion of starlight; since no such distortion is observed, he concludes the sphere does not exist.</summary>

<keywords>refraction, sphere of fire, Jean Pena, optical experiment, atmospheric physics, starlight, Aristotelian cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s critique of Jean Pena’s optical theories. Kepler is discussing how Pena tries to disprove the existence of a "sphere of fire" by denying all refraction, while also pointing out the flaws in Vitello’s earlier arguments for refraction based on lunar observations. Kepler notes that both authors are partially right but fundamentally confused about the physics involved.</meta>

...and while he shakes the walls of the **fiery sphere**, he is himself harmed by the excessive force of this **battering ram** <note>original: *arietis*. Kepler uses a metaphor: Pena's argument is like a siege engine that is so powerful or poorly handled that it damages the person using it.</note>. He believes it is essential to the strength of his argument to admit absolutely no **refractions** of the stars. Consequently, he does not hesitate to dismiss the reliability of the astronomers' observations that **Vitello** <note>Witelo (c. 1230–1285) was a medieval physicist whose work on optics was the standard text until Kepler wrote this volume.</note> cites. Vitello had claimed that light rays are refracted, and that this is noticeable in the **Moon**, whose latitude often appears different from what the **Tables of Motions** <note>Mathematical tables used by astronomers to predict where a planet or moon should be located at a specific time.</note> would allow. **Pena** counters that the cause is not refraction, but **parallax** <term>parallax: the apparent shift in an object's position when viewed from different locations. Because the Moon is close to Earth, its position looks different depending on where on Earth you are standing.</term>, a phenomenon well known to astronomers.

Good heavens, what a strange and muddled situation! For both men hold a true doctrine, yet both prove it improperly, and in the course of proving it, they fall into errors regarding related matters. Vitello says truly that the bending of starlight occurs because of the density of the air. He is also correct—though by chance—that this can be detected in the Moon. However, he was greatly mistaken in assuming that the Moon’s un-refracted position <note>The "true" position of the Moon if there were no atmosphere to bend the light.</note> could be accurately determined using the calculations available in his time. He set those calculations as a rule for evaluating observations and detecting refractions, and in this, he was vehemently deceived. 

Thus, I would not easily say that anyone before **Tycho Brahe** <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601) was a Danish astronomer whose precise observations provided the data Kepler needed to discover the laws of planetary motion.</note> successfully detected the refraction of the Moon—not only because the old calculations were uncertain, but also because previous observers were negligent. Brahe, however, detected refractions not only via the Moon (which is harder due to its rapid and variable motion) but primarily through the **fixed stars**. Yet even with the Moon alone, refraction could be easily detected, even if the calculations of its movement aren't perfectly certain. 

So much for Vitello’s mistake. Now let us examine Pena’s critique. He is correct in maintaining that no refraction occurs because of a "sphere of fire"; however, he is wrong to add that no refraction occurs *at all*, not even because of the air. Finally, he clumsily counters Vitello’s argument (which, as said, was already weak and flawed) by attributing to **parallax** what Vitello attributed to **refraction**. Yet astronomers know that these two phenomena have opposite effects. **Refraction** makes the Moon appear higher, while **parallax** makes it appear lower. Pena failed to weigh this fact. But as I said, there is no harm, even if Pena [refutes] Vitello's...

<summary>Kepler critiques the conflicting theories of Vitello and Pena regarding atmospheric refraction, noting that while Vitello correctly identified the phenomenon, his data was poor, and while Pena correctly rejected the "sphere of fire," he wrongly denied all refraction and confused its effects with parallax.</summary>
<keywords>refraction, parallax, Moon, Tycho Brahe, Vitello, Jean Pena, atmospheric density, astronomical observations</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s analysis of Jean Pena’s work on optics. Kepler is arguing that while Pena correctly identifies that the "sphere of fire" does not exist, he wrongly attributes the cause of refraction to the air alone, while neglecting the specific geometry of how light interacts with comets. The catchword "radio-" from the previous page leads into the discussion of "refractiones" (refractions).</meta>

*...does not escape **refractions**: for they are effects of the air, not of fire. They establish, therefore, the denser surface of the air, as was said above; but they utterly overthrow and eliminate the thinner region of fire, as **Pena** <note>Jean Pena (1528–1558) was a French mathematician who argued against the Aristotelian idea of a "sphere of fire" surrounding the Earth.</note> wishes. Therefore, from both sides, the excellence of **Optical demonstrations** shines forth, as much in establishing the distinction of the air from the **ether** <term>ether: the substance then believed to fill the heavens above the atmosphere.</term>, as in removing the fictitious **sphere of fire** <note>In ancient and medieval physics, the "sphere of fire" was a layer of burning element supposed to exist between the Earth's atmosphere and the Moon.</note>.*

*In the fifth place, **Pena** indicates how much ignorance holds physicists regarding the matter, location, and effects of **Comets**, unless they have entered the schools of **Optics**; and what this discipline teaches concerning such portents of nature to those who do not despise it.*

*Again, therefore, I recommend this truth to the reader: that the bodies of Comets (whether they be "hairy," "bearded," or "tailed" stars) are shown by **Optics** to be clearly transparent, based on the argument that they keep their tails turned away from the **Sun**.*

*Secondly, it is also true that those transparent bodies are denser than the **ether** in which they travel. It is also a third truth that, from the analogy of the motion of comets, we are taught much about the location of Comets, and we hold it certain that most of them move above the moon in the highest ether. But the fourth point that **Pena** adds is doubtful: whether a power of heating exists in Comets by the law of **Optics**, while the refracted rays of the sun—upon entering and exiting the body of the comet—are forced together into the sharp point of a **cone** <note>original: *coni mucronem*. Kepler is describing a "caustic" or focal point where light rays intersect after passing through a lens-like body.</note> behind the body, and by this gathering together they conceive a power of igniting. For even if the rays do come together into a cone in this way, the violence of inflammation would never follow except at that very point of the cone, in the depths of the ether. What, truly, does this have to do with that heat which is stirred up here on **Earth**? Furthermore, that visible **tail** of the Comets is not the cone of rays itself using the body of the comet as its base; but if we give much weight to this speculation, this tail is a *new* cone, beginning there where the other cone (whose base is in the body of the comet) ends in a point: which the law of optics teaches happens very close behind the body of the comet. Therefore, the rays of the sun, insofar as they constitute that visible track which we call the tail, are now diverging again. Indeed, igniting arises not from the **divergence**, but from the **intersection** of rays. Therefore, there is no power of igniting in the tail, but if there is any, it is in the intersection...*

<summary>Kepler uses optical laws to argue that comets are transparent bodies located in the ether above the moon. He disputes Pena's theory that comet tails cause heat on Earth by focusing sunlight, noting that light diverges in the tail rather than focusing.</summary>

<keywords>refraction, Jean Pena, sphere of fire, ether, comets, optical physics, comet tails, focal points, atmospheric density</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s commentary on Jean Pena’s work. In the previous section, Kepler discussed Pena’s theories on the transparency of comets and how they interact with light to form tails. This page transitions from the physical nature of comets to the location of the Milky Way and the physiological mechanics of vision.</meta>

...of rays closest to the body, from where such a tail begins.

Although indeed this reasoning of **Pena** <note>Jean Pena (1528–1558), a French mathematician whose preface to Euclid’s *Optics* challenged Aristotelian physics.</note> concerning the effect of the comet is doubtful, as I said; yet I am so far from judging it as something to be utterly despised, that I would rather recommend it to everyone as most noble, and a theory from which I believe the most hidden secret of Nature concerning the tails of comets can be drawn out. I wrote something on this matter in the German description of the **Comet that shone in the year 1607** <note>Now known as Halley's Comet; Kepler published a report on it in 1608.</note>: which I had also prepared in Latin with a most beautiful demonstration of the comet’s rectilinear <term>rectilinear: moving in a straight line.</term> path through the depths of the **ether**; but the printer cheated my expectation, and the little book remains in my desks, awaiting another opportunity.

**Pena** mentions the sixth utility of **Optics** in tearing down the opinion of the **Aristotelians** concerning the **Galaxy** <note>The Milky Way.</note>: and he teaches from **Optics** that the **Galaxy** is poured around within the **ether** itself, very far above the moon: since it presents no change of position <note>Original: *permutationem loci*. This refers to parallax; if an object is close to Earth, it appears to shift against the background when viewed from different locations. Because the Milky Way does not shift, it must be very distant.</note> relative to the fixed stars through different locations on earth and different positions relative to the horizon. Without a doubt, those who have hitherto looked up to **Aristotle’s** *Meteorology* <note>Aristotle argued the Milky Way was a phenomenon in the upper atmosphere, like a slow-burning fire.</note> and held it in high regard will recognize this great benefit of optics. Although those things which **Galileo** discovered concerning the **Galaxy** by the help of the **telescope** <note>Original: *perspicilli*. In 1610, Galileo used his telescope to prove the Milky Way was composed of countless individual stars.</note> will henceforth render this reasoning of **Pena’s** superfluous.

There follow in **Pena’s** preface some unsightly points, by which I ask that the student of **Optics** not be moved. **Vitellio** <note>Witelo (c. 1230–1280/1314), a Polish friar and physicist whose work *Perspectiva* was a standard textbook on optics.</note> most truly established that vision occurs by the **reception of rays**; I have confirmed this by the most evident experiments. Great was the boasting of the **Opticians** against the **Aristotelians** who defend the **emission of rays** <note>The "extramission" theory, which wrongly proposed that the eyes send out light to "feel" objects.</note> because of their own agreement among themselves. It is therefore to be lamented that this glory of the **Opticians** is stained by **Pena’s** contrary testimony, especially since **Pena** himself is an **Optician** and recommends **Optics** in that very preface. Yet let the cultivator of philosophy consider that the investigation of hidden things does not belong to a single century; often truth, coming as if by stealth into sight, offended by the negligence of philosophers, suddenly withdraws itself again, not deigning to grant a mere sight of itself to men, unless they are dutiful and industrious. Add to this that **Pena** to **Euclid**, whom he had translated anew in that little book...

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s critique of Jean Pena’s preface to Euclid’s *Optics*. While the previous page discussed the location of the Milky Way using optical parallax, Kepler now turns to the fundamental debate of optical theory: whether sight comes from rays leaving the eye (extramission) or rays entering it (intromission). He also begins to touch upon atmospheric phenomena like rainbows and halos.</meta>

...displayed, and he granted something to the one using the **emissions of rays** <note>The "extramission" theory, which held that the eye sends out feeler-like rays to "touch" objects.</note> out of respect for the opinion of antiquity—a pursuit irrelevant to philosophy. Therefore, I take these words from **Pena’s** own mouth, and I write back to him his own sentiment: I want a physicist who is not at all credulous, and for that reason, one experienced in optical demonstrations, who examines **Euclid** (he had said **Vitello** <note>Witelo (c. 1230 – after 1280), a medieval natural philosopher whose work *Perspectiva* was the standard optical text before Kepler.</note>) and other opticians accurately, and believes them only as much as he sees demonstrated by them. **Euclid** was a man second to none in learning and erudition, as his monuments show; but—as is the common lot of rising disciplines—he held preconceived opinions which he offered as axioms for demonstrations. One such opinion is that **sight occurs through rays hastening from the eyes to the object seen**, which is no more necessary than if you were to say vision occurs through the reception of rays. These things, I say, I think should be thrown back at **Pena** in this place. For it makes no difference to certain demonstrations which of these is true; and you see both used by me indiscriminately in Propositions 3 and 19. Yet this distinction must be noted: if we are dealing with the nature of a shining thing, it is useful for us to speak clearly, and to instill nothing other than the **emissions of rays from shining points**. But if we speak of the vision of shining things, and of the deceptions of sight, the deceptions themselves often invite us to speak almost captiously <note>Kepler suggests that when describing an optical illusion, it is sometimes easier to use the language of "eye-rays" even if it is physically incorrect.</note>, and to use the language of **emissions of rays from the eye**, when in reality they are **receptions of rays into the eye**.

Why one thing is seen with two eyes: **Pena** correctly refutes the false reasoning of **Vitello**, but he incorrectly praises the equally false reasoning of **Galen** <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the influential Greek physician.</note>. **Galen** used optical terms in a way that hardly follows optical laws—as if the **pyramids of vision** formed by the very act of seeing, and continued from the seen thing as if from a common base to the eyes, became something real and corporeal, which could be twisted away from the seen object by the distortion of the eye. You will find the true cause below in Proposition 62.

Aristotle saw long ago that the explanation of the **Halo**, the **Rainbow**, **Parhelia** <term>Parhelia: also known as "sun dogs," these are bright spots that appear on either side of the sun due to ice crystals in the atmosphere.</term>, and **Paraselenae** <term>Paraselenae: "moon dogs," the lunar equivalent of sun dogs.</term> must be sought from the discipline of **Optics**; nor those things which...

He also notes that atmospheric phenomena like rainbows and sun dogs must be explained through the laws of optics.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from Kepler’s critique of Jean Pena’s theories to his own defense of "Dioptrics"—the study of lenses. Kepler moves from discussing ancient and Renaissance optics to the cutting-edge astronomical discoveries made by Galileo using the telescope, setting the stage for his own mathematical explanations of how these new instruments work.</meta>

...[things which] are still missing in *Aristotle's Meteorology*, can be supplied from elsewhere.

I had also thought of adding a small book here about the **Rainbow**; it would have been a supplement to Aristotle’s inquiry on the rainbow, but the true causes of **Sun Dogs** <note>original: "Pareliorum"; also known as "mock suns," these are bright spots that appear on either side of the sun due to ice crystals in the atmosphere.</note> were still missing, and these are intertwined with the causes of ominous rainbows. Therefore, for the time being, I have set this task aside.

Since the science of Optics benefits natural philosophy so much, **Pena** is quite right to expect even more from optics—in Porphyrian <note>Referring to Porphyry (c. 234 – c. 305 AD), a Neoplatonic philosopher whose works often blended philosophy, theology, and theurgy.</note> magic and theology, and in the debunking of sleight-of-hand tricks. Nor does **Giambattista della Porta’s** *Natural Magic* <note>A popular 16th-century work that explored various scientific and pseudo-scientific wonders.</note> promise little; let the reader consult it, and he will see that the discipline of optics provides marvelous uses throughout all of human life.

Thus far, then, let **Pena** be heard by us, concluding his very learned speech on the excellence of Optics and its astounding effects in uncovering the nature of things.

Now it is time for me to keep my promises and show that, through this branch of optics which we call **Dioptrics** <note>The study of the refraction of light through lenses, as opposed to "Catoptrics," which deals with mirrors.</note> and its subject, **Spyglasses** <note>original: "Perspicillis"; here Kepler refers to the telescope, which was a very recent invention.</note>, we have learned far more marvelous things about the nature of reality in a short span of time—so much so, indeed, that everything we have produced so far from **Pena** via the benefit of optics might seem like child's play.

Everyone is currently reading **Galileo’s** *Starry Messenger*, as well as my own modest *Conversation* <note>Kepler's *Dissertatio cum Nuncio Sidereo* (1610), written in response to Galileo's discoveries.</note> with that messenger, and also my brief *Narration* confirming the *Starry Messenger*. Therefore, let the reader briefly weigh the main points of that *Messenger*, considering what great things were discovered by the benefit of that spyglass—the principles of which I demonstrate in this very book. Sight testified that there was a certain bright body in the sky which we call the **Moon**; it was demonstrated through optical principles that this body is spherical. Astronomy, through various reasonings built upon optical foundations, had even established its distance from the Earth as roughly sixty Earth-radii. Various spots used to appear on that body, and there followed the obscure opinion of a few philosophers—introduced by **Hecataeus** <note>Hecataeus of Abdera, a Greek historian of the 4th century BC.</note> into the myths about the island of the **Hyperboreans** <note>In Greek mythology, a legendary people who lived in a land of perpetual sunshine in the far north.</note>—of mountains and valleys, of moisture and

He argues that "Dioptrics" (the science of lenses) provides a more profound understanding of nature than any previous optical study.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument for the physical reality of celestial bodies. Having moved from the theoretical optics of Jean Pena, Kepler now uses the evidence provided by the newly-invented telescope to describe the Moon’s geography and defend the Copernican idea that the Earth moves.</meta>

*...the alternating images of the contents to be seen. But now the **telescope** <note>original: "Perspicillum"</note> places all these things so clearly before our eyes that one would have to be truly timid to still think it necessary to doubt while enjoying such a sight. Nothing is more certain than that the southern parts of the **Moon** <note>original: "Luna"</note> abound with many—and indeed immense—mountains, while the northern parts, being lower, receive the moisture flowing down from the south in vast lakes. Those doctrines which **Pena** had previously brought forth through the benefit of **Optics** <note>original: "Optices"</note>—doctrines that were drawn from thin visual evidence—were demonstrated through long, interconnected chains of reasoning, such that they were attributed to human Reason rather than to the Eyes. But here, the Eyes themselves are brought into the sight of hidden things, as if a new gateway to heaven had been opened. If anyone should now wish to test the power of Reason upon these new observations: who does not see how far the contemplation of Nature will push its boundaries? We must ask: *To what end* <note>original: "Cui bono"</note> do these stretches of mountains and valleys and vast spaces of seas exist on the Moon? And can we not suppose that some creature, perhaps less noble than man, inhabits those regions?*

*No less is decided here that question which, born almost alongside philosophy itself, is debated today by the most noble minds: *Can the **Earth** <note>original: "Terra"</note> be moved* (as the theoretical doctrine of the planets strongly requires) without the collapse of heavy things or the disturbance of the motion of the elements? For if the Earth were exiled from the center of the world, some fear that the waters, deserting the globe of the Earth, would flow back to the center of the universe. And yet we see that in the Moon, too, there is the force of moisture, occupying the low basins of that globe. Although that globe is carried around in the ether itself, outside the centers not only of the world but also of our Earth, the abundance of lunar waters is not prevented from clinging steadily to the globe of the Moon, tending toward the center of its own body. Therefore, through this example of the lunar globe, Optics reforms the doctrine of heavy and light things; and here it confirms my introduction to the **Commentary on the Movements of Mars** <note>original: "commentaria Martis motuum"; a reference to Kepler's seminal 1609 work, *Astronomia Nova*.</note>.*

*The followers of the **Samian philosophy** <note>original: "Samiæ philosophiæ"; a reference to the heliocentric (Sun-centered) theory. Kepler credits Pythagoras and Aristarchus, both from the island of Samos, as its ancient originators.</note> (for let me use this name to indicate its inventors, **Pythagoras** and **Aristarchus of Samos**)...*

<summary>Kepler uses telescopic observations of the Moon's mountains and "seas" to argue that it is a physical world similar to Earth. He uses this analogy to solve a major objection to heliocentrism, arguing that gravity is a local force that keeps water on a planet even when it is in motion.</summary>

<keywords>telescope, Moon, lunar mountains, lunar seas, gravity, Earth's motion, heliocentrism, Pythagoras, Aristarchus of Samos, Astronomia Nova</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument regarding the Earth’s motion. On the previous page, he discussed how the telescope makes distant objects visible, potentially resolving the ancient debate over whether the Earth moves. He now suggests that the Moon provides a unique perspective to prove this point.</meta>

...even a defense is prepared on the **Moon** <note>original: "Luna"</note> against the apparent immobility of the **Earth** <note>original: "Terra"</note> to our eyes. Indeed, we are taught in the study of **optics** <note>original: "opticis"</note> that if any of us were on the Moon, that person would see the Moon—their own home—as entirely immobile, while our Earth, the **Sun** <note>original: "Solem"</note>, and everything else would appear to be in motion. For such are the principles of how our vision is constructed.

**Jean Pena** <note>A 16th-century French mathematician who argued that the heavens were filled with air, not solid spheres.</note> previously mentioned how astronomers, using the principles of optics and great effort of reasoning, raised the **Milky Way** <note>original: "viam Lacteam"</note> from the elementary world (where **Aristotle** had placed it) into the highest heavens. But now, by the benefit of the recently invented **telescope** <note>original: "Perspicilli"</note>, the eyes of astronomers themselves are led directly to see the true substance of the Milky Way; so that whoever enjoys this spectacle is forced to admit that the Milky Way is nothing other than a mass of tiny stars.

What a **cloudy star** <note>original: "Nebulosa stella"; an early term for a nebula or star cluster.</note> was has remained entirely unknown until now. But the telescope, directed at such a "cloudy coil" (as **Ptolemy** <note>The 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer.</note> calls it), shows again—just as in the Milky Way—two, three, or four very bright stars placed in a very narrow space.

Who would have believed that the number of **fixed stars** <note>original: "Fixarum"</note> is ten or perhaps twenty times greater than what is recorded in Ptolemy's description of the stars, if it were not for this instrument? And from where, I ask, should we seek an argument about the limit or boundary of this visible world—that it is the sphere of the fixed stars itself—if not from this very multitude of stars detected by the telescope? This multitude acts as a kind of vaulted ceiling for the moving world.

How much an astronomer might err in determining the size of the fixed stars, unless he surveys the stars anew using the telescope, can likewise be seen in the work of **Galileo**; and below we shall even produce the letters of a certain German as testimony.

But that chapter of the ***Starry Messenger*** <note>original: "nuncij siderij"; referring to Galileo’s 1610 treatise *Sidereus Nuncius*.</note> surpasses all wonder, where by the benefit of a most perfect telescope, another world—as it were, a **Jovian world** <note>original: "mundus Iovialis"; the system of Jupiter and its moons.</note>—is reported to have been detected. And the mind of the philosopher considers, not without amazement, that there is some huge globe which, in its physical mass, equals fourteen terrestrial globes (unless here Galileo's telescope [reveals] something more precise to us...

<summary>Kepler explains how the telescope proves the Milky Way is composed of stars and reveals a universe far more vast than Ptolemy described. He specifically highlights Galileo's discovery of the moons of Jupiter as a revolutionary expansion of the known world.</summary>
<keywords>telescope, Milky Way, fixed stars, Galileo, Jupiter, Ptolemy, optics, Starry Messenger, Jovian world</keywords>
