<meta>This page continues Kepler’s discussion of the revolutionary discoveries made with the telescope. Having previously mentioned the Milky Way and the vast number of stars, he now turns his attention to Jupiter and its moons, using them as an analogy to defend the Copernican (heliocentric) view that the Earth moves despite appearing stationary to its inhabitants.</meta>

*...according to **Tycho Brahe’s** <note>The great Danish astronomer whose precise observations Kepler used to calculate the laws of planetary motion.</note> proportions, he will soon demonstrate) around which four of our moons—not unlike this moon of ours—circle. The slowest of these travels its circuit in a space of fourteen days, as **Galileo** <note>Galileo Galilei, who first published the discovery of Jupiter's moons in his "Sidereus Nuncius" in 1610.</note> has reported. The next after that, which is the most conspicuous of all, completes its circuit in eight days, as I myself discovered this past April and May; the remaining two follow an even shorter course of time. Here, **Reason**—summoned from my **Commentaries on Mars** <note>A reference to Kepler's 1609 work, "Astronomia Nova," where he derived the elliptical orbits of planets.</note> for a similar cause—suggests that the globe of **Jupiter** <note>original: "Iovis"</note> itself must rotate very rapidly, undoubtedly faster than the space of one of our days. This is so that the perpetual circuits of those four moons in the same direction may follow the rapid rotation of that massive globe around its own axis.*

*Furthermore, in those regions, our **Sun** <note>original: "Sol"</note>—the common hearth of both this terrestrial world and that Jovial world—which we perceive to be thirty minutes of arc in size, barely fills six or seven minutes. Meanwhile, having traversed the **Zodiac** over a space of twelve of our years, the Sun is found again among the same fixed stars. Therefore, the creatures who dwell upon the globe of Jupiter, while they contemplate the very brief courses of those four moons through the fixed stars, and while they watch both them and the Sun rise and set daily, would swear "by Jupiter the stone" <note>original: "Iovem lapidem jurarent"; Kepler is punning on an ancient Roman oath. He suggests that to the inhabitants of Jupiter, the planet feels as immobile as a stone.</note> (for I have just returned from those regions) that their own globe of Jupiter rests immobile in one place, while the fixed stars and the Sun—bodies that are actually at rest—turn around their own dwelling with a manifold variety of motions, no less than their own four moons. From this example, a follower of the **Samian philosophy** <note>A reference to Pythagoras of Samos, whose school suggested the Earth moved; here, Kepler uses it to mean the Copernican system.</note> will now learn much more than from the example of the Moon: what can be answered to someone who objects to the "absurdity" of the dogma of the **Earth’s** <note>original: "Telluris"</note> motion and who cites the testimony of our own sight.*

*O **telescope** <note>original: "Perspicillum"</note>, giver of much knowledge, more precious than any scepter! Is he who holds you in his right hand not established as a King and a Master of the works of God? Truly you:*

->*The great orbs above our heads, with all their motions, you subject to the human intellect.*<-

*If anyone were a little fairer to **Copernicus** <note>Nicolaus Copernicus, the astronomer who proposed that the Earth revolves around the Sun.</note> and to the lights of the Samian philosophy,*

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s defense of the Copernican system using Galileo’s recent telescopic discoveries. Having discussed the moons of Jupiter on the previous page, Kepler now addresses the skepticism regarding Earth's motion and introduces a famous anagram sent by Galileo regarding a new mystery.</meta>

Here, the skeptic alone stalls, doubting how it could be possible that while the **Earth** <note>original: "Telluris"</note> treads its planetary path through the ethereal fields, the **Moon** clings to it so constantly, like an inseparable companion, and meanwhile circles the globe of the Earth itself—much like a faithful little dog that surrounds its traveling master with various detours, now running ahead, now wandering to the sides. Let him look at **Jupiter**, which, as this **telescope** <term>telescope<note>original: "Perspicillo." This was the early term for the instrument before the word "telescopium" became standard.</note></term> reveals, does not drag just one such companion, as **Copernicus** assigned to the Earth, but certainly four of them, never deserting it, and each meanwhile driving its own circulation. But enough has been said about these matters in my *Conversation with the Starry Messenger* <note>Kepler’s "Dissertatio cum Nuncio Sidereo" (1610), his published response to Galileo's discoveries.</note>. It is time for me to turn to those things which were revealed by the use of this telescope after the publication of the *Starry Messenger* and my *Conversation* with it.

It has now been a year since **Galileo** wrote to **Prague** to say that he had observed something new in the heavens beyond his earlier findings. And lest there be someone who, out of a desire for detraction, might claim to have been the first observer, he allowed a period for others to publish what new things they might have seen; meanwhile, he described his own discovery through transposed letters <note>An anagram used to secure priority of discovery while further verifying the observation.</note> in this manner:

->s m a i s m r m i l m e p o e t a l e u m i d u n e n u g t t a u i r a s.<-

From these letters, I fashioned a semi-barbarous verse, which I inserted into my *Short Narrative* in September of last year:

->"Hail, twin-shielded offspring of Mars."<- <note>original: "Salve umbistineum geminatum Martia proles." Kepler mistakenly believed the anagram revealed that Galileo had found two moons orbiting Mars.</note>

But I wandered very far from the meaning of the letters; they contained nothing regarding Mars. And so as not to detain you, reader, here is the disclosure of the riddle <note>original: "Gryphi," referencing the Sphinx's riddles.</note> in the words of the author, Galileo himself.

->From Florence, November 13, 1610.<-

But moving on to other matters: since **Mr. Kepler** has printed in this latest narrative of his the transposed letters that I sent to Your Illustrious Lordship, and it has also been signaled to me how **His Majesty** <note>Emperor Rudolph II.</note> desires to know their meaning: here it is, I send it to Your Illustrious Lordship to share with His Majesty, with Mr. Kepler, and with whomever it pleases Your Illustrious Lordship, as I desire everyone to know it. The letters, therefore, combined in their true sense, say this:

<meta>This page follows the discussion of Galileo’s recent astronomical discoveries. *“I have observed the highest planet to be triple-bodied.”* <note>original: "Altissimum planetam tergeminum observavi." This was the solution to the anagram Galileo sent to Kepler: "smaismrmilmepoetaleumibunenugttauiras."</note> *This is to say that I have observed **Saturn**, to my great admiration, to be not a single star, but three together, which almost touch each other; they are completely immobile in relation to one another, and arranged in this way:* <image-desc>Three circles arranged in a horizontal line, with the center circle being notably larger than the two on the sides.</image-desc> *the one in the middle is much larger than the ones on the sides; they are situated, one to the east and the other to the west, in the same straight line exactly; they are not positioned perfectly according to the path of the **Zodiac**, but the western one rises somewhat toward the **North** <note>original: "Borea," referring to Boreas, the Greek god of the north wind.</note>; perhaps they are parallel to the **Equinoctial** <note>The celestial equator.</note>: if they are viewed with a **telescope** <term>telescope<note>original: "Occhiale." Now I have found a court for **Jupiter**, and two servants for this old man <note>A mythological reference to the god Saturn, who was often depicted as an old man; here, the "servants" are what we now know to be the rings of the planet.</note>, who help him walk, and they never leave his side: concerning the other planets there is nothing new. Etc.*

*Although these words differ little from Latin, I will nevertheless translate them so that nothing may delay the reader. Thus he spoke: but to come now to the other point, because **Mr. Kepler**, in that Narrative of his recently published in print, presented the letters which I sent transposed to your **Illustrious Lordship**; since it has also been signaled to me that **His Majesty** <note>original: "M. S." or "Majestatem Suam," referring to Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II.</note> desires to be taught their meaning; behold, I transmit it to your **Illustrious Lordship**, so that you may communicate it with **His Majesty**, with **Mr. Kepler**, and with whomever you wish.*

*The letters, joined together as they should be, say this:*
*“I have observed the highest planet to be triple-bodied.” Truly, I have discovered **Saturn**, with the greatest admiration, to be not a single star, but three very close to one another, so that they almost touch each other. They are entirely immobile relative to each other, and arranged in this manner:* <image-desc>Three circles in a horizontal row, with the central one being larger.</image-desc> *The middle one of these is much larger than the outer ones. They are situated, one to the east, the other to the west, in one straight line to a hair’s breadth: yet not exactly according to the length of the **Zodiac**: for the more western one rises non-[...]*

<meta>This page continues the translation of Galileo’s report on his observations of Saturn. The previous page ended with Galileo describing the alignment of the three bodies he observed making up the planet Saturn.</meta>

...nothing toward the **North** <note>original: "Boream"</note>, perhaps they are parallel to the **equinoctial** <note>The celestial equator.</note>. If you look at them through an **Eyepiece** <term>eyepiece<note>original: "Oculare." Another term for the telescope or its lens.</note></term> which does not multiply much, the stars will not appear well-distinguished from each other; but the star of **Saturn** will be seen as somewhat elongated, in the shape of an olive, like this: <image-desc>a horizontal oval shape representing Saturn as seen through a low-power telescope</image-desc>. But if you use an **Eyepiece** that multiplies the surface more than a thousand times, three globes will appear most distinctly, and they almost touch each other; nor will they be judged to be separated from each other by more than the width of a very thin and barely visible thread. And so, behold: a satellite system has been found for **Jupiter**, but for this **decrepit little old man** <note>original: "seniculo" (transcribed as "feniculo"). Saturn was traditionally associated with old age and the Greek titan Cronus.</note> [Saturn], two servants have been found to assist his walking, never departing from his sides. Regarding the remaining planets, I have found nothing new.

### [The Author’s Reflection]

Thus far Galileo. But if I had the choice, I would not make a **funeral feast** <note>original: "silicernium," a dry crust or a feast for an old man about to die; here referring to Saturn as an old man.</note> out of **Saturn**, nor make servants out of his companion globes; but rather, from those three joined together, a triple-bodied **Geryon** <note>In Greek mythology, a giant with three bodies or heads.</note>; from **Galileo**, **Hercules**; and from the **Telescope** <note>original: "Perspicillo."</note>, a **club**. Armed with this club, **Galileo** both conquered that highest of planets and—having dragged him out from the innermost sanctuaries of nature and down to Earth—exposed him to the eyes of us all. Indeed, now that the nest has been discovered, it is pleasing to seek by contemplation what kind of little birds are in it: what kind of life, if there is any life, exists between the pairs of globes almost touching each other; where not even...

...three cubits of sky lie open, but the space gapes open on all sides into a circle scarcely a fingernail wide. Do **Astrologers** truly assign to **Saturn** the protection of **miners** <note>original: "metallariorum." Saturn was traditionally the ruler of lead and those who worked underground.</note>, who, accustomed to living underground like moles, rarely breathe the free air under the open sky? Although the shadows here are a little more tolerable, because the **Sun**—which appears to them only as large as **Venus** appears to us on **Earth**—perpetually casts its rays through the gaps between the globes. Thus, those who stand on one globe are covered by the other as if by a paneled ceiling; they are illuminated from above by the protrusions of this ceiling of theirs, which are stretched out into the light of the sun, as if by certain torches. But the reins must be pulled on a mind that has taken possession of the free fields of the ether, in case perhaps later observations report something different from that earlier account, changed by the passage of time.

**Galileo** seemed to himself at the end of his letter to have put an end to the narrations about the planets and the new observations regarding them. But always keen-sighted... <note>The catchword "cax" refers to "perspicax," meaning keen-sighted or perceptive.</note>

<summary>Galileo describes Saturn’s "triple" appearance through a powerful telescope and compares the side bodies to servants assisting an old man. The author responds with mythological metaphors, casting Galileo as Hercules, and speculates on the strange conditions of life and light on such a crowded planetary system.</summary>

Thus that artificial eye—I mean the **Telescope** <term>telescope<note>original: "Perspicillum." Literally "a device for seeing through," this was the common term before the word "telescopium" was coined.</note></term>—has revealed more in a short time: read about these things in the following letter from **Galileo**.

->*From Florence, the 11th of December 1610.*<-

*I wait with great desire for the response to my last two letters, to hear what Mr. Kepler will have said regarding the strangeness of **Saturn**. In the meantime, I send him the cipher of another particular thing recently observed by me, which brings with it the decision of great controversies in astronomy, and in particular contains within itself a strong argument for the **Pythagorean** and **Copernican** <note>The "Pythagorean and Copernican" system refers to the Sun-centered (heliocentric) model of the universe, which was highly controversial at the time.</note> constitution; and in due time I will publish the deciphering and other details. I hope that I have found the method for defining the periods of the four **Medicean planets** <note>The four largest moons of Jupiter (Io, Europa, Ganymede, and Callisto), which Galileo named in honor of his patrons, the Medici family.</note>, which were judged with great reason to be almost inexplicable by Mr. Kepler, whom it will please, etc.*

*The transposed letters are these:*

*These immature things are now read by me in vain, o y.* <note>original: "Haec immatura à me jam frustra leguntur o y." This is an anagram. When the letters are rearranged in Latin, they form the sentence: "Cynthiae figuras aemulatur Mater Amorum" (The Mother of Love [Venus] imitates the phases of Cynthia [the Moon]). This was Galileo's way of announcing he had seen Venus going through phases like the Moon.</note>

*In Latin, it is as follows:*

*I wait with desire for a response to my last two letters; so that I may learn what Kepler says about the miracle of the star of **Saturn**.*

*Meanwhile, I send him a riddle <note>original: "gryphum"</note> of a certain new and remarkable observation, which serves the decision of great controversies in Astronomy, and specifically contains in itself a beautiful argument for the **Pythagorean** and **Copernican** Constitution of the World: in its own time, I will reveal the solution to the riddle and several other singular things. I hope I have discovered the Method of defining the periods of the four **Medicean stars**; which Kepler, not without the highest reason, thought were inexplicable, etc.*

*The transposed letters are those:*

*These immature things are now read by me in vain, o, y.*

Thus far Galileo. But if this letter has filled you, reader, with a desire to know the meaning hidden in those letters: come, read Galileo’s next letter.

First, however, I would like you to notice in passing what Galileo calls the **Pythagorean and Copernican** arrangement of the world. For [he points] a finger

<meta>This page continues the transition from Johannes Kepler’s commentary to the text of a letter from Galileo Galilei. *He refers to my **Cosmographic Mystery** <note>original: "Mysterium cosmographicum." Kepler's first major astronomical work, published in 1596.</note>, published 14 years ago, in which I took the dimensions of the planetary spheres from the astronomy of **Copernicus**, who makes the Sun stable in the center and makes the Earth mobile both around the sun and around its own axis. I showed that the intervals between those spheres correspond to the five regular **Pythagorean figures** <term>Platonic solids<note>The five symmetrical 3D shapes (tetrahedron, cube, octahedron, dodecahedron, and icosahedron) which Kepler believed defined the spacing of the planets.</note></term>, which were long ago distributed among the elements of the world by that author with an effort more beautiful than successful or legitimate; and for the sake of these figures, **Euclid** wrote his entire geometry.*

*Therefore, in that **Mystery**, one may find a certain combination of Astronomy and Euclidean Geometry, and through this, the most absolute consummation and perfection of both. This was the reason why I waited with great desire to see what kind of argument **Galileo** would bring forward in favor of this Pythagorean constitution of the world. Therefore, the letter of Galileo regarding this argument follows.*

->My most Illustrious and Reverend Lord and Patron.<-

*It is time that I decipher for Your Illustrious and Reverend Lordship, and through you for **Mr. Kepler**, the transposed letters which I sent to him some weeks ago; it is time, I say, as I am now entirely clear on the truth of the matter, so that not a single doubt or scruple remains. They shall know, then, how about three months ago, when **Venus** was seen as an evening star, I began to observe her diligently with the **telescope** <term>occhiale<note>Galileo's term for his telescope.</note></term> to see with the senses themselves that which the intellect did not doubt. I saw her then, at the beginning, in a round shape, clean and defined, but very small; she maintained such a figure until she began to approach her maximum **elongation** <term>digression<note>The maximum angular distance of a planet from the Sun as seen from Earth.</note></term>, though she continued to grow in size. Then she began to lose her roundness on her eastern side, away from the Sun, and in a few days was reduced to being a most perfect semi-circle; and she remained so, without any change, until she began to withdraw toward the Sun, moving away from the tangent. Now she is decreasing from the semi-circle and shows herself as **horned** <note>original: "cornicolata." This means she appeared as a crescent, like the moon.</note>, and will continue to thin until her disappearance, being reduced then to very thin horns, then passing to ap-*

<meta>This page concludes Galileo’s letter to Giuliano de' Medici (for transmission to Kepler), where he deciphers his famous anagram regarding the phases of Venus. This discovery provided the first physical proof that planets orbit the Sun, rather than the Earth.</meta>

...morning appearance, we shall see it crescent-shaped and very thin, with its horns pointing away from the sun; it will then continue to grow until its maximum elongation <term>greatest elongation<note>The point in a planet's orbit where it appears furthest from the Sun as seen from Earth.</note></term>, where it will appear as a semicircle, and it will remain so for many days without changing. Then, from the half-circle, it will quickly pass to a full circle, and it will remain round for many months. However, its diameter now is about five times larger than it appeared during its first evening appearance. From this wonderful experience, we have a sensible and certain demonstration of two great questions that have remained doubtful until now among the greatest minds of the world. One is that all the planets are by their nature dark <note>Meaning they do not produce their own light, but reflect the Sun's.</note> (the same thing happening to Mercury as to Venus). The other is that Venus must necessarily revolve around the Sun, as does Mercury and all the other planets—a thing well believed by the **Pythagoreans**, **Copernicus**, **Kepler**, and myself, but never before proven by the senses, as we have now done with Venus and Mercury. **Mr. Kepler** and the other **Copernicans** will therefore have reason to boast of having believed and philosophized well, even if we have been, and shall continue to be, regarded by the generality of book-philosophers <note>original: "filosofi in libris." A term for scholars who relied strictly on ancient texts rather than physical observation.</note> as people of little understanding and little short of fools. The words, therefore, that I sent transposed, and which said:

> *These immature things are already read by me in vain, o. y arranged.* <note>original Latin: "Hæc immatura à me jam frustra leguntur, o. y ordinate." This was the original anagram Galileo used to "timestamp" his discovery while still verifying his data.</note> *The mother of loves imitates the shapes of Cynthia.* <note>original Latin: "Cynthia figuras æmulatur mater amorum." The "mother of loves" is Venus, and "Cynthia" is the Moon. In plain English: Venus imitates the phases of the Moon.</note> That is, Venus imitates the phases of the moon.

Three nights ago, I observed the eclipse, in which there was nothing notable; only the edge of the shadow appeared indistinct, confused, and as if clouded, and this is because that shadow originates from the Earth at a very great distance from the Moon.

I wanted to write other details. But having been delayed a long time by some gentlemen, and the hour being very late, I am forced to finish. Please do me the favor of greeting in my name **Mr. Kepler**, **Hasdale**, and **Segeth** <note>Martin Hasdale and Thomas Segeth, associates of Kepler.</note>; and to Your Illustrious Lordship, with every reverence, I kiss your hands and pray to the Lord God for your happiness. From Florence, the first of January, the Year 1611.

->Of Your Illustrious and Reverend Lordship<-
->Your Most Obliged Servant,<-
->**Galileo Galilei**<-

C 3

*This is Galileo’s Letter, the summary of which you may take in Latin words.* <note>original: "Hæc Epistola Galilei, cujus summam cape latinis verbis."</note>

The time has come for me to reveal the method for reading the letters which I sent in a transposed order <note>This refers to Galileo's famous anagram "Haec immatura a me iam frustra leguntur o.y.," which he used to claim priority for his discovery before he was ready to publish it.</note> several weeks ago. The time, I say, is now, after I have been made most certain of the matter itself, so that I no longer harbor even the slightest doubt. Know therefore that about three months ago, as soon as the star of Venus could be seen, I began to look at it diligently through the glass <term>telescope<note>Galileo used the term "Oculare" here, referring to his "perspicillum" or spyglass.</note></term>; so that what I held as certain in my mind, I might also grasp by the sense of sight itself. 

In the beginning, therefore, Venus appeared in a perfectly circular shape, enclosed by a sharp and evident boundary, but it was very small. Venus retained this shape for a while, until it began to approach its greatest elongation <term>greatest elongation<note>The point in a planet's orbit where it appears furthest from the Sun as seen from Earth.</note></term> from the Sun; meanwhile, its apparent size grew continuously. From that point on, it began to lose its roundness on its eastern side—the side turned away from the Sun—and within a few days it condensed its whole appearance into a most perfect semicircle. That figure lasted without even the slightest change until the planet began to return toward the Sun, leaving the tangent of its epicycle <term>epicycle<note>A small circular orbit used in ancient and Renaissance astronomy to explain the complex paths of planets. Galileo is describing the planet's motion in terms familiar to his contemporary readers.</note></term>. 

At this present time, it loses its semicircular shape more and more, and it will continue to decrease until its disappearance, when it will fade into a very thin crescent <note>original: "subtilißimum cornu," literally "a most slender horn."</note>. After making the transition to its morning appearance, it will appear to us only as a sickle-shape, with a very thin crescent turned away from the Sun. Afterwards, the crescent will be filled more and more until the greatest elongation from the Sun, at which point it will appear as a semicircle, and that shape will last for many days without notable variation. Then, from a semicircle, it will gradually fill the whole orb, and it will preserve that perfectly circular shape for many months. 

Furthermore, at present, the diameter of the body of Venus is about five times larger than what it showed during its first evening appearance. From this wonderful observation, we have a most certain demonstration, perceptible by the senses, of two very great questions which until this day have been debated by the greatest minds on both sides. One is that all the planets are by their nature dark bodies <note>Meaning they reflect the Sun's light rather than producing their own.</note> (as we may now conclude the same of Mercury as of Venus). The other is that the highest necessity urges us to say,

22

<margin>
The author's argument concerning the placement of the orbits of Venus and Mercury around the Sun, as it stands in the Copernican and Pythagorean arrangement of the World, I accept simply, and I add nothing: except that I congratulate Pena <note>Jean Péna (1528–1558), a mathematician who argued that the heavens were made of air rather than solid spheres.</note>, who had previously proved the same things by another, weaker argument.
</margin>

That Venus (*and furthermore Mercury*) revolves around the Sun, *just as all the other planets: a matter indeed believed by the Pythagoreans, Copernicus, and Kepler, but never truly confirmed by the senses, until now in the case of Venus and Mercury. Kepler and the other Copernicans, therefore, have reason to boast that they have philosophized well, and that their belief was not in vain: however much it has happened to them, and may even continue to happen, that they are considered by the philosophers of this time—who "philosophize" only from books—by universal consensus to be stupid and little less than fools.*

The words, therefore, which he sent with transposed letters, and which said this: [These immature things are now read by me in vain o. y.] <note>original: "Hæc immatura à me jam frustra leguntur o. y."</note>, arranged in their proper order, sound thus: [The mother of loves imitates the shapes of Cynthia] <note>original: "Cynthiæ figuras æmulatur mater amorum." Galileo used "Cynthia" as a poetic name for the Moon and "Mother of Loves" for Venus.</note> *that is, Venus imitates the phases of the Moon.*

It has been three nights since I observed an eclipse of the Moon, in which nothing particularly noteworthy occurred. Only the edge of the shadow appeared indistinct, confused, and as if clouded over; the reason being that the shadow rises from the Earth, at a great distance from the body of the Moon.

I had other singular matters as well, but I am prevented from writing about them, etc. *Thus far Galileo.*

What shall we do now, friend reader, with our <term>spyglass<note>The "perspicillum," or telescope.</note></term>? Shall we make of it Mercury's staff <term>caduceus<note>The staff of Mercury, messenger of the gods, often used as a symbol of travel or transformation.</note></term>, relying on which we might sail through the liquid ether, and with Lucian <note>Lucian of Samosata, an ancient author who wrote "A True Story," involving a fictional voyage to the Moon and stars.</note> lead a colony into the deserted Evening Star, enticed by the pleasantness of the region? Or rather Cupid's arrow, which, having slipped through the eyes, causes the innermost mind—having received the wound—to burn with love for Venus? For what should I not say about the admirable beauty of this globe, if, lacking its own light, it is given such splendor by the borrowed light of the Sun alone—a splendor which Jupiter does not have, nor the Moon, though she enjoys an equal proximity to the Sun; whose light, if compared to the light of Venus, will indeed seem larger due to the apparent size of its body, but dull, dead, and as if made of lead. O truly golden Venus; will anyone doubt further that the whole globe of Venus is most polishedly crafted from pure, unadulterated gold: whose surface, placed in the sun, reflects such a vigorous splendor? Now let my experiments regarding the changeable light of Venus be added...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Galileo’s discovery that Venus has phases like the Moon. *in the blink of an eye; which I reviewed in the optical part of Astronomy: Reason will be able to conclude nothing else, except this, that the star of Venus is whirled around its own axis in a most rapid rotation, displaying different parts of its surface—some less and some more receptive to solar light—one after the other.*

*It is pleasing, indeed, to marvel with delight at the cleverness of Astrologers, who for so many centuries have held it as explored that the Loves and the pride of mistresses, and the manners and characters of lovers, are governed by this star of Venus. Clearly, Venus would not be "horned"* <note>original: "cornuta." In Latin, this refers both to the crescent shape of the planet and the "horns" of a cuckold—a common 17th-century joke.</note> *if she did not daily make so many men "horned"; as often as she lets herself down to long-awaited embraces, she suddenly recurs from the eyes and free sight of the lover under the haughty rays of the Sun as if to another husband, frustrating the desires of lovers. It was indeed a wonder that Venus herself was not also "born"* <note>original: "tíktesthai" (Greek: τίκτεσθαι). The author is making a pun on the planet being "born" (appearing) and "giving birth," relating the planetary phases to a reproductive cycle.</note> *like the Moon, since Venereal loves are the sole and unique cause of giving birth. Behold, therefore, how the most beautiful of stars, having deposited her mature offspring, so to speak, in the perfect circle of her appearance, lets herself down to the bottom of her Epicycle* <term>Epicycle: In the old Ptolemaic system, a small circular path followed by a planet, the center of which itself moves along a larger circle.</term>*, and into the vicinity of the Earth, empty and thinned into a horn, as if for the sake of conceiving a new offspring; and after she has been joined to the Sun, subjecting herself to the Sun as if to her husband in a lower place, as is the custom and nature of women; from there she gradually raises herself up again on the other side, and more and more, as if impregnated, she swells; until in the tenth month from conception* <note>The author uses "ten months" here as a poetic reference to the human gestation period, though the actual cycle of Venus is different.</note> *(for such is the interval between two conjunctions of the Sun ☉ and Venus ♀), she brings her full womb—I say the full circle of her appearance—to the summit of the Epicycle, above the Sun, and joined to him again, she brings her offspring home as if to the genuine father.*

*But enough of my reasonings. Let us now hear, by way of an epilogue, Galileo’s own reasoning, constructed from all the experiments he brought forth with the telescope* <term>Perspicillum: The early Latin term for the telescope, literally "an instrument for seeing through."</term>*. Thus he says again:*

->**To the Most Illustrious and Reverend, my Most Honored Lord**<-

*I have received particular pleasure and satisfaction in reading your last letter, Most Illustrious and Reverend Lord, dated the 7th of this month, and especially in that part where you mention to me the favorable inclination of the Illustrious Lord Counselor Wacker* <note>Johannes Matthäus Wacker von Wackenfels (1550–1619), an imperial counselor in Prague and a patron of the sciences who was one of the first to hear of Galileo's discoveries.</note> *toward me; which I infinitely value and appreciate; since that inclination primarily originates from his having encountered observations that necessarily demonstrate conclusions previously held as true by his Illustrious Lordship.*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary light and appearance. While the previous page focused on the phases of Venus and playful allegories regarding its rotation, this page moves into a technical argument regarding the source of light for planets versus fixed stars, likely written by Galileo Galilei or summarizing his observations.</meta>

*To further confirm my possession of such precious favor, I beg Your Illustrious Lordship to let him understand on my behalf that, in accordance with the belief of Your Illustrious Lordship, I have certain proof that just as all the planets receive their light from the Sun, being themselves dark and opaque; so the fixed stars shine by their own nature, not needing the illumination of solar rays, which, God knows if they even reach such a height—any more than the light of one of those fixed stars reaches us.*

*The principal foundation of my discourse lies in my observing very clearly with the telescope* <note>original: "occhiali," literally "eyeglasses," the term often used for the newly invented telescope.</note> *that those planets, as they are found closer to us or to the Sun, receive greater splendor and reflect it back more illustriously; and therefore Mars at perigee* <term>perigee: the point in an orbit where a planet is closest to Earth</term>*, being very close to us, appears much more splendid than Jupiter, although it is much smaller in size, and it is difficult with the telescope to strip away that irradiation* <note>a halo of light or glare</note> *that prevents one from seeing its distinct and round disk; which does not happen with Jupiter, which appears exquisitely circular. Saturn then, because of its great distance, is seen with an exact edge, both the larger central star and the two very small side ones* <note>Before telescopes were powerful enough to show rings, Galileo described Saturn as "tripartite," or composed of three distinct bodies.</note> *; and its light appears faint and dimmed, without any irradiation that might prevent the distinguishing of its three very distinct small globes.*

*Now, since we see very clearly that the Sun illuminates nearby Mars very splendidly, and that the light of Jupiter is much more faint (even if without the instrument it appears quite bright, which happens because of the size and whiteness of the star), and that of Saturn is very faint and dark, being much further away; how should the fixed stars appear, being unspeakably further away than Saturn, if their light derived from the Sun? Certainly very weak, murky, and dull.*

*But the total opposite is seen; for if we look, for example, at the Dog Star* <note>original: "Cane." This refers to Sirius, the brightest star in the constellation Canis Major.</note>*, we encounter a most vivid brilliance that almost takes away our sight with a vibration of rays so fierce and powerful that, in comparison, the planets—even Jupiter and Venus themselves—remain like an impure glass next to a most clear and fine diamond. And although the disk of it*

<meta>This page continues Galileo Galilei’s argument regarding the nature of light in the celestial bodies. He concludes his explanation of why planets appear different from stars and offers a formal closing to his correspondent, expressing high regard for Johannes Kepler and the Imperial Councilor Wackher von Wackhenfels. The page then transitions into a Latin summary of the preceding sentiments.</meta>

the Dog Star <note>original: "Cane," referring to Sirius in the constellation Canis Major.</note> appears no larger than the fiftieth part of Jupiter, nevertheless its irradiation <note>A halo of light or glare surrounding a bright object.</note> is so great and fierce that the globe itself is entangled and almost lost among its own rays, and is distinguished with some difficulty, whereas Jupiter—and even more so Saturn—are seen as well-defined and with a faint and, so to speak, quiet light. And therefore I believe we shall reason well by attributing the cause of the <term>scintillation: the twinkling or rapid change in brightness of a star</term> of the fixed stars to the vibration they make of their own native splendor from their innermost substance, whereas on the surface of the planets, the illumination that derives and originates from the Sun ends and finishes sooner. If I hear of any particular question sought by the same Lord Wackher, I shall not fail to labor over it to show myself as I am, most desirous to serve such a great Lord, and not indeed with the hope of adding to the conclusion reached by his discourse, for I understand very well that to whatever has passed through the finest sieve <note>original: "cribro," a metaphor for a rigorous or critical examination.</note> of his judgment and that of Mr. Kepler, no further refinement can be added; nor would I pretend to anything else than, by doubting and poor reasoning, to incite them to the discovery of new subtleties. The singular minds that flourish in great numbers in Germany have long kept me in a desire to see it, which desire is now doubled by the new favor of the Most Illustrious Wackher, which would make any small occasion that presented itself to me seem great. But I have occupied Your Illustrious and Reverend Lordship for too long; deign finally to offer and dedicate me as a most devoted servant to the Most Illustrious Mr. Wackher, also greeting Mr. Kepler warmly; and I kiss your hands with every reverence and pray to the Lord God for your greatest happiness. From Florence, the 26th of March 1611.
Gallileo de' Gallilei.

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of celestial bodies. Galileo is now addressing the physical nature of light in the solar system, specifically arguing that planets are dark bodies reflecting sunlight, while stars generate their own light. This is a crucial moment in the development of the telescopic evidence for the Copernican system.</meta>

...certain things which he himself had long held for truths: so that I might make this possession of so dear a favor firmer for myself, I ask that you report these things from me to him: that I have most certain demonstrations ready at hand, that, just as He holds, all the Planets indeed receive light from the Sun, being themselves by nature dark and opaque bodies; whereas the Fixed stars shine with their own natural light, not needing illumination from the Sun's rays. For whether the Sun's rays reach the highest region of the fixed stars with even as much clarity as the rays of the fixed stars descend to us from there, God knows. 

The primary foundation of my reasoning consists in this: that with the <term>Ocular: the telescope, or the lens system of the instrument</term> I have clearly observed that the Planets—as each one at any time is nearer to us and the Sun—receive a greater splendor and reflect it more brilliantly. Therefore Mars in perigee <note>original: "perigæus"; the point in an orbit where a planet is closest to Earth.</note>, being of course nearest to the earth, leaves Jupiter behind by a significant gap in splendor, even though in terms of the size of its body, it yields far to Jupiter. And thus it is difficult to capture this radiation of Mars with the Ocular; for it is so great that it hinders the sight, preventing it from distinguishing the roundly-defined disk of the body of the Martian star. This does not happen in Jupiter, for it appears exquisitely circular. 

After this, Saturn, on account of its own very great distance, appears most exactly defined; both the larger globe in the middle, and its two small pills <note>original: "pilulæ"; Galileo famously described the rings of Saturn as two smaller companion bodies or "ears" because his telescope could not yet resolve them as rings.</note> at the sides. For it appears with a faint and broken light, without such irradiation as might prevent the distinct perception of its three most clearly defined little globes. Since, therefore, we see Mars when nearby most splendidly illuminated by the Sun, while the light of the more distant Jupiter is much fainter (however much it appears bright enough without the use of an instrument, which happens to it because of the size and brightness of its body), and that of the most distant Saturn is the faintest and, as it were, watery: what kind of light, do you think, would appear from the fixed stars, which are further from the Sun than Saturn by an ineffable distance, if they were only illuminated by the Sun? Altogether most weak, murky, and dead. And yet we experience the plain contrary. For let us survey with our eyes, for example, the Dog Star <note>original: "stellam Canis," referring to Sirius.</note>; we shall encounter a most vivid glow—

<meta>catchword: ...vivid</meta>

<summary>On this page, Galileo provides telescopic evidence that planets are opaque bodies reflecting sunlight, noting how their brightness varies with distance, whereas stars must be self-luminous. He describes his early observations of Saturn's "pills" (rings) and the intense glare of Mars.</summary>
<keywords>Galileo, telescope, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Sirius, planetary light, fixed stars, perigee, astronomical observation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical properties of light as seen through the telescope. In the previous section, the author discussed how planets reflect sunlight while stars possess their own light. Here, the text concludes Galileo’s observations on the "twinkling" of stars versus the steady light of planets, before moving on to mention Simon Marius and the reception of these ideas in Germany and Prague.</meta>

...splendid, which as if it pricks the eye with a most rapid vibration of rays, of such vigor that, compared to it, the planets—suppose Jupiter, and even Venus itself—are so overwhelmed and diminished that they seem like the cheapest and most impure glass compared to the most polished and brilliant diamond.

And although the disk of the Dog Star <note>original: "stellæ Canis"; the star Sirius, the brightest star in the night sky.</note> appears no larger than a fiftieth part of Jupiter’s disk, its radiation is nonetheless huge and quite violent, so much so that the appearance of the disk hides itself within the "hairs" <note>An early modern way of describing the rays or diffraction spikes surrounding a bright point of light.</note> of its own radiation, entangles itself, and almost vanishes, and is not discerned except with some difficulty from the surrounding rays. On the contrary, Jupiter, and much more so Saturn, seem well-defined; their light is languid and, so to speak, quiet. Therefore, I think we shall philosophize correctly if we attribute the cause of the twinkling <note>original: "scintillationis"</note> of the fixed stars to the vibration of their own native splendor, implanted in their very substance; conversely, on the surface of the planets, let us say that this illumination is reflected from nearby, being derived from the Sun and spread into the world. These scientific matters are in Galileo’s letters; the rest I omit. You see then, studious reader, how the most skillful mind of Galileo—by my soul, a most excellent philosopher—using this spyglass <term>Perspicillum: the early name for the telescope</term> of ours like a set of ladders, scales the very furthest and highest walls of the visible world. He surveys everything firsthand, and from there looks down upon these little huts of ours—I mean the planetary globes—with the sharpest reasoning, comparing the outermost with the innermost, and the highest with the lowest, with solid judgment.

But because in philosophy there is never a lack of competing schools of thought or criticisms, and many throughout Germany will require the testimony of Germans here: come, I present to them a letter from a certain German concerning these same matters. From this it will also be clear that Galileo did not act poorly when, looking after his own affairs, he communicated his discoveries to us in Prague early on, though through riddles <note>original: "per gryphos"; referring to Galileo’s practice of sending discoveries as anagrams to ensure priority while he continued his research.</note>.

<margin>a. This freed Kepler from fear; for he was naturally very afraid for his honor if Marius had opposed the motion of the Earth while mentioning his name.</margin>
Thus, then, Marius <note>Simon Marius (1573–1625), a German astronomer who claimed to have discovered the moons of Jupiter independently of Galileo.</note> wrote to our common friend: *In the meantime, I am attempting another work: in which I first assert the immobility of the Earth, having entirely omitted personal matters;* <insert>a</insert> *but only arguments are examined against the Copernican reasons, which in our time Kepler with Galileo of Padua...*

<summary>This page concludes Galileo's argument that stars generate their own "vibrating" light while planets merely reflect "quiet" sunlight, and introduces a letter from Simon Marius regarding the Copernican system.</summary>
<keywords>Sirius, twinkling stars, planetary light, telescope, Galileo, Kepler, Simon Marius, Copernicus, Prague, riddles</keywords>

b. A first omen of victory before the battle, which Marius—due to human ignorance and the magnitude of this school of thought—restricts between two limits, though it is now almost public knowledge; unless all the flower of learned men be shut within the fences of the Academies.

> *He approves as a Mathematician, and seriously determines it to be so.* **b.** *I take my arguments for this assertion from the sacred writings;* **c** *with both physics* **d** *and Astronomy also agreeing. Next, the opinion of those who thought the celestial bodies were of such monstrous size will be refuted; and a new, more plausible measurement of their quantity will be handed down by me. In this matter, the Belgian instrument, commonly called the Spyglass* <note>original: "Perspicillum"; the early term for a telescope.</note>, *has helped me most. Third, I shall demonstrate that Venus is illuminated by the Sun no differently [than the moon], and that she is rendered crescent-shaped,* **half-divided** <note>original: "διχότομον" (dichotomon); referring to the phase where the planet appears as a half-circle.</note>, *etc., just as she was observed and seen by me many times and most diligently with the help of the Belgian spyglass from the end of last year,* **e** *up until April of the present year, when Venus was closest to the Earth, both in the west and in the east. Fourth, I shall deal with the new Jovian planets* <note>The "Medicean stars" or moons of Jupiter.</note>, *which are carried around Jupiter, just as the other planets are around the Sun, though with unequal spacing and periods. I have already investigated the periods of the two outermost ones and constructed tables, so that from them the distance in minutes to the right or left of Jupiter can be very easily known at any time. These last two chapters are completely unheard of in any age. Perhaps other things will also occur while I work. Thus far Marius.*

You have, therefore, friend reader, the reliability of the spyglass confirmed in the observation of new heavenly bodies, by the testimony of one German besides. What then should prevent me from composing this Geometric Panegyric <note>A formal work of high praise, here written in the language of geometry.</note> in this little book for so excellent an instrument, and you, reader, for the sake of honor, from being present with a ready mind and no ordinary attention while I recite it? By this work you will both sharpen your wit and, through the perception of causes, emerge more learned in philosophy, better equipped for mechanics and the invention of useful and pleasant things, and finally, more cautious and safer from the thousand ways in which the common people are usually led into error. Farewell, and take this prelude in good part.

**e.** At which very time Galileo wrote from Florence to Prague concerning the Mother of Loves <note>original: "Matre amorum"; a poetic astronomical code for the planet Venus.</note>, and even then predicted to Marius that these things would appear in this order.

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page marks the formal beginning of Johannes Kepler’s *Dioptrice* (1611). While the previous page provided introductory context regarding the astronomical discoveries of Galileo and Simon Marius, we now move into the mathematical and physical foundations of optics. Kepler is transitioning from the "what" of celestial observation to the "how" of the telescope's function. The catchword on the previous page was "DIOPTRI-", which connects directly to the title here.</meta>

# DIOPTRICS,
<note>original: "DIOPTRICE"; the branch of optics dealing with the refraction of light, especially through lenses.</note>

### or

### DEMONSTRATION OF THOSE THINGS WHICH HAPPEN TO
### sight and visible objects because of Spyglasses, that is, glass or
### transparent Crystals.
<note>original: "Conspicilla"; at this time, the word "telescope" was not yet the standard term; Kepler and his contemporaries often used "perspicillum" or "conspicillum," which we translate as "spyglass."</note>

### I. DEFINITION.

**Inclination** upon a surface is taken from the angle between the **perpendicular** <term>perpendicular: a line at a right angle (90°) to the surface</term> of the surface, and any other **ray** that intersects the perpendicular at a point on the surface.

### II. OPTICAL AXIOM.

Rays entering into a denser medium <note>such as moving from air into glass or water</note> at an angle are **refracted**, and once refracted inside the body, they move toward the perpendicular erected on the surface of the dense medium at the point where the ray strikes. These same rays, when exiting from a denser medium, are refracted, and once refracted outside the dense body, they move away from this perpendicular.

### III. OPTICAL AXIOM.

The refraction of rays is the same whether they enter or exit by their own nature, or are considered as such.
<note>Kepler is stating the principle of reversibility: light follows the same path regardless of which direction it is traveling.</note>

### IV. PROBLEM.

To skillfully measure the refractions of a hard transparent body at every inclination of the rays.

<image-desc>A perspective technical drawing on the right side of the page depicting an optical experiment setup. A rectangular transparent prism, labeled with vertices A, B, C, D, E, and F, is placed inside a recessed base or frame. Three light rays, labeled L, M, and N, strike the top surface (DE) of the prism from the upper left. The rays are shown as lines entering the medium and undergoing refraction. Internal points within the prism and base are marked with letters G, H, I, and K to illustrate the geometry of the light's path.</image-desc>

*Let there be a hard transparent body **AE**. Let it be bounded by one carefully prepared flat surface **DE**, upon which two other flat surfaces are erected parallel to each other and at right angles to the first, which shall be **BA** and **EF**. Match to this a frame* <note>original: "capulum"; a holder or casing for the experiment.</note> *made from any material, such as wood, whose surfaces, especially the inner ones*

<meta>catchword: may be [sint]</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the fundamental definitions and axioms of Johannes Kepler’s optics, specifically defining the angle of inclination and the basic laws of refraction as light enters and exits dense media.</summary>
<keywords>Dioptrics, refraction, optical axiom, inclination, perpendicular, spyglass, Kepler, light rays, transparent body</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Problem IV from the previous page, where Kepler is describing a physical experiment to measure how light bends (refracts) as it passes from air into a denser medium like glass or water.</meta>

*...let them be well-leveled, with the two sides rising from the base H at right angles, so that B E H and the others are right angles; and let the transparent body* <note>original: "pellucidum"; likely a piece of glass or crystal</note> *be stretched into the corner so it sits deep in the hollow corner of the small container* <term>capulus: a small box or vessel used for this optical experiment</term>*, filling it completely. However, let the side of the container D C project beyond the boundary of the transparent side D B by a small space B C. In height, however, let both B E be equal, so that the surface above appears as one, consisting of both the transparent and the opaque.*

*Once this is done and the bodies are joined, let side D C—which is a part common to both bodies through the section D B—be exposed perpendicularly to the Sun’s rays, at whatever inclination the plane B A has toward those same rays.*

*Let the rays of the Sun be L D, M B, and N C. Of these, the rays between M B H and N C encounter no transparent body except air; they will therefore proceed in a straight line across B C toward M B H and N C K. Thus, the edge C B will cast a shadow H K onto the base of the container, and sometimes onto its opposite side.*

*In this case, therefore, the declination* <note>the angle of the sun relative to the "top" or vertex of the experiment</note> *of the Sun from the vertex of the plane B A is found from the proportion of the height B E to the shadow E H. For as B E is to E H, so is the Total Sine* <note>original: "Sinus Totus"; in Kepler's time, this referred to the radius of the trigonometric circle, used here to calculate the tangent</note> *to the Tangent of the distance of the Sun from the vertex of the plane B A: that is, the angle E B H.*

*However, those rays of the Sun that fall between M B and L D strike the denser transparent surface B A and are refracted toward the perpendicular B E; thus M B is refracted into B G, and L D into D I. And B D will cast a shorter shadow, G I, through the Crystal. This quantity can be observed by eye if the base of the container was previously marked with ink into specific divisions. This is possible because the body covering the base is transparent.*

*Again, therefore, just as height B E is to the shadow E G, so is the Total Sine to the Tangent of the angle E B G.*

*By subtracting the angle E B G found here from the angle E B H found previously, the remainder is G B H: the measure of the refracted angle at this specific inclination, E B H.*

---

## V. PROBLEM.

->To prove the refractions of greater inclinations, and at the same<-
->time the previous ones, more conveniently by another way.<-

<meta>catchword: Fiat (Let it be done)</meta>

<summary>This page describes a practical experiment using a shadow-box (capulus) and a crystal to measure the angle of refraction. By comparing the shadow cast in open air to the shorter shadow cast through the crystal, Kepler uses trigonometry (sines and tangents) to calculate exactly how much the light has bent.</summary>

<keywords>refraction, trigonometry, tangent, sine, shadow-box, optical experiment, solar rays, crystal</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s investigation into the laws of optics. In the previous section, he described a method for calculating the sun's angle using shadows. Here, he moves to a more specific experiment using a cylindrical piece of crystal to measure exactly how much light bends—refracts—when entering a solid medium from the air.</meta>

Let a cylindrical body <note>original: "corpus cylindraceum"</note> be made from a transparent plate thick enough—say, half a finger's width. Let this be labeled **A G**, and its thickness **F A**. Let the plate be perforated along the path of the diameter of the circle, so that **F A** is a long opening passing through the center **C** and exiting at **G**. Or, in place of an opening, let a ruler be fixed upon the cylindrical body along the path **A C G**, with sights <term>dioptris: sighting devices or alidades used to align the instrument with the sun</term> of equal height at **A** and **G**. Let the circular edge <note>original: "limbus circularis"</note> be divided into 360 parts, starting from **E**, so that **A E** is a quadrant <note>a 90-degree arc</note>. 

Now, let the opening or the sight **A G** be directed toward the Sun, and let the sunlight that entered through **A** be visible beyond **G**, on the opposite spot or wall. Since the entire semicircle is illuminated at once, with a quadrant stretched out on both sides from **A**, it is clear that a line drawn tangent to the surface of the cylinder at **E** (let this be **D E**) is parallel to **A G**. Thus, it comes from the Sun as the outermost of the rays falling onto the semicircle of the cylinder.

[A geometric diagram of a circle representing a cross-section of a cylinder. A horizontal diameter is labeled with points F, A, C (the center), and G. Point E is at the bottom of the circle, with a tangent line labeled D E. Point B is on the lower right arc of the circle. A radius connects center C to point B, and another line connects C to E. Small hatch marks indicate shading or rays entering at F/A and G.]

Therefore, move an opaque stylus around the cylindrical surface from **A F** toward **E**, and observe where its shadow falls on the opposite edge near parts **G B**. Suppose that when it is placed at **E**, the shadow falls at **B**. Half of the circumference **E B**, therefore, measures the angle of refraction of the ray **D E**, which has the greatest inclination from the vertex, since it touches the cylindrical surface of the crystal at **E**.

### ->VI. AXIOM.<-

->The refractions of crystal and glass are nearly the same.<-

### ->VII. AXIOM.<-

->The refractions of crystal, up to thirty degrees of inclination, are perceptibly proportional to the inclinations.<-

### ->VIII. AXIOM.<-

->The angle of refraction in crystal is, up to the stated limit,<-

<meta>Catchword: minum,</meta>

<summary>Kepler describes a practical experiment using a cylindrical crystal and sighting tools to measure the "angle of refraction"—how much light bends when hitting a surface at its most extreme angle. He concludes with axioms stating that glass and crystal behave similarly and that light bends proportionally at lower angles.</summary>

<keywords>refraction, optics, crystal, cylinder, geometry, light rays, axioms, measurement instruments</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's experimental setup involving a cylindrical crystal. The previous page established that the angle of refraction in air is approximately one-third of the angle of inclination. This page formalizes these observations into axioms and propositions regarding the limits and proportions of refraction.</meta>

...as a rule, the refraction is approximately a third part of the inclination in the air.

## IX. AXIOM

The maximum refraction of Crystal is approximately 48 degrees. <note>Kepler is identifying the maximum possible bending of light as it passes between media, a precursor to the concept of the critical angle.</note>

## X. OPTICAL AXIOM

Inclination <term>inclinatio: the angle at which a ray of light strikes a surface</term> causes refraction. Equal inclinations of rays located in the same medium cause equal refractions (or equal angles of refraction); a greater inclination causes a greater refraction; and no inclination causes none—that is, a ray striking perpendicularly is not refracted.

## XI. OPTICAL AXIOM

Rays falling from different luminous points onto the same point of a denser surface cross one another, and the positions of the incident rays are swapped in the refracted rays, just as if the crossing occurred without refraction.

*This is proved in the Optics through Axiom X.*

## XII. PROPOSITION

Refractions, when precisely weighed, are not proportional to the inclinations in the air.

*For by Proposition VIII, when the inclination is 30°, the refraction is 10°. Triple both. Therefore, in this proportion, an inclination of 90° would result in a refraction of 30°; yet experience, according to Axiom IX, gives 48°.* <note>Kepler is demonstrating that the relationship between the angle of incidence and the angle of refraction is not a simple linear ratio, debunking earlier simplified theories.</note>

## XIII. PROPOSITION

No ray that is inclined more than 42° upon a surface from within the body of the Crystal will be able to penetrate that surface from the peak <note>original: "a vertice"</note>.

*In the diagram <note>original: "in Schemate"</note>, let the body of the crystal be **AC**, and the flat surface be **FCO**. Let **AC** be inclined upon this surface more than 42°. Therefore, angle **FCA** will be less than 48°. If **AC** exits into the air, the refracted ray outside in the air will either touch the surface...*

<summary>Kepler defines the physical limits of how light bends in crystal, noting that refraction is not a simple linear proportion of the angle of incidence and establishing a maximum refraction angle of 48 degrees.</summary>
<keywords>refraction, optics, crystal, angle of incidence, proportional bending, Kepler, optical axioms, light rays</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the proof of Proposition XIII, which establishes the "critical angle" for light traveling within a crystal. The previous page introduced the idea that if a ray's internal angle is too steep relative to the surface, it cannot exit. This page completes that proof and applies it to a practical optical problem regarding shadows.</meta>

...would end at CO, or it will not reach it but will be raised above it, as if at CG. But neither is possible. For by Axiom IX, the refraction of CO itself <note>original: "contingentis," referring to a ray grazing the surface</note> is 48°. Therefore, the refracted ray of OC is CH, which is further inward than CA, because the angle FCA is assumed to be less than 48°. Because OC is refracted into CH and not into CA, it follows that AC will not be refracted into CO by Axiom III <note>Axiom III refers to the principle of reversibility: if light travels from A to B, it can travel from B to A along the same path</note>. Nor is GC refracted into CA. For by Axiom XI, rays GC and OC coming to the same point C cross one another; and if the refraction of the higher ray GC is lower than CH, it certainly cannot be higher than CA. Therefore, the ray AC cannot pass through point C.

---

### XIV. PROPOSITION. PROBLEM.
### To project shadows toward the Sun.

->A Crystal Cube accomplishes this.<-

Let FO be a cube and B β represent the Sun. Let A ω be a small body <term>corpusculum: a tiny object or particle</term> on the surface of the cube FA. Therefore, the rays BA and β ω, which form the boundaries of the shadow on the outside, are refracted into AC and ω κ. These lines CA and κ ω are necessarily raised more than 48° above the points of the surface A ω, according to Axiom IX. 

However, since the angle of the Cube AFC is a right angle, and CAF is more than 48°, the angle FCA will be less than 42°. Thus, AC and ω κ are inclined more than 48° (and thus also more than 42°) from the peak <note>the "vertex" or perpendicular</note> of surface CF. Therefore, by Proposition XIII, the rays AC and ω κ will not penetrate the surface FC. 

Because of the principles of Optics, they will be entirely reflected <note>Kepler is describing what we now call Total Internal Reflection</note> onto the surface OD, at equal angles ACF and DCO. And because the angle of the cube COD is a right angle, and DCO (being equal to ACF) is less than 42°, the angle CDO will be more than 48°. Consequently, it is inclined less than 42° from the peak of surface DO; therefore, it is able to exit at E; likewise, κ δ exits at ε. 

In this way, the shadow of the small body A ω falls at E ε in a reversed position, and it appears closer to the Sun than the object A ω itself, if the lines DE and δ ε were to be extended further.

<page-num>5</page-num>
<sig>A 3</sig>

<summary>This page concludes the proof that light hitting an internal surface at a shallow angle cannot exit (the critical angle) and demonstrates Proposition XIV: how a crystal cube uses total internal reflection to project a shadow back toward the light source.</summary>

<keywords>refraction, critical angle, total internal reflection, crystal cube, shadow projection, optics, geometry of light</keywords>

This page continues the argument by explaining how a small object on the surface would cast its shadow peak toward the light source.</meta>

In the same way it can be demonstrated that if an upright little tower <term>turricula</term> is placed at ω, the peak of the shadow E will be turned toward the Sun.

### XV. PROPOSITION.

->Rays can penetrate the linear angle of a triangular Prism<-
->formed from glass or Crystal with an equilateral triangle.<-

<image-desc>A geometric diagram of a prism represented by triangle ABC with vertices labeled A (top), B (bottom left), and C (bottom right). A horizontal line segment DE is drawn inside the triangle, parallel to the base BC. Light rays are shown entering and exiting: a ray from F enters the left side at point D and continues through to exit the right side at point E towards G. Another ray enters from H at point E, passes through to point D and exits towards K. A reflected ray is shown from D towards I, and another line segment extends from D to M on the base. Points are labeled F, I, A, H, D, E, B, M, C, G, K.</image-desc>

*Let there be an equilateral section ABC within the Prism. Draw DE parallel to BC itself, which represents some ray. I say that an exit into the air is open to it on both sides, both at D and at E. For the angle ABC is 60°, and consequently the angle ADE is also 60°. The complement, or the distance of the point D on the surface DA from the vertex <note>The "vertex" here refers to the perpendicular or normal line drawn from the surface</note>, is 30°, which is less than 42° <note>The author previously established 42° as the "critical angle" beyond which light is trapped inside glass</note>. Therefore, the ray ED will exit toward F. So also, from the opposite side, the ray DE will exit toward G.*

### XVI. SENSORY AXIOM.

The most pleasant colors of the Rainbow <term>Iris</term> arise when the refraction is of such a great magnitude; and this occurs both if the eyes look through the glass, and if the Sun shines through it.

### XVII.

When the Sun irradiates a prism, three kinds of rays result: the Sincere ray, one with the color of the glass, and one with the colors of the Rainbow.

<image-desc>Identical geometric diagram as described above, illustrating light rays (refraction and reflection) interacting with a triangular prism ABC. Labels: F, I, A, H, D, E, B, M, C, G, K.</image-desc>

*For let F be the Sun. Let it radiate toward D. Here, the density of the solar ray is, as it were, divided; its smallest part is reflected <note>original: "repercutitur"</note> toward DI, at an angle ADI equal to the angle BDF at which it falls. It therefore vibrates a "sincere" ray <term>sincerus: pure or unaltered</term>, though a thin one, through DI to I. It is sincere because it is not tinted by the glass, as it does not enter the body of the glass.*

However, the greater part of the density of the ray FD penetrates D and is refracted into DE. But at E it is divided again, the reason—

<summary>This page discusses the geometry of light passing through an equilateral triangular prism and introduces the "sincere" ray, which reflects off the surface without entering the glass. It also notes that the most vibrant rainbow colors are produced by significant refraction.</summary>
<keywords>prism, refraction, rainbow colors, sincere ray, equilateral triangle, optical geometry, critical angle</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of light passing through glass prisms. The previous page (Proposition 15) established that light can pass through a 60° equilateral prism because the angles of incidence are less than the critical angle of 42°. This page concludes that thought and moves to Proposition 18, which discusses why light cannot pass through a 90° corner of a glass block.</meta>

...of density. For the greater part [of the light] passes through E, and because of the double large refraction, it casts the colors of the Rainbow <note>original: "Iridis"</note> towards G.

The very thin remainder of this ray, DE, is reflected from the surface AC into EM. But if DE strikes AE a little more obliquely, it is then refracted into EM more obliquely than in this case. For if you decrease the angle DEA, then the angle MEC must also be decreased, according to the law of reflection. And thus, finally, EM will strike the base BC perpendicularly, and therefore nothing will be refracted at point M. However, when the ray FD has passed through the glass body twice in this manner—once as DE and again as EM—and exits straight through M, it casts a ray tinted with the color of the glass towards K, appearing more directly from the region of A. For we are taught in the study of Optics <term>Opticis</term> that bright rays are tinted when they pass through colored media.

### PROPOSITION 18.

If a body made of Crystal or glass has a right angle, and it is placed between the eye and a visible object, it will not transmit the rays of the object to the eye. Instead, the surface of the Crystal facing the object will appear opaque and tinted with the color of the body itself.

<image-desc>A line diagram showing a right-angled triangle. The top vertex is labeled A, the vertex at the right angle (bottom left) is labeled F, and the bottom right vertex is labeled C. A line segment CA forms the hypotenuse.</image-desc>
</margin>
*For let there be a ray CA inside the body. It will be inclined either equally upon the surfaces FC and FA, or unequally. If it is inclined equally, it will be inclined at an angle greater than 42°—specifically 45°. Therefore, it will not pass through either one surface or the other, according to Proposition 13 <note>The author uses 42° as the "critical angle" for glass; at 45°, light undergoes total internal reflection and cannot escape the glass</note>. But if it is inclined unequally, it was demonstrated in Proposition 13 that it will not pass through at least one of them. Therefore, no ray can pass through both surfaces of a right-angled crystalline body at the same time.*

### 19. OPTICAL AXIOM.

The location of an object is judged by the direction <term>plaga</term> in which the visual ray first exits from the eye, regardless of how much that ray might be changed by refraction along its path between the object and the eye. This is because the eye cannot perceive what happens to the rays when they encounter different media...

<meta>catchword: ...outside.</meta>

<summary>This page explains the rainbow-like effects of light passing through prisms and demonstrates why a 90-degree glass corner acts as a mirror rather than a window due to total internal reflection. It also introduces a fundamental axiom of optics: that our eyes perceive the location of objects based on the initial direction the light enters the eye.</summary>
<keywords>refraction, rainbow colors, critical angle, total internal reflection, right-angled glass, optical axiom, perception of location</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on prisms from the previous page, specifically focusing on how the orientation of a prism affects the perceived position of an object. It marks a transition from "flat crystals" (prisms) to "curvilinear" or curved lenses.</meta>

...happens outside itself; but the eye thinks those rays continue always in the same direction <note>original: "plagam"</note> as they began.

### PROPOSITION 20.

With the angle of the prism turned upward, those things that are opposite appear above; when turned downward, they appear below; when to the right, they appear to the right; when to the left, they appear to the left.

*Let us return to the previous drawing for Proposition 17. Let the upward angle be A, and the eye be F. Therefore, the ray FD is carried into DE, and at point D it deflects by 20 degrees (by Proposition 16) from the path DH. Furthermore, DE is carried into EG, deflecting by another 20 degrees from the path DE, and thus by 40 degrees from the path FDH—which is almost half of a right angle <note>A right angle is 90 degrees; 40 degrees is close to the 45 degrees mentioned as "half".</note>. Yet, although the objects at G are actually below, the eye at F thinks it sees them at H above, according to Proposition 19.*

---

->Thus far concerning flat Crystal; now concerning curved ones:<-
->First, concerning Light.<-

### DEFINITION 21.

Let the movement of light toward a location be expressed by the word **to verge** <term>vergere</term>. Rays are said to **converge** when, proceeding from a source, they come closer and closer together. They **diverge** when, proceeding from a source, they move further and further apart from each other. Therefore, those rays which converge will, after meeting at a point <term>sectione facta</term>, thereafter diverge.

### DEFINITION 22.

Radiant points <term>puncta radiantia</term> are called **far** or **remote** when they are at such a distance that the diameter of the eye's pupil vanishes in comparison to it. They are **near**, however, when there is a perceptible proportion between the diameter of the pupil and the distance.

### POSTULATE 23.

A certain distant point of a visible object, although it radiates in a
