...the world from all sides. However, with respect to the eye or the telescope <term>perspicillum</term> <note>At the time of writing, "perspicillum" was the common Latin term for the newly-invented telescope.</note>, at such a distance that the diameter of the lens has no perceptible proportion to it, the rays reaching the edges of the eye or telescope are assumed to be parallel. Of these, only a single ray can be perpendicular to the curved surface it strikes.

### XXIV. DEFINITION.

Therefore, the rays from a single point of a nearby visible object **diverge** toward the pupil of the eye. However, individual rays from multiple points of any visible object **converge** toward the center of vision <term>centrum visus</term>. This occurs if the radiation is free <note>meaning unobstructed by any intervening lens or medium</note>. It is therefore very important to distinguish when we are discussing the radiation of a single point versus the radiations of multiple points compared with one another.

<image-desc>A narrow, vertical diagram of a ray bundle. At the top apex, two points are labeled B and C. Lines radiate downward to a horizontal base line marked D on the left and E on the right. A central ray extends from the apex down to a point A at the center of the base. The triangular area is shaded with many fine vertical lines representing the path of the rays.</image-desc>

*CD, CA, and CE diverge toward the eye DE; similarly, BD, BA, BE and all intermediate rays diverge. But BA and CA converge toward the center of the eye, A.*

---

->On the Lens.<-

### XXV. DEFINITION.

A lens <term>lens</term> is a piece of glass or crystal in the form of a circular disk, which is wider than it is thick <note>original: "profundior", literally "deeper".</note>.

### XXVI.

A **convex** lens is one which is convex on both surfaces, or on only one surface while the other remains flat.

The same is to be understood for a **concave** <note>original: "cauâ"</note> lens. Both types may be called "pure" <note>original: "pura"</note> by common terminology.

### XXVII.

**Mixed** lenses are those which are convex on one surface and concave on the other, with a perfect circular curvature on both sides; these are clearly the opposite of the "Pure" lenses.

<summary>This page defines the behavior of light rays relative to the eye's pupil and the center of vision, while introducing the basic geometric categories of lenses: convex, concave, and mixed.</summary>
<keywords>optics, lens, convex, concave, divergence, convergence, telescope, radiation</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical definitions of optical lenses, following the introduction of the term "lens" on the previous page. It transitions from physical descriptions of glass shapes to the geometric principles of how they focus light.</meta>

### XXVIII.

"Convex," "concave," and "mixed" are understood in the neuter gender to refer to a telescope <term>perspicillum</term> <note>At this time, "perspicillum" was the standard term for a telescope or magnifying glass.</note>, a glass, or a body, etc. It means the same as a convex, concave, or mixed lens <term>lens</term>.

### XXIX.

The physical size of the lens itself is one thing, and the size of the convexity or concavity within the lens is another. The former is the size of the body; the latter is the size of the shape <note>Kepler is distinguishing between the diameter of the glass disk and the radius of the circle used to grind the curve.</note>.

### XXX.

This physical size of the body has a double aspect. It is either absolute, as when the disks or circles of the lenses themselves are measured and compared to one another; or it is relative to the circle of its own convexity—namely, what portion of its own circle of convexity the lens represents.

### XXXI.

When we speak of a convexity or concavity of a small or large circle, it is understood to refer to the shape and conformation, not to the physical body of the glass.

### XXXII.

The convexity or concavity of a small circle is "large" <note>meaning a steeper, more pronounced curve</note>; that of a large circle is "small" <note>meaning a shallower, flatter curve</note>.

### XXXIII. Postulate.

That both surfaces of a convex, concave, or mixed lens should have the center of their respective circles on the same line, which passes through the central "navel" <term>umbilicum</term> <note>The optical axis passing through the center of the lens.</note> of the lens.

->The convergence of the lens.<-

### XXXIV. PROPOSITION.

If a point sends parallel rays into a convex lens that is a portion of a circle smaller than 30°, and the lens is positioned perpendicularly to them—even if nothing else happens to the rays except that they are refracted upon entry—then, while that single ray which passes through the center of the sphere remains unrefracted <note>original: <italic>irrefracto</italic></note> as it strikes the surface perpendicularly, the others, having undergone refraction, meet the perpendicular ray at approximately one and a half diameters <term>sesquidiametrum</term> of the sphere.

<summary>Kepler defines the geometric relationship between a lens's physical size and its curvature, establishing that steeper curves belong to smaller circles. He concludes with a proposition on how parallel rays converge at a specific distance (the focal point) after entering a convex lens.</summary>

<keywords>convexity, concavity, lens geometry, refraction, optical axis, focal point, telescope, perspicillum</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Proposition XXXIV, moving from the premise of the proposition into the geometric proof and calculation of the focal point in a crystal sphere. It relies on the definitions of curvature and axes established on the previous page.</meta>

Let there be some distant point that shines light upon a section of a crystal sphere <term>sphæra crystallina</term> <note>A common term for a glass sphere used in optical experiments.</note>, B D. Let the arc B C D be less than 30°. According to Proposition 24, these rays of light will be parallel. Among these rays, only I C is perpendicular to the surface, as it passes through the center A.

In addition to the perpendicular ray I C, let any one of the parallel rays in the air be chosen—let it be H G. Because H G strikes the surface B G C at an angle, it will be refracted <term>refractio</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes from air into a denser medium like glass.</note> toward the perpendicular line (G A) drawn from the point of impact G, in accordance with Proposition 2. As a result, after passing point G, the rays I C and H G are no longer parallel and will eventually meet. Let their meeting point be F, and let the ray H G be refracted along the path G F. We assume that nothing further happens to ray H G after it passes point G. I maintain, therefore, that the distance A F is double the distance C A, and thus A F is equal to the diameter <term>diametrum</term> of the sphere B C D.

The ray H G, being parallel to the perpendicular I C, is inclined at an angle equal to G A C. If the refraction <note>The "angle of refraction" in Kepler’s terms refers to how much the ray's path is deflected from its original course.</note> were equal to this inclination, the ray H G would be bent toward G A—that is, toward the center itself. However, because the refraction is not equal to the inclination, nor is it "three-thirds" (total), but rather "one-third" of the inclination (according to Proposition 9 <note>Kepler used a simplified rule of thumb: for small angles of incidence in glass, the light bends toward the normal by about one-third of the angle of incidence.</note>), the refracted ray G F deviates from the line G A by two-thirds of the inclination G A C. 

Therefore, angle F G A is two-thirds of angle G A C. Now, because the angles in triangle A G F (specifically A G F and A F G) together must equal the exterior angle G A C, it follows that angle G F A is one-third of G A C, and is thus half of angle F G A. According to the principles of trigonometry <term>doctrina Triangulorum</term>, the ratio of the sides G A to A F is the same as the ratio of the sines <term>sinus</term> of their opposite angles (the half-angle G F A to the double-angle F G A). For angles smaller than 15°, sines are nearly proportional to the angles or arcs themselves. Therefore, they are roughly in a 1:2 ratio. For this reason, the ratio of G A (or C A, the radius <term>semidiameter</term>) to A F is as one to two. Consequently, the total distance C F is approximately one and a half times the diameter <term>sesquidiameter</term> <note>If CA is 1 unit (the radius), then AF is 2 units. The distance from the surface C to the focus F is 1+2=3 units. Since a diameter is 2 units, 3 units is exactly 1.5 (sesqui-) diameters.</note>.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram of optical refraction. Vertical parallel lines representing light rays descend from the top. A central ray, labeled 'I' at the top, passes through vertex 'C', center point 'A', and terminates at point 'F'. A side ray, labeled 'H' at the top, enters the spherical surface at point 'G'. From 'G', the ray is refracted along line 'G F', intersecting the central axis at point 'F'. Construction lines and points 'S', 'B', 'X', and 'D' define the spherical segment and its geometry. The diagram illustrates how a ray entering at point G is focused to point F relative to the center A.</image-desc>

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof for the focal point of a crystal sphere, demonstrating that parallel light rays converge at a point roughly one and a half diameters from the surface.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from Proposition XXXIV, which discussed rays entering a crystal from the air, to Proposition XXXV, which examines the inverse: parallel rays already inside a crystal body as they exit through a convex surface into the air. It continues the geometric proofs regarding the focal points of light in spherical mediums.</meta>

### XXXV. PROPOSITION.

->If parallel rays travel within a convex crystal body:<-
->they will meet with the perpendicular ray outside, at a distance almost<-
->equal to the diameter of the convexity below the convex surface,<-
->provided the arc is less than 30°.<-

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating Proposition XXXV. It depicts multiple vertical parallel rays (labeled L, 2, O, T at the top) entering a crystal body from above. The crystal's boundary is a convex curve labeled PQR, with Q at the vertex. Ray O is the central perpendicular axis. A specific ray TR is shown entering at T, hitting the convex surface at point R, and being refracted towards the central axis, meeting it at point S. Additional points labeled include V, N, and M along the path of the rays and the central axis, and X on the extension of ray TR. The diagram visualizes the convergence of parallel rays after passing through a convex medium.</image-desc>

Let there be a crystal body <term>corpus Crystalli</term> P O R, bounded by the convex surface P Q R. Let some parallel rays travel through this body, of which the middle and perpendicular ray is O Q. Let one of the other rays be T R. I say first that the ray T R is refracted <term>refringi</term> <note>refringi: to be bent or broken at the interface of two mediums.</note> outward at point R into the path R S, with an angle of refraction half as small as the angle of inclination <term>inclinatio</term> <note>The angle of "inclination" is what we today call the angle of incidence; Kepler defines "refraction" as the angle of deviation (how much the path actually bends).</note>. This is because S R X and T R O are the inclinations of the rays S R and T R. Therefore, for every two parts that the angle T R O contains, the angle S R X contains three. This follows because the angle of refraction is a third part of the inclination, according to Proposition 8. Since, therefore, a ray S R entering the crystal would be refracted into R T, then according to Proposition 3, a ray R T exiting the crystal will likewise be refracted into the path S R. Thus, the refraction of ray T R as it exits from the dense medium is half of its internal inclination T R O <note>If the ratio of the angles is 3:2, the "bending" (the difference) is 1 part, which is half of the internal 2 parts.</note>.

I further state that the ray R S meets the central axis O Q at a distance almost equal to the whole diameter <term>diametro</term> of the circle P Q R. For R S O is the amount of refraction, and it is half of T R O (which is equal to R O S) and a third part of X R S. Now, as the sine <term>sinus</term> of angle X R S is to the sine of angle R S O, so is the distance O S to the distance O R. But the sines of such small angles behave nearly exactly like the arcs of the angles themselves. Therefore, the sine of angle X R S is nearly triple the sine of angle R S O. Because of this, the length O S is also triple the length of O R or O Q. Since O Q is the radius <term>semidiameter</term> <note>Kepler uses "semidiameter" to mean the radius of the curve.</note>, the distance from the vertex Q to the meeting point S will be nearly the length of the diameter.

### XXXVI. PROPOSITION.

->If the rays within a dense body are not parallel, but instead<-
->converge toward the convex boundary of the dense medium, in a shorter distance<-

*For let OQ and LN <term>converge</term> <note>Move toward each other.</note> toward QN. And let NZ be parallel to QO, refracted into NS. Therefore LN and ZN intersect each other. Thus the refracted path of the outer ray LN, compared to ZN, becomes more internal than NS (the refraction of ZN) by Proposition XI. It therefore meets with QS above S, say at M. And the distance QM is shorter than the diameter QS.*

## XXXVII. PROPOSITION.

If a source of light <term>punctum radians</term> is placed closer to a convex surface than the diameter of that convexity, its rays—once refracted inside the dense body—will not become parallel, but will spread apart <term>divergent</term>.

*For if QS is the diameter of the convexity, let point M be a source of light closer to the lens than S, with rays MN and MQ spreading apart. Therefore, their refracted paths NL and QO will also spread apart toward LO, as shown in the previous proposition by Proposition XI, although they will spread slightly less than before.*

## XXXVIII. PROPOSITION.

Rays arriving parallel from a single distant point and falling perpendicularly onto a crystalline or glass lens that is convex on both sides will meet at a single point after the lens. This point is closer than the diameter of the circle forming the rear surface, and closer than one and a half times the diameter <term>sesquidiameter</term> <note>A sesquidiameter is a ratio of 1.5 to 1.</note> of the front-facing surface.

*Let the lens be DG, convex on both sides, with the line AF passing perpendicularly through the centers of the convexities. Since AD, CB, and any others are effectively parallel in the air (by Proposition XXIII), the paths DG and BE inside the crystal will converge toward EG (by Proposition XXXIV), as if they were going to meet at point F. Therefore, by Proposition XXXVI, the point F at which...*

## XXXIX. PROPOSITION.

Given the same conditions as before, if both convexities are formed from the same circle, the convergence after the lens will occur at a point that is distant by roughly the radius <term>semidiameter</term> of the front-facing convexity—that is, at its center.

For let BD and EG in the previous diagram be equal convexities, and A and P be the centers of the circles. Let the circles intersect at I, with lines GI extended to K, and DI to M. And through the intersection I, let the perpendiculars AL and PN be drawn from the centers. And through intersection I, let HO pass parallel to AF. Since BD and EG in the previous proposition differ very little, let them be assumed equal, and let the truly equal lines DI and GI be taken for them. Because, therefore, HI is inclined over DIM, it declines from the perpendicular...

<meta>catchword: In angulo</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the proof that light rays passing through a biconvex lens converge more quickly than they would through a single surface, and begins Proposition 39, calculating the focal point for a perfectly symmetrical (equiconvex) lens.</summary>

<keywords>refraction, biconvex lens, convergence, optics, geometry, focal point, diameter, sesquidiameter, semidiameter</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric demonstration from the previous page, specifically calculating the refraction of light rays through a biconvex lens (a lens curved outward on both sides) where both sides have equal curvature. It establishes the focal point of such a lens relative to its radius.</meta>

In the angle HIN, which is equal to OIP or IPD, the refracted ray of HI itself, within the convexity, will deviate from OI toward IP by a third part of OIP, according to Proposition VIII. At the same time, LIO is equal to NIH, because AI and IP are equal and HIO is parallel to AP. Therefore, the refracted ray coming inside the dense body will strike its opposite surface KIG (the perpendicular of which through I is AL) at an angle that is a third larger than LIO. Thus, that refracted ray within the crystal body has four parts of inclination on the opposite surface. But when exiting through I into the open air, it must take on an inclination in the air that is half again as large, because a ray falling from the air into a convex body loses a third part of its inclination within the body, according to Proposition VIII. Therefore, that ray inclined across the lens has six parts in the air, whereas the angle NIH or LIO has three parts. The angle of that inclination is therefore double the angle LIO. Yet LIP is also double LIO, because LIO and OIP are equal. Therefore, IP is that ray coming from HI, twice refracted: once at the entry point I of the facing convexity DIM, and again at the exit point I of the rear convexity GIK. Therefore, P—the center of the facing convexity <term>obversi</term> <note>original: "obversi"; the surface of the lens facing the light source.</note> BDI—is the point of convergence <term>concursus</term> <note>original: "concursus"; the focal point where light rays meet.</note> of the parallel rays CB, AD, and HI, provided the curvatures are equal. Compare Propositions XXXIV, XXXV, and XXXIIX for the sake of memory in this way:
Three semi-diameters <term>semidiametris</term> <note>original: "semidiametris"; the radius of the circle forming the lens's curve.</note> after a single facing convexity, two after a single rear convexity, and one after both combined.

## XL. PORISM.
<note>original: "PORISMA"; a porism is a corollary or a mathematical proposition aimed at affirming the possibility of finding something.</note>

It is clear from this that if the curvatures are unequal, the point of convergence will be behind the lens at a distance that lies between the semi-diameters of both convexities. Specifically, it is greater than the semi-diameter of the smaller curve, because the other surface belongs to a larger circle; if it had been of equal curvature, the measure of the semi-diameter would have been at this interval. But it is smaller than the diameter of the smaller curve, because the surface of the smaller is not acting alone. Finally, it is smaller than the semi-diameter of the larger curve, because if the circle of the smaller surface had been equal to it, then the measure would have been the semi-diameter of the larger; but now it is not equal, but smaller.

<gloss>refraction, convexity, crystal, lens, semi-diameter (radius), porism (corollary), convergence, parallel</gloss>

<summary>This page concludes the proof that for a biconvex lens with equal curvatures, the focal point (convergence of parallel rays) is located exactly at the distance of one radius from the lens. It also introduces a corollary for lenses with unequal curvatures, stating their focal point falls between the radii of the two surfaces.</summary>

<meta>This page follows the geometric proofs of how light refractions behave in biconvex lenses. The previous page established that the focal point of a lens with equal curvatures is located at the center of the lens's own curvature.</meta>

### XLI. PROPOSITION.

Rays from a distant point of a visible object meet close to the lens; the meeting point <term>concursus</term> <note>original: "concursus"; the focal point where light rays intersect after being bent by the lens.</note> of rays from a closer point is further away behind the lens.

*For according to Propositions XXXIII, XXXV, and XXXIV in their three diagrams, the meeting point for a point at an infinite distance is F, S, or P. Conversely, as the light-emitting point <term>puncto radioso</term> approaches the lens—moving from far to near—and is placed at F, S, or P, the meeting point moves out toward infinity, according to those same propositions and Proposition III. Since the extreme points are established, the intermediate points are also established: as the point moves beyond F, S, or P, the meeting of rays occurs within a finite distance, though it remains far away as long as the visible object is very near. Conversely, as the visible object moves further into the distance, the meeting point approaches F, S, or P themselves. Finally, according to Proposition XXXV: if the lens is convex on both sides, and the light-emitting point is distant from the lens by the length of the diameter, the meeting point will also be at a distance of the diameter, with the rays existing parallel to each other within the lens.*

->The effects of the lens in itself.<-

### XLII. DEFINITION.

Since any convex lens forces the rays of a single luminous point toward one specific point, and that point moves further away behind the center if the light source is near than if it is far (according to Proposition XLI); therefore, whenever the "point of convergence" is mentioned simply, without any additional detail, it should be understood as that point toward which the radiations of a distant point—specifically, parallel rays—are forced and meet.

### XLIII. PROBLEM.

To paint visible objects upon a white wall using a convex lens.

*In a <term>camera obscura</term> <note>original: "camera obscura"; literally "dark room." A darkened chamber where light enters through a small hole or lens to project an image of the outside world onto a surface.</note>, let a convex lens occupy a single small window. Let a piece of paper be placed at the point of convergence. For the point of a visible object upon the paper, by all the rays with which it shines into the lens, is brought back together into nearly one*
<meta>catchword: punctum</meta>
point.

It also provides early instructions for using a camera obscura to project images onto paper.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how a lens gathers light from individual points of a visible object to form a complete image on a surface like paper.</meta>

<sig>C</sig>
...is gathered in one point. Since visible things consist of infinite points, infinite such points will be "painted" on the paper—that is, the entire surface of the visible object.

### XLIV. PROPOSITION.

->The image formed by a lens is inverted.<-

For the lens acts as the base upon which two cones stand on either side; the vertex <note>the "vertex" is the sharp point or tip of the cone.</note> of one is at the visible point [of the object], and the vertex of the other is at the point of the picture on the paper.

### XLV. DEFINITION.

->For the purpose of instruction, let us call such a pair a "Pencil."<-

Now indeed, all the pencils <term>penicilli</term> <note>original: "penicillum"; literally "a little brush," used here to describe the bundle of light rays that taper to a point.</note> of all points meet in the lens as if in a common base of cones; having passed through the lens, they diverge again, and they take up opposite directions. In this illustration, there are three pencils, AB, CD, and EF, meeting in the convex lens GH, as if in a common base.

### XLVI. PROPOSITION.

->Just as the diameter of the picture relates to its distance from the lens,<-
->so too does the diameter of the visible object relate to its distance<-
->from the lens, approximately. For the axes of the pencils<-
->(the straight lines drawn from the visible point to the corresponding<-
->point on the image) almost all intersect one another...<-

<image-desc>
A vertical optical diagram illustrating the inversion of light rays passing through a convex lens. At the top, three source points are labeled: C (left), A (center), and E (right). From each point, a narrow cone of rays (described in the text as a "penicillum") descends toward a biconvex lens positioned horizontally and labeled G on the left and H on the right. 

The central bundle of rays from point A passes straight down through the center of the lens to point B at the bottom. The bundle from point C on the top left passes through the lens and crosses to point D on the bottom right. Conversely, the bundle from point E on the top right passes through the lens and crosses to point F on the bottom left. This demonstrates how the lens acts as a common base for the cones of light, resulting in an inverted arrangement (F-B-D) relative to the source (C-A-E).
</image-desc>

It also introduces the principle of proportionality between an object's size and its image size based on their distances from the lens.</summary>

...at one point, which is very near the center of the lens. Therefore, the vertically opposite angles <note>original: "κατά κορυφὴν" (kata koryphēn); these are the angles formed opposite each other when two lines cross.</note> are equal, according to Proposition 15 of Euclid's first book. They also have bases proportional to their legs on both sides, according to Proposition 4 of Euclid's sixth book.

### XLVII. PROBLEM.

How to quickly find the radius of curvature <term>radius of curvature</term> <note>original: "semidiametrum convexitatis"; literally "the half-diameter of the convexity." In modern optics, this describes how sharply the lens surface curves.</note> if the lens is convex on both sides with equal curvature.

*Place a piece of paper where distant objects are depicted most clearly of all. For according to Proposition 43, the paper will be at the point of convergence <note>The "point of convergence" or "focus" is where the light rays meet to form a sharp image.</note>. Therefore, according to Proposition 39, it will be distant from the lens by the radius of curvature.*

### XLVIII. PROBLEM.

How to find the same if the lens is convex on one side and flat on the other.

*Turn the flat side of the lens toward the distant visible object, and do so perpendicularly; so that the rays, entering at a right angle, are not refracted <note>original: "frangantur"; literally "broken." This refers to light bending as it moves from air into glass.</note> at all. Then place the paper where the object is depicted clearly. Therefore, according to Proposition 43, the paper will be at the point of convergence, and according to Proposition 35, it will be behind the lens at a distance of almost the entire diameter of curvature.*

### XLIX. PROBLEM.

To measure the diameter of curvature of a lens with equal convexity on both sides using a nearby visible object.

*Hold the lens exactly in the middle between the paper and the visible object, and do so perpendicularly and precisely; increase or decrease the distance of both from the lens by equal amounts until the image on the paper becomes very clear.*

*For because the object is depicted upon the paper, the paper is therefore at the point of convergence of the rays from a point of the visible object, according to Proposition 43. But because the visible object and the paper are equally distant from the lens, the paths of the rays inside the body of the lens will be parallel. For if they were not parallel, no part of any ray (except for the innermost one, drawn perpendicularly through the center <note>original: "umbilicum"; literally "the navel."</note> of the lens) would strike <note>original: "incurre-"; this is a catchword that completes on the next page as "incurreret" (would strike/run into).</note> both of the equal surfaces with an equal inclination—*

## L. PROBLEM.
**How to ignite objects with a lens that is equally convex on both sides.**
*Hold the lens perpendicular to the Sun and place the flammable material <note>original: "ustile"; an object or substance meant to be ignited.</note> at the point of convergence. This point will be at a distance of one radius of curvature <term>radius of curvature</term> <note>original: "semidiametro convexitatis"; literally the "half-diameter" of the curve of the lens surface.</note>, according to Proposition 39, because the rays from the center of the Sun are parallel, according to Proposition 23.*

## LI. PROBLEM.
**How to perform the same task with a lens that is flat on one side.**
*This occurs at a distance of almost the full diameter of curvature behind the lens, according to Proposition 35.*

## LII. PROBLEM.
**How to illuminate letters at night using a convex lens, by the light of a single bright star, so that they may be read.**
*Let the star shine perpendicularly into the lens. Place the paper with the letters to be read behind the lens. If the lens is equally convex on both sides, the distance should be one radius of curvature, according to Propositions 43 and 39. If it is flat on one side, the distance should be the diameter, according to Proposition 35. But if it has unequal curvatures, the distance will be more than the radius of the smaller curve, but less than the diameter, according to Proposition 40.*

## LIII. PROBLEM.
**How to project light a very long distance at night using a convex lens.**

### LIV. PROBLEM.

->To measure the distance of a visible object using a lens that is equally<-
->convex on both sides from a single position.<-

*For if the image of the object is projected <note>original: "pingitur"; literally "painted," referring to the focused image appearing on a surface.</note> onto the paper at a distance from the lens greater than the diameter of its curvature <term>diameter of convexity</term> <note>In early optics, the "diameter of convexity" refers to the diameter of the sphere from which the lens's curve was cut. This is twice the radius.</note>, the object will be closer to the lens than that diameter. Indeed, if the paper is at a distance of one diameter, the object will also be one diameter away, according to Proposition 35. Therefore, if the paper is closer than one diameter, the object will be further than one diameter, according to Proposition 41. Finally, if the paper holding the perfect image is at the distance of one radius <note>original: "semidiametro"; literally a "half-diameter."</note> of the known curvature, the distant object will be so far away that it can no longer be measured by the image, according to Proposition 39.*

### LV. PROBLEM.

->To achieve the same thing with a convex lens by another method:<-
->if the size of the visible object is known.<-

*This is done according to Proposition 46. For as the length <note>original: "longitudo"; the height or width of the image as measured on the paper.</note> of the image is to its distance from the lens, so the known size of the visible object is to its distance from the lens.*

### LVI. NOTE.

*I. Giambattista della Porta <note>An influential Italian polymath (1535–1615) whose work "Magia Naturalis" discussed many optical illusions and devices.</note> promises a Problem: to burn at an infinite distance by means of a burning line <term>burning line</term> <note>original: "lineam ustoriam"; Porta theorized that light could be focused into a continuous line of heat rather than a single point.</note>. He records this regarding mirrors, but others believe it to be true of a convex lens. Whichever you follow, you are attempting the impossible. It contradicts the science of Optics.*

*First, burning occurs because of the intersection of rays. An intersection is a point, not a line. Second: if it burns to infinity, it must therefore also burn at the very surface of the lens from which the light exits; for this reason, the lens would be destroyed. Third, if a ray acquires the power to burn, it acquires it from the gathering together of many rays*

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how light rays refract through surfaces, concluding a refutation of the idea that multiple rays can emerge from a single point at different angles. *...into one. But this is impossible. For a single ray falls upon a single point. And for a single point on any surface, there is also only one single refraction <term>refraction</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes from one medium, such as air, into another, such as glass.</note> of any ray passing through that point. Therefore, after that point, there is also only one ray, not many distinct ones of different inclinations, which are gathered into one by refraction. But more will be said on this matter below, where I shall associate concave lenses with convex ones.*

->Thus far regarding the convex lens and its uses without regard<-
->to the eye. Now concerning those uses which it has in assisting<-
->vision. And first, Concerning Vision Itself.<-

### LVII. PHYSICAL AXIOM.

The axes passing through the centers of the pupil and the humors <note>The transparent, fluid-like substances that fill the eyeball, such as the vitreous humor.</note> of the eyes are, by natural motion or rather by rest, parallel; but they are voluntarily twisted to gaze at nearby objects.

### LVIII. DEFINITION.

Distinct vision is that in which the finest parts of an object shine forth and come into view. Confused vision is that in which, while the larger parts appear, the smaller ones remain hidden and are, as it were, erased or smeared over, with their boundaries blurred together. Strong or clear vision is when an object is seen as if in much light; weak or obscure vision is when an object is seen as if in thin light, such as during an eclipse of the Sun or by the light of the Moon.

### LIX. PROPOSITION.

The surface of a dense medium <note>In this context, a transparent material like glass or water.</note> which, after refraction has occurred, causes parallel rays coming through the body to meet perfectly at a single point, is related to a hyperbola <term>hyperbola</term> <note>A specific type of mathematical curve. Kepler is noting that a simple spherical lens does not focus light perfectly; a "hyperbolic" shape would be required for a perfect focus.</note>.

*Let there be a part of a circle, A B C D E F G, with center H, and let the perpendicular line H D be sufficiently extended. And let there be parallel lines R A, P B, L C, K E, M F, and Q G.*

<summary>Kepler transitions from the physics of lenses to the mechanics of the human eye, defining the differences between clear and blurred vision and introducing the mathematical shape required for a perfect focus.</summary>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical and physical exploration of how light rays converge through a lens. Following the previous discussion on the nature of vision and the properties of convex surfaces, the author now demonstrates why a perfectly spherical lens fails to focus light onto a single point—a phenomenon we now call spherical aberration—and proposes that a hyperbolic shape is required for the crystalline humor of the eye.</meta>

*But if all refractions <term>refractions</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes from one medium, such as air, into another, such as glass or the eye.</note> were proportional to the angles of incidence <term>incidentiæ</term> <note>The angle at which a ray of light strikes a surface.</note>, then all parallel rays, after being refracted, would meet at the same point—say, at I—according to Proposition 35. But because they are not proportional, according to Proposition 12, but rather increase beyond the expected measure at large inclinations <note>Steep angles where the light hits the outer edges of the curved surface.</note>, the rays LC and KE indeed meet at I, but the closer rays PB and MF meet higher up at N, and the outermost rays RA and QG meet even higher still at O.*

*Therefore, for the points O, N, and I to unite at N, it is necessary for smaller refractions to occur at the edges A and G, and larger ones at the inner points C and E. The refraction will be smaller at A and G if the inclination of rays RA and QG to the surface is smaller there; and it will be larger at C and E if the inclination of rays LC and KE is larger.*

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating the refraction of light rays. Seven parallel vertical rays (labeled R, P, L, H, K, M, Q from left to right) strike a curved convex surface at points A, B, C, D, E, F, G. The central ray H-D passes straight through to point I. Rays L-C and K-E converge at I. Rays P-B and M-F converge at point N on the central axis, which is higher than I. The outermost rays R-A and Q-G converge at point O, the highest point on the axis. The diagram demonstrates spherical aberration or the need for a non-spherical surface to achieve a single focal point.</image-desc>

*Now, a smaller inclination of RA to AB occurs if the endpoint B approaches the ray R—that is, if there is some surface that intersects the circular surface ABC at point A, moving higher than the path of ABC. If this same surface intersects BCD again at E, the inclination of the ray LC upon it will be larger. The same applies at points E and G. Thus, the new line <note>This "new line" refers to a non-spherical curve that would correct the focal points to a single spot.</note> intersects the old circular one at four points. A hyperbola <term>hyperbola</term> <note>A type of open geometric curve.</note> does exactly this. An ellipsis <term>ellipsis</term> <note>An oval shape.</note> does not; for an ellipsis does not intersect a portion smaller than a semicircle except at two points. As for the parabola <term>parabola</term> <note>A U-shaped curve often seen in the path of a projectile.</note>, though it behaves similarly, it is nonetheless not like the sought-after surface for this reason: none of them accommodates itself to a specific angle. But the surface we seek must accommodate itself to a specific angle of 96°, because the maximum refraction is 48°, the double of which is 96°, according to Proposition 9.*

### LX. PROPOSITION.

*The crystalline humor <note>The natural lens inside the human eye.</note> of the eye is a convex lens in the shape of a hyperbola; and the retina <term>retiformis tunica</term> <note>original: "retiformis tunica" — literally, the "net-like cloak," referring to the retina's appearance.</note>, full of spirits <note>In 17th-century science, "spirits" were thought to be subtle fluids that carried sensory information through the nerves.</note>, located behind the crystalline humor, is...*

<meta>catchword: est pa- (is par-)</meta>

<summary>This page explains spherical aberration, showing that a circular lens cannot focus light perfectly. Kepler argues that the human eye's lens (the crystalline humor) must be hyperbolic in shape to ensure all light rays meet at a single point for clear vision.</summary>
<keywords>refraction, spherical aberration, hyperbola, crystalline humor, retina, geometry of the eye, lens shape, focal point</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the eye focuses light. The previous page demonstrated that a hyperbolic shape—rather than a perfect sphere—is necessary to bring all light rays to a single, sharp focal point. Here, the author applies these geometric principles to the physical anatomy of the eye, specifically the lens and the retina.</meta>

...serves the role of paper, and visible things are painted upon it in a real picture. That the **crystalline humor** <term>crystalline humor</term> <note>The lens of the eye, which early scientists called a "humor" because they believed it was a semi-fluid substance.</note> is a most transparent convex lens is established by the experience of anatomists. The same [anatomists] testify that the shape of its posterior part is **hyperbolic**; and that the **retina** <term>retiform tunic</term> <note>Literally the "net-like" layer; we now call this the retina. The author compares it to paper because it receives the "image" projected by the lens.</note> is spread out everywhere into a circle or hollow sphere around the crystalline humor at a fixed distance from it; and furthermore, that it is a reddish-white, like paper.

*These points being established, through Proposition 43 it follows that a picture of visible things exists on the retina; and through Proposition 59, because it is of a shape related to a hyperbola, it is consistent that this occurs to provide the **pencils of light** <term>penicillis</term> <note>A "pencil" of light refers to a bundle of rays originating from a single point and converging to another.</note> with a perfect and pure sharpness, and that through them the most distinct picture is formed.*

### PROPOSITION LXI.

Vision is the sensing of the affected retina, which is full of **visual spirit**; or, To see, is to feel the affected retina, insofar as it is affected.

*The retinal tunic is painted by the colored rays of visible things. This picture or illumination is a kind of **passion** <term>passio</term> <note>In the philosophy of the time, a "passion" was not an emotion, but a state of being acted upon or "impressed" by an external force—in this case, light acting upon the eye.</note>, not merely superficial, like when chalk is rubbed against a wall or light falls upon it, but also qualitative, penetrating into the **spirits** <term>spiritus</term> <note>The "visual spirits" were thought to be a subtle, fluid-like substance within the nerves that carried sensory information to the brain.</note>. I prove this first from the nature of light, which, if strong and condensed, burns (according to Proposition 50). But if there were the same proportion of the most subtle light falling upon the retina to the most subtle thinness of the spirits within the retina, as there is outside in the air of the densest burning light to the thick corpulence of those things which are burned: then an action of penetrating light and a "passion" of the retina and its spirits would follow within the retina no less than burning (the action) of light and destruction (the passion) of the matter being burned follows outside. I prove this secondly from experience. Eyes fixed upon a strong light are affected to such a degree that even when removed from the brightness they saw, they nevertheless retain and carry its image with them, sometimes for quite a while. Therefore, that retinal picture is a penetrating passion. But this picture does not yet complete the whole of vision, unless the **appearance** <term>species</term> <note>The "species" was the formal image or informational content of the object being perceived.</note> of the retina thus affected passes through the continuation of the spirits into the brain, and is there presented at the threshold of the soul's faculties: which happens as follows.*

<meta>Catchword: Which- (original: Quem-)</meta>

<summary>This page defines vision as a physical and sensory process where the retina acts like paper to receive a focused "picture" of the world. The author argues that light doesn't just touch the surface of the eye but physically alters the "visual spirits," which then carry the image to the brain.</summary>
<keywords>retina, crystalline humor, visual spirits, hyperbola, optics, physiology of vision, after-images, species</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the physical optics of the eye (the lens and retina) to the neurological and psychological process of perception. The author is moving from how light forms a picture to how that picture is "felt" by the brain.</meta>

Just as every external sense is perfected through reception and impression—that is, through a **passion** <term>passio</term> <note>In the philosophy of this period, a "passion" refers to the state of being acted upon by an external force, rather than an emotion.</note> where the "form" or image of an external thing is impressed upon the organ—this passion is called sensation. In the same way, deep within the brain, there is something (whatever it may actually be) called the **common sense** <term>sensus communis</term> <note>The "common sense" was believed to be the internal faculty that gathered data from all five external senses to create a unified perception.</note>. It is upon this faculty that the image from the "visual instrument" (the eye) is impressed, having been "painted" there by the light of the visible object. Therefore, the events that occur in the eye—which sits outside the seat of the common sense—are impressed upon that internal sense by an immaterial form that descends from the affected or "painted" organ and is carried to the thresholds of the brain.

But the nature of this impression is a hidden mystery. It cannot be safely claimed that this image is carried inward through the physical channels of the **optic nerves** <term>nervorum opticorum</term> which cross one another. For there is another, more obvious use for these nerves: they supply **visual spirit** <term>spiritum visivum</term> <note>Early scientists believed a weightless, fluid-like "spirit" flowed through the nerves to enable sight and movement.</note> from both sides of the brain to each eye. These nerves are crossed <note>The optic chiasm, where the optic nerves from each eye intersect.</note> so that if one side of the brain were injured, or if the nerve exiting it were blocked, the other eye would not be immediately deprived of this vital spirit.

Since the optic nerves have this clear purpose, it is unclear whether they also serve the additional role of carrying the image from the eye back into the brain. Or, perhaps, there are other spirits—finer than the physical ones spread through the **retina** <term>retiformem</term>—which do not require a physical passage but travel freely through the whole body. These finer spirits might pick up the "affections" (the changes or sensations) of the limbs and communicate them to that faculty of the brain called the common sense. It is possible that the image is transferred from the retina to the brain through the passage of the optic nerve, not because the nerve is a hollow pipe, but because it is filled with spirit from the seat of the common sense all the way to the eye. In this way, the continuity of the spirit allows the sensation to pass from the eye to the brain, much like how a ripple in a still pond, caused by a thrown pebble, propagates all the way to the shore as long as the surface of the water remains continuous.

One might say that just as the **Sun** <term>Sol</term> illuminates all things with straight, transparent lines of light, the faculty of the **Soul** <term>Animæ</term> within the brain illuminates the sensory organs with "spiritual lines." These lines can follow any curve, provided they are continuous. For then, just as transparent air does us no good if an opaque object blocks the Sun...

<summary>This page explores how visual images captured by the retina are transmitted to the "common sense" in the brain, theorizing that "visual spirits" acting like ripples in water carry the sensation through the optic nerves.</summary>
<keywords>common sense, sensation, optic nerves, visual spirit, retina, perception, soul, optics, physiology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transmission of visual information from the eyes to the brain. It specifically focuses on the role of "visual spirits" and the problem of why we perceive a single image despite having two eyes—a foundational concept in the study of binocular vision.</meta>

...and ourselves: thus likewise the **spirit** <note>original: *spiritus*</note> that stands by the **retina** <term>tunica retiformis</term> <note>Literally the "net-like" tunic, the light-sensitive layer at the back of the eye.</note> will be of no use if, for whatever reason, that **spirited** conduit is intercepted higher up and further inside the head, and ceases to be continuous. From this comes that sudden extinction of light in diseases—not through a retreat of the **spirits**, but through their breaking off and interception by a constricted, obstructed, or severed passage.

These things concern the other "passion" <term>passio</term> <note>In this context, a "passion" is the state of the sensory organ being acted upon or "impressed" by an external object.</note>, which is the **common sense** <term>sensus communis</term> <note>The internal faculty thought to unify all sensory data into a single perception.</note>, and is caused by the **image** <note>original: *specie*</note> of the affected instrument: which is its object.

## PROPOSITION LXII.

When both instruments <note>the eyes</note> are affected similarly, we seem to see one image; but when the retinas of the two eyes are affected or painted differently within, two visible things are represented to us instead of one.

> *For there is no sensation of the instrument in the common sense insofar as it is a bare instrument. Or if there is, it is permanent and unsuitable for producing any new sensation. Rather, it is the sensation of the instrument insofar as it is affected, according to Proposition LXI.*

If, therefore, they are similarly affected, there will also be a similar impression or passion from both affected parts in the common sense, which is one and the same. For the "trace," so to speak, which the right eye impresses upon the common sense through its own state, the left eye also impresses through its own, as far as producing a new sensation in the brain is concerned. The second part of the Proposition follows from Proposition LXI. For if vision is the sensation of the affected instrument as it is affected, and there are two instruments, each affected uniquely, then two impressions will be made upon the common sense, and thus there will be two sensations of the same thing.

*Therefore, the **crossing** <term>decussatio</term> <note>The optic chiasm, where the nerves from each eye cross.</note> of the optic nerves within the brain does not serve to recognize the unity of an object seen with two eyes. This is also contradicted by the fact that these nerves are always crossed; yet we do not always seem to see one thing, even if we see one thing with each eye.*

## PROPOSITION LXIII.

<vocab>spirit, common sense, retina (net-like tunic), crossing of the nerves, vision, optic nerve</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that single vision occurs when both eyes receive identical "impressions" on the common sense, rather than being a result of the physical crossing of the optic nerves. It also describes blindness in certain diseases as a physical interruption of the "visual spirit" flowing to the brain.</summary>
<keywords>binocular vision, optic chiasm, visual spirit, common sense, retina, sensory perception, optic nerve</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of how images are formed within the eye. Having previously established that the retina is the surface where the "painting" of vision occurs, the author now addresses the mechanics of focus and the physical limitations of the eye's anatomy in viewing objects at different distances.</meta>

It is impossible for the retina <term>retiformis</term> <note>Literally "net-like"; the light-sensitive inner layer of the eye.</note>, while remaining in the same position within the eye, to have a clear image painted upon it by both near and distant objects at the same time.

*For according to Proposition 41, the light rays from a distant point converge closer behind the lens than those from a near point. Now, according to Proposition 43, a precise image is formed exactly at this point of convergence; therefore, at any point other than where these rays meet, the image becomes blurred. Consequently, according to Proposition 60, vision also becomes indistinct. Thus, in a position where near objects are precisely painted, the rays from a distant point have not yet met; therefore, distant objects are painted unclearly there, and vice versa. It follows that if we see distant things clearly with the retina in a certain position relative to the crystalline lens, we will necessarily see near things unclearly while the retina remains in that same position.*

## PROPOSITION LXIV.

There are some who see distant things clearly and near things unclearly, whom Aristotle calls "presbyopes" <note>original: πρεσβύτας (*presbytas*); literally "elders," referring to the farsightedness that often comes with age.</note>; there are others who see near things clearly and distant things unclearly, whom Aristotle calls "myopes" <note>original: μύωπες (*myopes*); shortsighted individuals.</note>; there are some who see both near and distant things unclearly; and finally, there are those who see both clearly.

*This proposition is physiological and pertains to the field of medicine. Those who see both near and far objects unclearly at the same time suffer from a disease of the eye, being either dim-sighted or entirely blind. This "condition" <note>original: πάθος (*pathos*)</note> results from a defect in the eye's physical structure.*

*Those who see both near and far clearly at the same time possess an eye that is both healthy and mobile in its shape. Since, according to Proposition 63, the retina cannot be painted equally well by both near and far objects if it stays in the same position, yet in these people it is painted equally well (following Propositions 60 and 61), it must be that in their eyes either the retina moves relative to the crystalline lens <term>humor crystallinus</term> <note>The lens of the eye, which early scientists believed was the primary organ of sight.</note>, or the crystalline lens moves relative to the retina. It is highly probable that a healthy, vigorous, and youthful eye possesses this ability. We can already see a natural, visible movement in the front of the eye at the pupil—which constricts in bright light and dilates in dim light. It is likely that the retina behind the lens has a similar power: to dilate its cavity so that the back of the eye is drawn toward the lens when distant things must be seen, and conversely to constrict its cavity so that the back moves further away when near things are being inspected. Or, perhaps this natural movement belongs instead to the "spider-web" membrane, or the arach-* <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the word "arachnoid" on the next page.</note>

<meta>The catchword "noidi" at the bottom of the page indicates the completion of the word "arachnoidi," referring to the arachnoid membrane of the eye.</meta>

<summary>This page explains the optical necessity of the eye's internal components moving to focus on objects at varying distances. It identifies the conditions of nearsightedness and farsightedness and posits that a healthy eye must physically adjust its internal structure to maintain clear vision.</summary>
<keywords>optics, retina, focus, crystalline lens, presbyopia, myopia, eye anatomy, vision, Aristotle</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the physiological explanation of how the eye adjusts its internal structure to focus on objects at varying distances, following the definitions of far-sightedness and near-sightedness.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<sig>D 2</sig>

*...of the arachnoid membrane* <term>arachnoid tunic</term> <note>original: *noidi tunicæ*; the author is referring to the delicate, web-like membrane surrounding the lens.</note>*, which has the lens of the crystalline humor fixed in its center and connects it to the uvea* <note>The vascular middle layer of the eye.</note> *by black rays sent out all around. For these black rays, called ciliary processes* <term>processus ciliares</term>*, seem to be separated like the teeth of a comb, so that each one acts as a kind of distinct muscle. When all of these pull back into themselves together and are thus made short, this "diaphragm" of the eye is made narrower; as the sides of the eye are contracted, it makes the shape of the eye somewhat oblong or an ellipsoid* <note>original: *Ellipoiden*</note>*, where the back or the cavity of the retina* <term>retiformis</term> <note>Literally "net-like" membrane.</note> *recedes from the crystalline humor. However, when the ciliary processes in the web-like tissue are thinned out and thus stretched out in length, the circle drawn through the sides of the eye is enlarged, and the eye becomes more lens-like in shape, with the back of the retina approaching the crystalline lens. This occurs through the service of the same uvea, which also narrows and loosens the pupil. For this purpose, the humors of the eye (except for the crystalline lens) are fluid and can be compressed.*

*Those who see only one of the two distances distinctly have an eye that is indeed healthy, but is already becoming hardened, habituated, and as if aged. For it is a false belief that only the elderly fail to see near things distinctly, or that only the young fail to see distant things. These conditions happen indiscriminately to both, according to the habits of their bodies or the activities of their youth. For one who from childhood is devoted to hunting, fowling* <note>The hunting or trapping of birds.</note>*, sailing, or traveling, habituates the eye to distant things; but because it is also necessary to eat and to speak with people face-to-face, the eye remains practiced in looking at near things as well. Over time, however, this practice is weakened; thus it happens that almost all who suffer from no vision defect in youth see only distant things distinctly in old age. For it is more natural to keep the eyes parallel than to twist them toward near things, according to Proposition 57. In old age, the eye becomes fatigued, so that, having retained its natural direction, it lets go of those things seen only with effort. And this defect usually comes to them late, in deep old age.*

*Conversely, those who from childhood lead a sedentary life indoors, focusing on books and delicate manual crafts, quickly become accustomed to near things; nor are they ever drawn away from this habit as age progresses, but rather they become more and more blind to distant things.*

<meta>catchword: Sunt</meta>

<summary>This page explains the mechanical action of the ciliary processes in changing the eye's shape to focus and discusses how lifestyle—such as outdoor hunting versus indoor scholarly work—determines whether a person becomes near-sighted or far-sighted.</summary>
<keywords>ciliary processes, crystalline lens, retina, myopia, presbyopia, eye anatomy, habituation of sight, aging and vision</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the two types of vision—long-sightedness and near-sightedness—by correlating them with physical stature and personality traits. It then transitions from the physiology of the eye to the geometry of optics and the use of lenses.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

There are also people of the first type <note>Referring to the long-sighted individuals discussed on the previous page.</note> who are more prone to drunkenness, sleepiness, idleness, and pondering—that is, those who frequently neglect care for things right before their feet and under their hands. Their eyes are therefore directed most often in a parallel orientation <note>original: *situm parallēlun*</note>, in which position only remote things are seen distinctly.

But people of the second type <note>The near-sighted.</note> are instead sober, watchful, industrious, and intent on present things.

Thus, the former are almost always of tall stature, because they have an eye more remote from its base and look further ahead; the latter are instead short <note>original: *pumili*; literally "pigmies" or dwarfs, used here for shorter people.</note>. However, this is not always the case. For it has been said that the natural habit of the body also claims something for itself here.

## LXV. PROPOSITION.

When the rays of a single radiant point converge by whatever means toward the eye, it is impossible for distinct vision to occur. For every eye is designed to see either distant or near things distinctly. Distant things radiate as if in parallel <note>original: *παραλλήλως* (parallēlōs)</note> according to Proposition 23. Near things send diverging rays into the eye according to Proposition 24. Therefore, no distinctly visible point radiates in such a way that its rays converge at the point where they touch the eye.

->So far concerning the eye and vision: what follows concerns the uses of the lens with respect to the eye.<-

## LXVI. OPTICAL AXIOM.

Objects of a known distance and unknown magnitude, when unexpectedly perceived under a large angle of vision, appear large; under a small angle, they appear small.
Proven in the *Optics* <note>Kepler refers here to his own 1604 work, *Ad Vitellionem paralipomena*.</note> from Proposition 19.

## LXVII. OPTICAL AXIOM.

The intervals between the eye and a small object are in inverse proportion <note>original: *in eversâ proportione*</note> to the visual angles: that is, the further any object recedes, the smaller the angle by which it is seen.

<meta>catchword: Things.</meta>

# LXVIII.

An object of a known size but unknown distance—such as the face of an adult man—when perceived unexpectedly by a single eye under a large visual angle, appears to be nearby; if seen under a small angle, it appears remote, according to Proposition 67.

*This is the converse proof of the previous one. However, the vision must be performed with a single eye; because the duality and distance between the two eyes (and no less the movement of the head, which acts as a substitute <note>original: "vicem supplens"; the author is describing how moving the head creates "parallax," allowing one eye to see from different positions</note> for several eyes separated from one another) makes the distance of an object known from being unknown, provided that distance is proportional to the separation of the eyes.*

# LXIX.

Since, therefore, all distant things are thought to be at the same distance—because that distance is unknown, yet for that very reason (being so far off) it is conceived as if it were known (for instance, we conceive of the sky as a single surface <note>The author refers to the "celestial sphere," the ancient and intuitive idea that stars are all fixed to a dome at an equal distance from Earth.</note> in which all the stars are located, regardless of the difference in their actual distance)—it follows that remote things of unknown size seen under a larger angle are thought to be larger, and under a smaller angle, smaller, in absolute terms. This follows from Proposition 66.

*As if, by some means, the angle by which the Moon is seen were enlarged; we would then think the Moon itself had been made larger. This is because we conceive nothing else regarding the distance of the Moon than this: that it remains in the same sky, no matter what angle it is seen under.*

# LXX. PROPOSITION.

Through convex lenses, if the eye is placed within the vicinity <note>That is, placed closer to the lens than the point where the light rays meet.</note> of the point of convergence of the rays flowing from a single visible point, the visible object is represented in its own orientation—for example, upright if the object itself is upright—and so forth.

*Let the lens be A B. Let the visible object be C E, no longer a single point, but a quantity <note>A physical object with height or width.</note>. Let the extreme points of the visible object be C and E. Let the radiation from point C be C B F, C H F, C A F, etc., and let the point of convergence be F. Likewise, let the radiation from point E be E B D...*

Now let the eye be located between the convergence points D and F and the lens AB, in some intermediate place, such as at IG, with the size of the pupil opening being IG. Therefore, an eye positioned this way does not admit the entire <term>pencil of rays</term><note>A "pencil" (Latin: *penicillum*) refers to a bundle of light rays originating from a single point and passing through a lens.</note> EADBE from point E, but only the part EKIDGBE, which joins at the part of the lens KB. Similarly, the opening IG does not admit the entire pencil CA FBC from point C, but only the part CAIHGHC, which joins at the part of the lens AH. Therefore, any of the rays between KI and BG shows point E—the right side showing the right-side point. And any of the rays between AI and HG shows point C—the left side showing the left-side point. Wherefore, in the same orientation that the parts of the light pencils AHGI and KBGI fall upon the eye GI, in that same orientation are the vertices of the pencils, or the visible points C and E, truly located.

### LXXI. PROPOSITION.

Every upright representation of distant upright visible objects through convex lenses is necessarily blurred; and the more blurred, the farther the convex lens is from the eye.

*For according to the previous propositions from 34 to 40, the rays from each point of a distant visible thing (represented by point C in the previous diagram), such as rays CA, CH, and the rest, are parallel (according to Proposition 21) until they reach the convex lens. Then, after refraction takes place in the convex lens, they now converge toward the eye IG. But according to Proposition 65, when rays from a single point converge toward the eye, distinct vision is impossible. And since convergence is the cause of blurring <note>original: "confusio"; meaning a lack of focus or a "confused" image where the light does not land on a single point of the retina.</note>, greater convergence will be the cause of greater blurring. Now, the convergence is greater in*

*...the greater part of the pencil of rays is intercepted by the eye, specifically when the eye is farther from the lens. Therefore, the confusion of the upright vision will be greater if the lens is farther from the eye.*

### LXXII. PROPOSITION.

For the elderly, some upright representations of nearby visible objects through convex lenses appear distinct.

*According to Aristotle, those who see distant things distinctly but are weak-sighted regarding nearby things are called "the elderly" <note>original: τοῖς πρεσβύταις (toīs presbytais); these are what we now call presbyopes, whose eyes lose the ability to focus on close objects</note>, as discussed in Proposition 64. Therefore, such a person, according to Proposition 23, has eyes accustomed to receiving rays from any single point as parallel lines. Now, according to Propositions 35 and 39, there is a specific point beyond the lens or eyeglass where, if a point of a visible object is placed, the rays from that point will travel toward the eye as parallel rays after passing through the lens. Therefore, the visible object is represented distinctly to these people through the convex lens.*

*And note: mathematical demonstration defines the limits of things with precision. Nature, however, wanders to and fro on either side of those limits without great discomfort to vision, unless it wanders too far.*

### LXXIII. PROPOSITION.

An eye placed at the point where parallel rays meet still sees nearby objects as upright.

*For an eye placed at the point of convergence <term>point of convergence</term><note>Often referred to today as the focal point; the spot where light rays meet after passing through a lens.</note> of parallel rays (that is, rays coming from a remote and distant point according to Proposition 23) is still within the boundaries of the convergence of rays from a nearby visible point, according to Proposition 41. Therefore, according to Proposition 70, the visible object will still be represented as upright.*

### LXXIV. PROPOSITION.

When the eye is positioned exactly at the point where the rays flowing from a point on an object meet, it does not see that radiating point distinctly through the lens, but in the most confused manner possible.

For the *rays* of a single point, once refraction has *occurred* in a lens, converge toward a point of meeting <note>original: punctum concursus; what we would today call the focal point</note>. If, therefore, the eye is positioned at that point of meeting, the rays converge toward the eye. But according to Proposition 65, when rays converge in this way, their source and origin are not seen distinctly. And since the convergence of all those rays that can pass through a single lens is greatest at that point, the confusion of the image will be the greatest of all at that location.

## LXXV. PROPOSITION.

**An eye positioned beyond the point where the rays of a single visible point meet sees the points of that visible object through a convex lens in an inverted position.**

I do not say that at *any* distance whatsoever from the point where the rays meet, one sees the entire visible object inverted <note>original: everso situ; literally "in an overturned position," meaning upside down or flipped left-to-right</note>. For to see a large part of the visible object, a great distance is required. But in general, I say that the inversion of that visible object follows the crossing of the point where the rays meet.

For instance, in the diagram for Proposition 70, let the eye be positioned not at I G—within the meeting points D or F—but at O P, removed beyond these points by such a distance that the entire visible object C E can be seen. That is to say, let the rays be extended: the ray E A D L P from the right-hand point E through the leftmost part of the lens, and the ray B F from the left-hand point C through the rightmost part of the lens, extended to the point where they meet (let this be point L) and further; let the pupil of the eye O P be beyond this meeting point.

Therefore, the right-hand point E, via ray E A D L P and its neighboring rays (which move toward H at points near the left part of the lens A, and with refraction having occurred...

->PROPOSITION LXXVI.<-

**The point of inversion—or that point in which the two lines flowing together from two points of the visible object into the center of the eye intersect—that point, I say, is between the visible object and the lens.**

For it will be proven, as previously in Proposition LXXV, that the right parts of the lens correspond to the left parts of the visible object, and vice versa. Therefore, no intersection of the <term>visual cones<note>The bundle of light rays that spreads out from a single point on an object and is captured by the lens or pupil.</note></term> occurs between the eye and the lens, but rather between the lens and the visible object. Moreover, what is true of the cones as a whole must also be true of the center lines of the cones, which fall upon the center of the <term>pupil<note>referred to here as the "pupilla," the aperture of the eye.</note></term>; and likewise of those rays that fall upon the edges of the pupil. As in the diagram for Proposition LXXV, at point S the lines *E A D L P* and *C B F L O* intersect, having fallen upon the edges of the pupil *P O*. The intersection at *L*, however, is part of the meeting of the cones *O D P* and *O F P* at *O P*, which is not considered here, because above in Proposition LXX, it did not invert the position of the object. Those were then the cones *I A C H G* and *I K E B G.*

...sees points that diverge significantly: In the inversion of the <term>visible object<note>The "visibile," or the object being viewed through the optical system.</note></term>, all points of the object after the intersection at D and F have rays that diverge again toward the eye O P, according to Proposition 21. ## PROPOSITION LXXVIII.

**A nearsighted eye <note>original Greek: μύωψ (myops)</note> sees any object, whether near or far, clearly once it has been inverted by a convex lens, provided the eye is at a certain distance from the intersection of the rays coming from a single point of that visible object.**

According to Aristotle, "myopes" <note>original Greek: μύωπες</note> are those who see near objects clearly but are dim-sighted regarding distant ones. See page 64.

Their eyes, therefore, are accustomed to rays that diverge significantly from a single point. But according to Proposition 75, inversion occurs beyond the point of intersection. According to Proposition 21, the rays of a single glowing point C, which were diverging toward the lens KB and, having passed through the lens, were converging toward the point of intersection F, now—having also passed through that point—diverge again toward the eye O P. They thus become suitable for this eye to see that point C clearly.

I say, however, that this occurs at one specific location relative to the <term>intersections<note>The "concursum," the point where the light rays meet before spreading out again.</note></term> D and F of the radiations of the visible object DE being observed. For the capabilities of different eyes are distinguished according to greater or lesser <term>divergences<note>The spreading out of light rays as they move away from a point.</note></term>, as per Proposition 64. At a greater distance of the pupil O P from the intersections D and F, there is less divergence, because the angle ODP or OFP will be smaller if the base O P remains the same while the sides OD and PD become longer. Therefore, a specific distance from the D and F intersections serves each individual eye.

## PROPOSITION LXXIX.

**A single convex surface with a small curvature is equivalent to two convex lens surfaces in gathering rays to a point.**

<summary>This page explains the optical mechanics of nearsightedness and farsightedness, demonstrating how the distance between the eye and the point where rays intersect (after passing through a lens) determines the clarity of the image.</summary>

...surfaces taken from a circle twice as large.

*Let the lens AB be equally convex on both sides, formed by circles ADB and ACB, whose centers are F and E. Therefore, by Proposition 39, the <term>convergence point<note>The point where light rays meet after passing through a lens; the focal point.</note></term> is F. Let half of DF or CE be taken, which shall be GL. With G as the center and GL as the radius, let a circle HLI be drawn, which alone shall cause the refraction of parallel rays coming from the direction of center G. Let GL be continued to K, and LK be double GL, and therefore equal to DF. Therefore, by Proposition 35, parallel rays refracted in HLI will meet at K. Thus, a single convexity HLI of a small circle performs the same as the two convexities of AB from a circle twice as large; because the convergence point is equally distant from the <term>dense body<note>The glass or material of the lens.</note></term> on both sides, specifically DF and LK are equal.*

## PROPOSITION LXXX. <note>original: XXC. PROPOSITIO. In this numbering system, XXC represents 80 (20 before 100).</note>

**Every upright image of a visible object seen through a convex lens is necessarily larger than it actually is.**

*For by the converse of Proposition 70, if the image is upright, the eye is within the proximity of the <term>convergence point<note>The point where light rays intersect.</note></term> of rays flowing from a single point of the visible object. And no intersection of the <term>cones<note>Light spreads out from a point in a cone shape; Kepler is describing the bundle of rays entering the eye.</note></term> from the visible points into the <term>pupil<note>The opening of the eye that lets in light.</note></term> (or of the lines entering from those points into the center of the eye) occurs between the object and the eye, according to Proposition 76. Let the lens be AB, the eye C, and the visible object DE. Since several points of the visible object are presented, of the individual lines descending from each of them into the center of the eye—or vice versa—either only one will be perpendicular to the lens, or none at all. Therefore, either all rays will be <term>refracted<note>Bent as they pass from the air into the glass of the lens.</note></term> in the lens, or all except one, according to Proposition 10.*

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof that a single highly-curved lens surface can focus light identically to a lens with two shallower curves. It also introduces Proposition 80, explaining that a convex lens creates a magnified, upright image when the eye is positioned between the lens and its focal point.</summary>
