--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric explanation of Proposition 80, which demonstrates why a convex lens magnifies objects. The previous page established that a biconvex lens can be treated as a single equivalent convex surface for the purpose of the proof. This page concludes that proof and begins Proposition 81 regarding the field of view.</meta>

Now, according to Proposition 79, the two convexities of a lens perform the same role in refraction as a single convexity that contains both within itself. 

Therefore, let the doubling of the convexity not confuse us here; let there be a single convex surface equivalent to both, marked **AHB**. When points **D** and **E** are connected to the eye at **C** by straight lines that cut the <term>dense medium<note>The glass of the lens.</note></term> at **I** and **K**, it is clear from what has been stated that these will not be the actual visual paths for points **D** and **E**. Indeed, those lines remain straight [in our construction], while the laws of optics require that the ray **CI**, upon hitting the surface at point **I**, should deflect away from the straight path **ID** and move toward the line perpendicular to the surface at that point. In doing so, it falls inward, between **D** and **E**. Similarly, once refraction occurs, the ray **CK** will not continue straight toward **E**, but will fall inward from the path **KE** toward **D**. 

And thus the lines **CI** and **CK**, and the angle **ICK**—through which the visible object **DE** could have been seen without the lens—no longer grasp the visible object **DE** once the lens is placed in between. Instead, they grasp something smaller, which will be judged to have the magnitude of the whole of **DE**.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating optical refraction. At the bottom is a point C representing an eye. Above it is a lens section marked A, H, B. Rays CA and CB pass through the edges of the lens, while CI and CK pass through points closer to the center. At the top, a horizontal line contains points F, D, E, G, N. Refracted paths lead to D and E, while visual projections extend straight to F and G.</image-desc>

Therefore, in order for the whole of **DE** to be grasped, rays must come from the eye that are further out than **CI** and **CK**, such as **CA** and **CB**. If these are separated by the correct distance, then once refraction occurs at **A** and **B**, they will grasp points **D** and **E**, so that the visual paths are **CAD** and **CBE**. Since, however, the angle **ACB** is larger than the angle **ICK** (through which the object is seen when the lens is removed), the visible object **DE** will therefore be judged larger than it truly is, according to Proposition 68. For according to Proposition 19, the eye does not know what happens to the rays **CA** and **CB** as they pass through points **A** and **B**; it assumes they continue in a straight line, as if they were **CAF** and **CBG**, where the imagined size **FG** is larger than the actual size **DE**.

## PROPOSITION LXXXI. <note>original: XXCI. Using a Roman numeral system where XXC is 80 and XXCI is 81.</note>

The further the eye is from a convex lens toward the <term>point of convergence<note>The focal point where parallel rays meet after passing through the lens.</note></term>, the narrower the portion of the <term>hemisphere<note>The visible field of view or the sky.</note></term> it sees through the lens, and the smaller it judges that part to be.

<meta>catchword: Since (Latin: Cum)</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the proof that convex lenses magnify objects by increasing the visual angle and begins a new proposition regarding how the field of view narrows as the eye moves toward the focal point.</summary>
<keywords>refraction, convex lens, magnification, visual angle, focal point, field of view, optics</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric demonstration of how lenses alter the perception of space. The previous page concluded the proof of Proposition 80, explaining why a lens makes objects appear to have a different magnitude due to the refraction of light rays entering the eye.</meta>

For since both the lens and the things perceived through it on either side are seen at the same angle—and indeed at a smaller angle when the lens is far away than when it is near—it follows from Proposition 67 that the portion seen when the lens is distant is perceived as smaller. Furthermore, a truly smaller part of the object is perceived through a lens that is further away. For let us consider the lens **AB** in the previous diagram, which is further from eye **C** than from eye **O**. Since the lines **OA** and **OB** are more interior than **CA** and **CB**, their refracted rays—once the intersection is made at **A** and **B**—will be more exterior, according to Proposition 11. Let the refracted ray of **OA** be the exterior ray **AM**, and the refracted ray of **OB** be the exterior ray **BN**. It is therefore evident that from the refracted rays **AM** and **BN** coming from the near eye **O**, a larger portion of the <term>hemisphere<note>The "hemisphere of the world" or the total field of vision available from a single point.</note></term> is cut off; however, from the refracted rays **AD** and **BE** coming from the more distant eye **C**, a smaller portion of the hemisphere is cut off. This will be much more evident if, while the inclinations of the refracted rays remain the same, the eyes **O** and **C** are brought together into one, and the lens takes on different positions.

## XXII. PROPOSITION.

-> An eye observing a distant visible object near <-
-> a lens, when it has moved further away toward the point of convergence, <-
-> will see that same object larger than when it was close. <-

*This appears to contradict the previous proposition, and is therefore placed here for the sake of clarification. For take note: all things seen through a distant lens are perceived collectively at a smaller angle, according to Proposition 81. Yet individual things seen separately through the lens, whether it be near or far, are seen at a larger angle when the lens is distant. For the angle by which the entire lens is viewed, and the angle by which some object is viewed through a small part of the lens, behave in opposite ways. While the lens is moved further away, the former angle is diminished, while the latter is increased; and with it, the portion of the lens through which that object is viewed increases. This happens first so that it may grasp the same visible object, and then so that it may represent that same object as larger—to such an extent that when the eye falls upon the <term>point of convergence<note>The focal point where refracted rays meet.</note></term> itself, a single point of the visible object is perceived through the entire lens. This same point, when the eye was close, was perceived through a small portion of the lens, either smaller than or certainly no larger than the <term>pupil<note>original: "pupilla"; the opening of the eye through which light enters.</note></term> of the eye.*

<vocab>lens, eye, refraction, of the hemisphere, proposition, point of convergence, pupil</vocab>

<summary>This page explains a paradox of optics: although a distant lens occupies a smaller portion of our field of view, the individual objects seen through it actually appear larger as the eye moves toward the lens's focal point. This demonstrates the geometric relationship between the observer's distance, the lens's position, and the resulting magnification.</summary>
<keywords>refraction, magnification, visual angle, point of convergence, convex lens, dioptrics, field of view</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the geometric demonstration supporting Proposition 22, which explores how the distance between the eye and a lens affects the perceived size of a distant object. The previous page introduced the proposition, noting a seemingly counter-intuitive effect where moving further from the lens can make an object appear larger.</meta>

Now for the demonstration. Let it be then, as established above in Proposition 79, that the power of a lens convex on both sides <note>original: "lentis utrimque convexæ"; this refers to a standard magnifying lens curved outward on both faces.</note> is concentrated into the surface **AB** of a dense body <note>A "dense body" refers to the glass medium of the lens.</note> extending as far as the visible object. Let that surface face toward the eye. And let the eye be placed at point **F**, which is nearby, and at point **C**, which is further away. Moreover, let there be points **D** and **E** on surface **AB**, toward which lines **FD** and **FE** are drawn from the near eye **F**; these lines form the angle **DFE**, the <term>visual angle<note>The angle formed at the eye by the rays of light coming from the extremities of an object, which determines its perceived size.</note></term> by which the visible object is perceived. I say that the eye at **C**, being further away, requires a larger angle to perceive that same distant visible object.

*A geometric diagram showing optical refraction. Below a curved horizontal line (surface AB), two points are labeled: F (closer to the curve) and C (further below). Rays emerge from F (FD and FE) and from C (CA and CB), passing through the surface AB. Above the surface, these rays refract and converge at points G and H. At the very top, horizontal labels L, G, H, and M are positioned. The diagram illustrates how the angle of vision (subtended at F or C) changes with the distance of the eye from the refracting surface.*

For let the refracted rays **DG** and **EH** be extended from points **D** and **E** as far as the visible object. If that distant visible object is not seen from point **C** by a larger angle, then let us assume it is seen by an equal angle; thus, let **CA** and **CB** be drawn from **C** to the surface parallel to **FD** and **FE**, so that angles **ACB** and **DFE** are equal. Since, therefore, **CA** and **CB** are more inclined toward surface **AB** than **FD** and **FE** are, then **CA** and **CB** will be <term>refracted<note>Refraction is the bending of light as it passes from one medium, such as air, into another, such as glass.</note></term> more than **FD** and **FE**, according to Proposition 10. Wherefore the refracted rays of **CA** and **CB** (for this reason and according to Proposition 34) will intersect with the refracted rays of **FD** and **FE** on opposite sides: because **CA** is parallel to **FD**, just as **CB** is to **FE**. They will intersect, and let the points of intersection be **G** and **H**. And let the refracted rays of **CA** and **CB** be **AG** and **BH**. Since it was posited that the visible object is seen by angle **ACB**, it will be seen and perceived by the refracted rays **AG** and **BH**. However, it is also seen and perceived by the refracted rays **DG** and **EH**.

<meta>Catchword: Therefore (original: Ergo)</meta>

*Therefore, the boundaries of the visible object must necessarily be **G** and **H**. Thus, the visible object will not be distant, but close; which contradicts our assumptions. Therefore, the eye at **C** will not see this distant object by means of rays **CA** and **CB** and angle **ACB** (which is equal to **DFE**), but by exterior lines, say **CI** and **CK**, and angle **ICK**, which is larger than **ACB** or **DFE**: so that the refracted rays **IL** and **KM**—produced from **CI** and **CK**—can extend almost parallel to **DG** and **EH**, in order to encompass the extreme points of the distant visible object.* <note>The author is arguing that as the eye moves further from a lens, the visual angle must actually increase to keep the distant object in view, explaining why objects appear larger as you step back from a magnifying glass.</note>

## PROPOSITION 83.

*A geometric diagram illustrating optical refraction through two convex lenses. At the bottom, a point **O** represents the eye. Above it is a small convex lens **ST** with its center of curvature at point **V**. Above that is a larger convex lens **PQ** with its center of curvature at point **R**. Light rays originate from **O**, pass through points **S** and **T** on the smaller lens, and points **P** and **Q** on the larger lens, eventually extending toward points **Y** and **X** at the top. Construction lines **SV** and **TV** are drawn parallel to radii **PR** and **QR** respectively.*

If the eye views the same distant visible object through two different convex lenses individually: if the distance of each lens from the eye is in the same proportion to the diameter of its <term>convexity<note>The degree of curvature of the lens; here likely referring to the radius of the sphere from which the lens surface is formed.</note></term>; the visible object will appear the same size through each lens individually. However, if the proportion is varied, the object will appear larger through that lens whose distance is greater in proportion.

*Let the eye be **O**, and let **PQ** be a large lens described around center **R**. Let points **P** and **Q** be connected to **O**, and let the smaller lens **ST** be positioned on the points of these lines; let this lens be described from the point of their intersection **V**, by drawing lines **SV** and **TV** from points **S** and **T** parallel to **PR** and **QR**. And let the rays **OP** and **OQ** be refracted into **PVY** and **QX**.*

*Since, therefore, **VS** and **RP** are parallel, and likewise **VT** and **RQ**: the straight lines **OS** and **OQ** incident upon them will make equal angles **OPR** and **OSV**, as well as **OQR** and **OTV**. But **VTS** and **RQP** are also equal, as they lie between the lenses and their <term>radii<note>original: "semidiametros"; literally half-diameters.</note></term>: therefore **OTS** and **OQP** will also be equal, since equal parts have been subtracted. Thus, **OT** falls upon **TS** and **OQ** upon*

<meta>catchword: upon</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a demonstration on visual angles and introduces Proposition 83, which establishes that magnification depends on the ratio between the lens's distance from the eye and its curvature.</summary>

*...are inclined over [lens] **QP**. Therefore, the refractions on both sides will be equal. The rays refracted from **S** and **T** will then be parallel to the rays **PW** and **QX**; let these [refracted rays] be **SY** and **TZ**. And since they are parallel, they will encompass the same visible object perceptibly <note>original: "ad sensum," meaning to the limit of human perception</note> according to Proposition 23, and under the same angle **POQ** or **SOT**. Therefore, the object will be judged to be of the same size, according to Proposition 66. Moreover, just as **VS**, the <term>radius<note>original: "semidiameter." In early optics, this refers to the radius of the sphere that defines the lens's curvature.</note></term> of lens **ST**, is to its distance **SO** from the eye, so is **PR**, the radius of lens **PQ**, to its distance **PO** from the eye—and the same is true if the terms are swapped. Thus, the first part of the proposition is clear. Now for the second part.*

*I say now that if the proportion of the distances is different from the proportion of the radii—for example, if the eye **O** is at a distance **SO** from lens **ST**, but the eye **V** is at a distance **PV** from lens **PQ**—then the visible objects will appear larger through lens **PQ**, whose distance **PV** from the eye is greater in proportion to its radius **PR** than the distance **SO** of lens **ST** from the eye **O** is in proportion to its radius **SV**. This is because the ratio of **OS** to **SV** is the same as **OP** to **PR**, yet **OP** is shorter than **VP**.*

*For by Proposition 22, when lens **PQ** is in place, the visible objects appear larger to the eye at **V** than to the eye at **O**. But according to what has been demonstrated so far, the objects appear equal to the eye at **O** whether viewed through lens **ST** or lens **PQ** in this position. Therefore, the visible objects appear larger to the eye at **V** with lens **PQ** than to the eye at **O** with lens **ST**.*

->PROPOSITION 84.<-

The further the eye moves beyond the point of convergence, the smaller the inverted objects appear.

*The proof of this proposition is understood more through explanation and a comparison with the preceding ones.*

*For let us begin from Proposition 37 in reverse, and let the eye take the place of the radiant point; for it is the same according to Proposition 3. Thus, if the eye is so close to the lens, then its rays passing through the lens diverge, even after being refracted toward the visible object; and it happens, as demonstrated in Proposition 70, that the visible object appears upright. However, as the eye moves a little further from the lens, the visible objects are magnified according to Proposition 22, although the quantity of them seen is reduced according to Proposition 21. From there, as the eye comes near the <term>point of convergence<note>The focal point where the light rays meet and cross.</note></term>, its rays having entered the lens,*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how light behaves when passing through a convex lens. The previous page established the proportional relationship between the lens's curvature (radius), its distance from the eye, and the resulting magnification. Here, the author describes the specific transition point where an image flips from being upright to being inverted as the observer moves away from the lens.</meta>

having entered, they become parallel by the converse of Proposition 35. If you move the eye further from the lens by more than a fingernail’s breadth <note>original: "latum unguem," a Latin idiom for a very small distance</note>, all the **rays of the eye** <note>Early optical theory often drew "rays" as lines originating from the eye to calculate geometry, even if the author understood light entered the eye.</note> refracted through the lens begin to converge—first at a point behind the visible object (if the lines were extended), and then exactly at a single point on a distant visible object. At that moment, nothing of that visible object is discerned except for a single point; that point appears as large as the lens itself, and extremely blurred. If you pull the eye a little further back from the lens, that convergence of rays or lines from the eye (refracted in the lens) now leaves the visible object and moves closer toward the lens. But because the converging rays cross one another and continue beyond their intersection (by Proposition 21), these lines—drawn from the eye through the lens—fall upon the visible object in an inverse order beyond this intersection point (per Proposition 76). First, they capture the smallest particle of the object, nearly a point; thus, it begins to happen, as demonstrated in Proposition 75, that the visible object appears **inverted** in some small part of itself.

Then, as the eye is moved further and further away, that intersection point descends more and more toward the lens (by Proposition 41), and the angle of the intersection becomes larger, encompassing more of the visible objects. This continues until the eye is at a very great distance; then the lines from its center reach the lens almost parallel, and it happens (as in Proposition 34) that they meet at a certain and measured point beyond the lens. Therefore, however large the angle **BFD** is in the diagram for Proposition 34, just as large a portion of the **hemisphere** <note>original: "hemisphærio." Here, the author refers to the entire field of vision available to the observer.</note> is seen in an inverted position. For the lines **BF** and **DF**, having traveled further, cross each other again and thus fall upon the visible objects.

However, those visible objects that are closer than this intersection of lines (extending from the center of the eye beyond the lens) are always excluded from this inversion. Thus, it can happen that within the same field of view, certain distant things appear inverted while other nearby things appear upright.

These things being established: first, the further the lens is from the eye (per Proposition 67), the smaller the angle under which it is perceived, and with it, everything seen through it in an inverted position also appears smaller. Furthermore, as the lens moves away from the eye, more of the visible hemisphere is received into it, as has already been explained. Therefore, more things are seen together, but smaller, in a more distant position of the eye, than...

## 85. PROBLEM

**To present visible objects clearly with a single convex lens, but inverted and smaller.**

Let the eye be placed beyond the <term>point of convergence (the focal point where light rays meet after passing through the lens)</term> at a specific point according to the measure of its own visual ability. For, according to Proposition 78, a <note>original: "Μύωψ" (Myops)</note> nearsighted person will see clearly; however, according to Proposition 75, the orientation will be inverted, and according to Proposition 84, the objects will be smaller than they truly are, depending on whether the eye requires a distant point for clear vision.

->Thus far concerning a single convex lens; now concerning convex lenses joined together.<-

## 86. PROBLEM

**To present visible objects larger and clear using two convex lenses, but in an inverted position.**

Let two convex lenses be arranged toward the eye in such a way that the one further away would, on its own, send an inverted image to the eye—though not a clear one—because the eye is closer to the lens than the point where clear images are formed (per Proposition 78). For example, imagine if in the diagram for Proposition 75, the <term>divergence of rays (the spreading out of light)</term> from a single point—labeled D C and D P—and the resulting angles O D P were too wide for the eye to process, and the eye at position O P were outside the points of convergence D and F.

Then, a second, closer lens is placed between that first lens and the eye. It is positioned so that the eye is within this second lens's point of convergence—as if, in the diagrams for Propositions 70 and 75, the eye were at position I G. In this case, the eye viewing through this second lens alone would see objects as upright but similarly blurred, though for the opposite reason (per Proposition 71). Therefore, because the divergence from the further lens is too great, the opposite <term>convergence (the narrowing of light rays)</term> now provided by the closer lens will "heal" that excessive divergence. In this way, the light is corrected and reaches the eye in an improved state to provide clear vision.

<summary>This page describes how a single convex lens can produce small, inverted, but clear images for nearsighted observers, and introduces the concept of using a second convex lens to correct distorted light rays for a magnified, clear, yet inverted view.</summary>
<keywords>convex lens, point of convergence, focal point, nearsightedness, myopia, ray divergence, ray convergence, optical correction, compound lenses</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstration of optical principles using multiple convex lenses. The previous page (Problem 86) began explaining how two convex lenses can create a magnified but inverted image by having the first lens create an inverted image that the second lens then magnifies before it reaches the eye.</meta>

*And because the image of a visible object is inverted by a single lens: the closer lens, however, does not invert again what it receives from the more distant lens, but transmits it to the eye exactly as it receives it, according to our assumption. It receives, relative to the visible object, an inverted image; therefore, it sends it to the eye inverted relative to the visible object.*

*And because the inverted image itself appears larger than the object when near the <term>point of convergence (the focal point where light rays intersect)</term>, equal when further away, and smaller when even further away, per Proposition 84; therefore, this inverted image, once magnified by the closer lens, will in the first two cases result in being larger than the actual object. In the final case, it will be larger, equal, or smaller, depending on the ratio of the lenses to one another, which is left to the craftsman's choice. Certainly, however, it will be larger than the size the lens nearest the eye received from the more distant lens, per Proposition 80.*

### XXCVII. PROBLEMA.

->*To provide clear and upright visible objects with two convex lenses, but smaller.*<-

*These two convex lenses must have a sufficiently different degree of convexity. The eye should be placed beyond the focal points of both, closer to the point of clarity for one lens and further from the point of clarity for the other, so that neither lens by itself would allow the inverted image to be seen clearly. If the lenses are aligned in this position with the eye, their opposing defects will cancel each other out, and clear vision will result.*

*However, for the image to be <term>upright (in its natural orientation)</term>, it must be inverted twice. To achieve this, the closer lens must also be positioned far enough from the more distant one to be beyond its <term>points of convergence</term>.*

The rays converge at a focal point (labeled with points I, G, D, and F) and then diverge until they hit a second, smaller convex lens near the bottom, labeled O and P. Finally, the rays converge once more toward the base of the diagram at points T and Y, demonstrating the inversion and refocusing of the image.</image-desc>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition 87, which explains how a pair of convex lenses can be used to see an object in its upright (correct) orientation, rather than inverted. It provides a geometric proof of how light rays travel through two lenses to reach the eye, and why this specific arrangement results in a smaller image.</meta>

*For let **AB** be the visible object, and **CD, EF** be the lens further from the eye. Let **K** be the <term>point of convergence (the focal point where the light rays cross)</term>. If, therefore, the image of **AB** itself is inverted by this single lens: the point where the image appears inverted will be further beyond **K** from the lens, according to Proposition 75. Let that location be **L**. And because the appearance of the lens **EF** itself—and with it the inverted image of the object **AB**—must be inverted a second time by another lens, which is **GH**, the inverted image of the object **AB** is contained by the lines **ADFL** and **BCEL**. It is therefore necessary for lens **GH** to be placed beyond **L**, according to Proposition 76. However, **L** was distant from lens **EF** beyond the point of convergence **K**. Therefore, the second lens **GH** will be moved much further away, beyond the point of convergence **K**; so that the rays **FLG** and **ELH** coming from the extremities of the object, having finally undergone a second refraction in **G** and **H**, meet again at last and are directed to the eye at point **I**.* <note>By inverting the image twice—once through each lens—the final image reaching the eye at point I is "erect" or right-side up.</note>

*Finally, this image is smaller than the visible object. For first, the appearance of lens **EF** itself (and of those things seen through it), when inverted by lens **GH** and appearing distinct, will be smaller at **I**, according to Proposition 85. But by the same principle, with the eye positioned at **L**, the visible object **AB** itself also appears to occupy less space in the lens when inverted by lens **CD** than its actual size. Because **L** cannot be very close to the point of convergence **K**, lest there be too much <term>confusion (visual blurring or lack of focus)</term>. For **L** must be close to the <term>point of distinction (the point where the image is perfectly in focus)</term>, as must **I**. Therefore, for a double reason, the visible object **AB** is represented as small.*

### XXCIIX. PROPOSITION 88. PROBLEM.

   **To project visible objects onto paper in an upright position using two convex lenses.**

*A problem long sought after. Let the convex lenses be arranged as in Proposition 87, specifically such that the lens closer to the paper is beyond...*

<image-desc>
A vertical optical ray diagram is positioned in the right-hand margin. At the top, a horizontal line segment AB represents a visible object. Converging rays from points A and B pass through a double convex lens (or two lenses in close proximity) labeled CD and EF. These rays intersect at a focal point K on the central vertical axis. Below K, a point L is marked on the axis. Further down, the rays diverge and pass through a second convex lens labeled GH. After passing through this lens, the rays converge again toward a point I at the very bottom of the axis.

<meta>The text cuts off at "beyond" (ultra K), which matches the catchword "tra K" (part of "ultra K") provided in the metadata. This continues the setup for the next proposition.</meta>

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof that using two convex lenses can reinvert an image to appear upright to the eye, though the resulting image is diminished in size. It also introduces the practical application of projecting such upright images onto paper, a precursor to the technology of the camera obscura and telescopes.</summary>
<keywords>convex lens, refraction, focal point, image inversion, upright image, optics, light rays, projection, Proposition 88</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical proof from Proposition 87. On the previous page, the author demonstrated how two convex lenses can invert an image twice to make it appear upright (erect) to the eye, though this specific arrangement resulted in a smaller image than the original object. This page concludes that proof and moves toward solving the problem of how to make that upright image larger using a third lens.</meta>

...beyond the **K** <term>points of convergence (the focal points where light rays cross)</term>. For the <term>light pencils (bundles of rays)</term> that end in a point near **K** expand again beyond **K** and diverge from one another. Thus, a second <term>convex lens (a lens that curves outward like a bowl turned over)</term> receives them; through a new <term>refraction (the bending of light as it passes through the glass)</term>, it both focuses each individual ray again and makes them all converge toward a new intersection. Once that point is passed, the rays diverge once more, and thus they strike the paper in their original order with their focused points. This occurs in the diagram on page 86, just as if the visible object **CE** were translated into the image **DF**, and **OP** were now not the eye, but a second lens placed below it. But if lens **OP** is placed very close below the image **DF**, the image **TV** requires the paper to be positioned far away, and it becomes large.

## LXXXIX. PROBLEM.

->*To present visible objects upright, distinct, and larger using three convex lenses.*<-

Let two convex lenses and the eye be arranged so that what was stated in Proposition 87 occurs, with this one exception: that the eye is closer to the point of distinction <note>The "point of distinction" refers to the specific distance where the image appears perfectly clear or in focus.</note> and thus sees the object confusedly. For a third convex lens applied in the manner described on page 86—with the second lens there, namely so that the eye is closer to the lens than the point of convergence—will cause the <term>image (the "species" or visual appearance)</term> (which has been twice inverted and is now upright, and thus made smaller) to be enlarged again. If the proportion of the lenses is correct, this enlargement will exceed the previous reduction caused by the two lenses in Proposition 87. Clarity, moreover, will follow from those causes cited on page 86.

->*Thus far concerning convex lenses; now follows the discussion of concave lenses.*<-

## XC. PROPOSITION.

Light rays from a single glowing point, whether parallel or diverging, if they enter a <term>concave surface (one that curves inward like a cave)</term> of a denser material (provided the glowing point is outside the center of that surface), diverge further through the denser body.

From glowing point **A**, let diverging rays **AB** and **AC** descend into **BC**...

<meta>This page concludes a demonstration regarding the divergence of light rays passing through a concave boundary into a denser medium, likely finishing Proposition 90. *...the concave surface of a denser material, the center of which is **D**, within the span of **AB** and **AC**. I say that **AB** and **AC**, having undergone <term>refraction (the bending of light as it enters a new material)</term> at points **B** and **C**, diverge even more once they are inside the area below **BC**. To prove this, let <term>perpendiculars (lines drawn at a 90-degree angle to the surface)</term> **DB** and **DC** be drawn from center **D** to the surface, and let them be extended some distance to **E** and **F**. Let the paths of **AB** and **AC** also be extended toward **G** and **H**. Therefore, since ray **AB** is angled toward the surface of the denser material, it is refracted at point **B**; having been bent from its original path **BG**, it will incline toward the perpendicular **BE** (according to Rule II), becoming path **BL**. Similarly, **AC** will be refracted at **C**, and having been bent from its original path **CH**, it will incline toward the perpendicular **CF**, becoming path **CM**. However, the lines **DBE** and **DCF** <note>original: "DBF, DCF" — likely a typo for DBE and DCF</note> diverge more because they originate from a closer point [**D**] than the paths **AG** and **AH**, which originate from a more distant point [**A**] but pass through the same points **B** and **C**. Since **BL** and **CM** move toward those more widely diverging perpendiculars and away from the less diverging paths **BG** and **CH**, they must diverge more than the original rays **AB** and **AC**, and they do so within the dense body.*

<note>The diagram illustrates a geometric proof of how light behaves when passing from a medium like air into something denser like glass or water when the boundary is curved inward (concave). It uses the center of the curve (D) to determine the "perpendicular," which is the reference point for how much the light will bend.</note>

### 92.

->Rays diverging within a denser body toward its concave boundary,<-
->having passed through it, diverge even more.<-

*Suppose rays **BL** and **CM** diverge toward the concave boundary **LM** of a dense material <note>such as glass or water</note>. Its center is **P**, from which the perpendiculars **PL** and **PM** extend to points **L** and **M**. Let the paths of **BL** and **CM** be extended to **Q** and **R** beyond the points where they hit the surface, **L** and **M**. Because these rays **BL** and **CM**, while traveling inside the dense medium, strike the surface **LM** of a <term>rarer (less dense)</term> medium—or, what is the same thing, the boundary of the dense medium they are currently in—they will undergo <term>refraction (the bending of light)</term>. They will bend away from the perpendiculars **PL** and **PM**. Consequently, the refracted rays will not follow the paths **LQ** and **MR**, but will be bent further outward (according to Rule 2). Let these new paths be **LN** and **MO**. Since the original paths **BLQ** and **CMR** were already diverging, the refracted paths **LN** and **MO** will diverge even more.*

### 93. PROPOSITION.

->If light rays travel through a dense body as parallel lines,<-
->they will diverge after passing through its concave boundary.<-

<image-desc>A small hand-drawn diagram illustrating optical rays passing through a curved lens-like body. Two parallel lines labeled with Greek letters (beta, delta, gamma, epsilon) represent incident rays. The body has curved boundaries. *Let **beta-delta** <note>original: "β δ"</note> and **gamma-epsilon** <note>original: "γ ε"</note> be parallel rays. Of these, no more than one can be perpendicular to the surface at points **beta** or **gamma**. The rest, striking the surface at an angle, will be refracted away from their perpendiculars according to Rule 2. Therefore, as they exit, they will diverge as before, becoming **beta-zeta** <note>original: "β ζ"</note> and **gamma-eta** <note>original: "γ η"</note>. Likewise, having passed through the other boundary **delta-epsilon**, they will diverge into **delta-theta** <note>original: "δ θ"</note> and **epsilon-kappa** <note>original: "ε κ"</note>.*

->radiating point relative to the lens, will always diverge more after passing<-
->through the lens if it is concave on both sides, or even flat on one side.<-

*For if this were not true, it would not be true for a source point located within the center of the concavity, because in that case (according to Proposition 91), the divergence within the medium is reduced. Likewise, it would not be true if the lens were flat on both sides. And it would be least true...*

<meta>This page continues the geometric exploration of how light behaves when passing through curved, dense media. It transitions from general principles of refraction in concave surfaces to the specific application of these principles in correcting nearsightedness with concave lenses.</meta>

...if both conditions occur together. And indeed it is true when both occur. For suppose there is a dense <term>parallelepiped (a solid block with parallel sides)</term> **CB**, **ED**. Let the rays within it be inclined toward each other as **EC** and **DB**, with equal angles **CED** and **BDE**. These will be <term>refracted (bent)</term> at points **C**, **E**, **B**, and **D**. According to Proposition 3, the refracted rays **EG** and **CA** will be parallel; likewise **DF** and **BA**, because **CB** and **ED** are parallel. Therefore, the <term>divergence (spreading apart)</term> in **AC** and **AB** is equal to that in **EG** and **DF**. Now, let the surface **CB** be hollowed out by the circle **CHB**. The inclination of **EC** upon this concave surface will therefore be reduced, which means the refraction will also be smaller. Thus, let the upper refracted ray be **CI**, and in the other case **BI**. Therefore, **IC** and **IB** will now diverge less than **EB** and **DF**. And they will diverge even less if **ED** is also hollowed out, because **CE** will be more inclined upon the new surface. And the refracted rays will diverge more than the current **EG** and **DF**, as if they were **EL** and **DO**.

### 95. PROPOSITION.

->Distant visible objects are represented clearly at a single point to the<-
->eye of a <term>nearsighted person</term> <note>original Greek: τῷ μύωπι (to the myope)</note> by using a sufficiently concave lens.<-

For distant points radiate parallel rays according to Proposition 23. Since <term>nearsighted people</term> <note>original Greek: μύωπες (myopes)</note> are accustomed to nearby objects, they are consequently accustomed to diverging rays (according to Proposition 24); for this reason, they see remote things confusedly. However, concave lenses cause parallel rays to diverge, as shown in Proposition 90. They thus allow the points of those parallel rays to be seen distinctly. This does not happen, however, in every position of the concave lens. For the same point **A**, when radiating through a concave lens **CE** that is further from the eye **BD**, utilizes only a small portion of the lens **CE**. This is because any light that radiates into a wider area misses the eye due to excessive divergence. In contrast, the same point **A** with a closer lens **OI** utilizes a larger portion **OI** to spread the rays from **A** into the entire pupil **BD**. Yet the small portion **CE** is closer to the <term>perpendicular (the straight line at a 90-degree angle to the lens)</term> from **A** into the lens than the...

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s geometric analysis of concave lenses. Having established how these lenses help the nearsighted eye focus by spreading light rays apart, he now explains why these lenses make objects appear smaller and why the field of view narrows as the lens is moved further from the face.</meta>

...wide **OI**. Therefore, the <term>inclination</term> <note>The angle at which light strikes a surface.</note> of the closer rays **AC** and **AE** toward the surface is less than that of rays **AO** and **AI**. Consequently, the <term>refraction</term> (the bending of light) **ACB** and **AED** is also less than **AOB** and **AID**, following Proposition 10. Because of this, the <term>divergence</term> (the spreading apart of rays) **CB** and **ED** is less than **OB** and **ID**. Now, since every eye requires a specific amount of divergence to see clearly, there is a specific correct position for every lens.

### PROPOSITION 96.
### Visible objects appear smaller when seen through concave lenses.

Let **BD** in the previous diagram be the visible object and **A** the center of the eye. Since the rays from **A** are refracted outward by the lens **CE** (according to Proposition 94), it is clear that if we connect points **B** and **A**, and **D** and **A**, the angle **BAD**—at which the object would be seen by the naked eye—will be larger than the angle **CAE**, which is the angle at which the object **DB** is seen through the lens **CE** (according to Proposition 66). Therefore, the object will be perceived as smaller. 

The eye does not "know" what happens to the rays **AC** and **AE** at the points **C** and **E** on the glass; instead, it assumes the rays continue in straight lines, following Proposition 19. If they actually traveled in straight lines, they would only capture a small part of the visible object **AD**. However, because they are refracted, these rays actually capture the entire object. Thus, the appearance of the whole object is squeezed into a space that would normally only hold a small part, making the whole appear smaller than it truly is.

### PROPOSITION 97.
### If a concave lens is moved further away from the eye, fewer visible objects will reach the eye through the lens.

Let the eye be at **A**, with the lens **BC** placed nearby. Now, let the eye be at **D**, with the lens **EF** further away, though the lens itself is the same size as **BC**. Therefore, the width of the lens **EF** is equal to the width of **BC**, but the distances **DE** and **DF** are longer than the distances **AB** and **AC**. Consequently, the angle **BAC** (from the closer eye) is larger than the angle **EDF** (from the eye further away). Now, let the rays be refracted, and let the refracted rays be **BG**...

**CH** and **EI**, **FK** according to Proposition 94. Therefore, the rays **BG** and **CH** always diverge more than **EI** and **FK**. For let **ELF** be a triangle that can be compared to triangle **BAC**. Since the rays **DE** and **LE** descend from points **D** and **L** to the same point **E** on the denser surface <note>The surface of the glass lens.</note>, they will cross each other once <term>refraction</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes from air into glass.</note> occurs at point **E**. The lower ray **LE** will emerge as the upper ray **EM**, and similarly **LF** will emerge as **FN**, according to Proposition 11. Therefore, **EM** and **FN** diverge more than **EI** and **FK**, and consequently, they capture more of the <term>hemisphere</term> <note>original: "hemisphærio"; referring to the total field of view or the visual world.</note>. For this reason, **BG** and **CH**—refracted by a lens that is close by—will capture more than **EI** and **FK** which are refracted by a lens that is far away.

### PROPOSITION 98.

**If a concave lens is moved further from the eye, the visible objects appear smaller, provided the lens does not become closer to the object than it is to the eye.**

*For as the lens is moved away, its <term>apparent size</term> <note>original: "magnitudo aspectabilis"; how large the object appears to the observer's eye.</note> decreases at a steady rate, according to Proposition 67. However, it does not lose its grasp on distant objects at that same rate. Although it always receives fewer objects as it moves away (according to Proposition 97), that decrease is only a small fraction of the whole if the objects are far off. This is because the refractions change very little at a greater distance, just as the <term>inclinations</term> <note>The angles at which the light rays strike the lens.</note> of the rays **LE**, **DE**, etc., onto the lens **EF** (in the previous diagram) change very little when the distance is increased. Therefore, more is lost from the apparent size of the lens itself than from the total number of things seen through it. Consequently, the entire collection of objects is seen within a smaller angle, and therefore each individual object within that view appears smaller as well.*

<meta>catchword: Aliter: (Otherwise:)</meta>

<summary>Kepler demonstrates through geometry that moving a concave lens further from the eye shrinks the apparent size of objects because the visual angle narrows more significantly than the total field of view.</summary>

<meta>This page provides an alternative geometric proof for Proposition 98, which concerns how the distance of a concave lens from the eye affects the perceived size of an object. It continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the divergence of light rays.</meta>

*Alternatively: Let the eye be at point A, and let the straight-line rays be ABF and ACG, which form the angle FAG. Let these rays pass through a nearby lens BC and a distant lens FG. The rays will therefore be <term>refracted</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes through the glass of the lens.</note> outwards at the points on lens BC, according to Proposition 94. Let the refracted rays be BE and CD. However, since the lines AF and AG intercept a larger portion of the lens at the distance of FG, the <term>refraction</term> at FG will be even greater than at BC, according to Proposition 11. Therefore, the rays exiting at FG will diverge more widely than those exiting from points B and C; consequently, they will eventually intersect with them. Let them intersect, and let the points of intersection be E and D, with these refracted rays being FE and GD. Therefore, since FE and GD become more external <note>Meaning they spread further outward from the center.</note> than BE and CD after their meeting and intersection, it follows that no visible object (except for one whose edges happen to be at the exact points of intersection E and D) will be seen through both the near lens and the far lens at the same angle BAC or FAG. For objects further away than the intersection points E and D, such as the visible object HI, which is contained by the refracted rays BI and CH of the nearby lens, it will not be contained by the refracted rays FE and GD coming to the eye at that same angle FAG. Instead, it will be contained by rays further inside the lens FG, which come to the eye A at a smaller angle. Thus, objects appear smaller through the more distant lens GF than through the nearer lens CB, according to Proposition 66.*

<meta>This page transitions from discussing the perceived size of objects through concave lenses to the conditions required for clear, distinct vision. ## 99. PROPOSITION.

A concave lens, if it is to be applied very close to the eye—either for all people at a fixed distance, as when spectacles <note>original: *perspicilla*</note> sit upon the nose—then each person requires their own specific lens to achieve distinct vision.

*For according to Proposition 95, every concave lens has a certain distance relative to the eye's ability to see clearly. If the choice of distance is taken away <note>Meaning, if you cannot move the lens back and forth to find the "sweet spot" for focus.</note>, the eye must instead be granted a choice of lenses, otherwise it will see distant objects unclearly. For either the lens will not be concave enough, and thus will not correct the blurriness caused by the parallelism of the rays, or it will be too concave, and thus will induce excessive <term>convergence</term> <note>While a concave lens spreads light out (divergence), the author here refers to the resulting optical requirement for the eye to focus those rays; "convergentia" may also refer to the way rays appear to meet at a point behind the lens.</note>, causing a blurriness of the opposite kind to the first.*

## 100. PROPOSITION.

Lentes which, due to excessive concavity, produce a blurred image when placed very close to the eye, will produce a distinct image when held at a certain distance, and vice versa.

<margin>$\theta$</margin> <note>original: "pilla" probably a typo for "pupilla" (pupil)</note> When the pupil is applied to the lens at KL, it now encompasses and intercepts rays FG which are diverging too much; therefore, point A will be seen unclearly at eye-position KL, but distinctly at eye-position HI.

Thus far, we have dealt with convex lenses separately, and concave lenses separately; there follows now the discussion of joined concave and convex lenses.

### CI. DEFINITION.

**Tube** <note>original: *Tubus*</note> is used to denote an opaque hollow cylinder, whose two openings are closed by transparent glasses; specifically, it refers to that optical instrument with which we look at distant things as if they were close at hand. <note>This is a formal definition of the telescope, which at the time of writing was a revolutionary new invention.</note>

### CII.

Of its openings, one with its glass is directed toward the eye in a functional position, while the other is directed toward the visible object.

### CIII. POSTULATE.

That in the tube, the line passing through the centers of the convexities and concavities of both glasses be one and the same. That is, that the glasses be parallel, and that the line meets them at right angles. <note>This describes the "optical axis." For a telescope to work, the centers of both lenses must be perfectly aligned on a single straight line.</note>

### CIV.

If a concave lens intercepts the radiations of a single point which, having passed through a convex lens and undergone <term>refraction</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes from one medium (air) into another (glass).</note>, are converging...

<meta>This page continues the geometric analysis of how a concave lens affects rays of light that have already been bent by a convex lens. This is the foundational theory for the Galilean telescope (which uses a convex objective and a concave eyepiece) and the projection of images.</meta>

*For if NL, OM converge* <note>original: *convergant*</note> *toward the concave [surface] LM as if they were to meet at point* $\lambda$. *Therefore, once <term>refraction</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes from air into glass or vice versa.</note> has occurred at LM, the now-refracted rays LB, MC traveling through the dense body converge less toward the other concave surface BC, as if they were going to meet at point D, according to the converse of [Proposition] XCII. Indeed, by that same principle, when LB and MC have undergone a second refraction at BC, the refracted rays BA and CA converge even less and finally meet at A. And thus the point of meeting, A, is moved further away, for it ought to have occurred at* $\lambda$.

*But if the refraction is a little greater, then the final refracted rays BA, CA will extend to infinity before they meet, according to the converse of [Proposition] XCIII. Finally, if the first refraction is so great that the rays* $\rho \delta, \kappa \epsilon$ *converging toward* $\delta \epsilon$ *become parallel* $\delta \beta, \epsilon \gamma$ *inside [the glass], then by the converse of [Proposition] XCIII they will diverge again as* $\beta \zeta, \gamma \kappa$.

# CV. PROBLEM.
## To project visible objects onto paper using a concave and a convex lens in a larger size than with a convex lens alone, but inverted.

In the diagram for Proposition XLIV, let *GH* be a convex lens, and let *F, B, D* be the points of convergence, or the <term>apices of the pencils</term> <note>original: *apices penicillorum*. A "pencil" of light refers to a cone of rays originating from a single point of an object.</note>. Let a concave lens *LN* be placed a little above *FBD*. Then the visible object *CAE* will first be projected upon the concave lens near *DBF*, but a bit unclearly, because the concave lens intercepts the tips of the light pencils: and it will be projected in an inverted position, because the crossing of the pencils has already occurred at *GH* and the tips of the pencils have now almost spread out from one another, each being forced into a narrow space within itself. Therefore, the individual pencils passing through the concave lens according to [Proposition] CIV either end in a more distant point *SPT*, and then the picture becomes distinct upon paper placed there, or the rays of a single pencil proceed in parallel, and then the picture remains in that...

...with the slight blur with which it first arrived at the concave lens; or finally, the <term>pencils of rays</term> <note>original: *penicilli*; Kepler uses this term to describe a narrow bundle of light rays originating from a single point on an object.</note> diverge and spread out, and then the image becomes more and more blurred as the paper is moved away from the concave lens. However, the image *SPT* is rendered larger than the image *FBD* (which would be formed by the convex lens *GH* alone), because the rays *F* and *D*, once <term>refracted</term> <note>Refraction is the bending of light as it passes from one medium, like air, into another, like glass.</note> in the concave lens *LN*, are bent outward toward *S* and *T* through the outer edges *L* and *N*—the outer rays always being bent more than the inner ones, which pass through the center.

## CVI. NOTE.

Regarding what Giambattista della Porta <note>Giambattista della Porta (1535–1615) was a famous Italian scientist and dramatist whose work on "Natural Magic" included many influential, if sometimes exaggerated, optical experiments.</note> claims—that he can first collect the sun's rays and then, once collected, send them out to infinity to burn things—even though he speaks of mirrors, it seems it ought to be understood as referring to <term>lenses</term> <note>original: *perspicillis*; in this era, the word could refer to eyeglasses or the newly invented telescope.</note>, because he deliberately hid his true meaning. But if this is to be understood as referring to lenses, the technique will be none other than first collecting many rays with a convex lens, and then, just before they reach their point of meeting, intercepting them with a concave lens. This concave lens would make the converging rays parallel, as was stated in Proposition CV. Therefore, see what was said against this in Proposition LVI. To those points I now add this further observation: even if you correct Porta’s phrasing regarding an "infinite burning line" to mean the same thing as a <term>burning cone</term> <note>original: *conus ustorius*; the cone-shaped path of light rays converging to a focal point where heat is most intense.</note> extended as far as you wish, so that even then, through the intersection of the rays...

<column-break/>

After passing through the convex lens, the rays continue to converge toward a second, biconcave lens positioned further down, labeled L on the left and N on the right. After passing through this second lens, the rays are shown diverging. The central ray follows a straight path from A through the centers of the lenses to points B and P. The outer rays from C and E pass through the edges of the lenses (G and H, then L and N) to reach points S and T respectively.

...the burning is sought at the end of the cone; nevertheless, nothing is achieved. For if the intersection <note>original: *sectio*; referring to the point where light rays cross.</note> causes the burning, a strong intersection will provide a strong burning, and a weak one a weak burning. But at the vertex of a very long cone, the intersection will be at its weakest.

### PROPOSITION CVII.

When a concave lens is placed very close to the eye—which, if used alone, would make visible objects appear blurred—any convex lens of a larger circle <note>“Larger circle” refers to a lens with a larger radius of curvature, meaning it is flatter and less powerful.</note> at a certain distance from the concave lens makes the objects distinct and enlarges them.

*For according to Proposition 100, concave lenses made from too narrow a circle [too curved], if they are applied very close to the eye, return blurred images because of the excessive <term>divergence</term> <note>Divergence is the spreading out of light rays as they leave a lens.</note> of the rays. But according to Proposition 71, the radiations of a single point passing through a standalone convex lens placed before the eye within the center of convergence <note>The focal point where rays meet.</note> provide blurred vision because of <term>convergence</term> <note>Convergence is the coming together of light rays toward a single point.</note>.*

*And according to Proposition 104, that excess of divergence and this convergence cancel each other out when the lenses are combined into a tube <note>original: *tubū*; this is the basic arrangement of a Galilean telescope.</note>. Once the convergence is removed and the excessive divergence is corrected, distinct vision follows. However, the excessive divergence in any concave lens placed near the eye is removed by a specific distance of the convex lens from the eye. For when the convex lens is near the eye, the remedy for this excessive divergence (the convergence) exists in only a small amount. As in the diagram for Proposition 70, with the concave lens being at IG, the outer rays AI and HG, intercepting the portion of the concave lens IG, converge at a small angle IFG. Conversely, as the convex lens moves away from the eye, the remedy is present in a large amount. For example, if the concave lens with the eye were a little above F, the outer rays of a single point C would be AF and BF, intercepting the same portion of the concave lens at a larger angle AFB.*

*Moreover, a convex lens with a larger circle [radius of curvature] is required, because if the circle of convexity were equal to the circle of concavity—so that the convex side of one would sit within the hollow side of the other—and the rest of its convexity were almost parallel to the rest...*

<summary>Kepler explains the optical theory behind the Galilean telescope, showing how a convex objective lens corrects the blurring (divergence) caused by a concave eyepiece.</summary>
<keywords>telescope, concave lens, convex lens, divergence, convergence, refraction, optics, Johannes Kepler</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the proof of Proposition 107 from page 56, explaining why a flatter convex lens is necessary to correct the blurring caused by a highly curved concave lens in the early telescope design.</meta>

which, if joined immediately to the cavity of this lens, would almost correct each other and cancel out the other's effect. In such a case, the eye—suffering from blurred vision when looking at distant objects—would find little or no remedy in the excess of the other lens. However, once this continuous convex lens is separated from the concave one, the rays falling upon the concave lens would converge even more. Thus, the concave lens could no longer even make them parallel, let alone divergent. The same applies even more strongly to a convex lens of a smaller circle <note>“Smaller circle” refers to a lens with a tighter radius of curvature, making it more powerful and "thick."</note>. Therefore, the convexity of a larger circle <note>A flatter, less powerful lens.</note> remains the suitable choice.

Finally, I state that the appearance of visible objects is enlarged if the circle of convexity is larger. For according to Proposition 80, a standalone convex lens enlarges visible things. And although according to Proposition 96, a standalone concave lens diminishes visible things—and it is true that both the convex lens and the objects seen through it are larger if the convex lens is used alone than if a concave one is placed in between—nevertheless, according to Propositions 22 and 98, this enlargement and diminution is greater for more distant objects. Therefore, since the concave lens is near the eye, its diminution will be almost non-existent; and since the convex lens is further from the eye, its enlargement will be greater.

## 108. PROPOSITION.

If a convex lens is placed at any distance from the eye, any concave lens which would produce blurred vision if applied to the eye alone—and which is of a smaller circle <note>More curved.</note> than that used by the convex lens—will show distinct vision if placed at a certain distance and position between the eye and the convex lens.

This is, as it were, the converse of the previous proposition, but more flexible. For in the previous case, the position of the concave lens was fixed very close to the eye, and therefore...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition 108 regarding the arrangement of convex and concave lenses, specifically focusing on the placement of the eye in relation to the point where light rays meet.</meta>

...a single position of the convex lens could be chosen; here, however, a position for the convex lens is given that is not just one, but manifold in both quantity and quality, and in turn, the position of the concave lens can be chosen.

First, let us consider this specific arrangement of the convex lens: that the eye is placed within the **point of convergence** <note>The point where light rays meet after passing through a lens; the focal point.</note>. In this case, there is a closer relationship to the previous proposition and to the specific study of the **ocular tube** <term>tubus ocularis: an early term for the telescope</term>.

Therefore, in the next diagram (repeated from Proposition 70), the position of the concave lens and the eye will be between the convex lens **AB** and the points of convergence **D** and **F**. Let it be at **IG**. There will then be a certain measure of convergence for the rays **AI** and **HG** at the angle **IFG**. To ensure that this convergence does not hinder distinct vision, it must either be removed entirely—for the eye of a **farsighted person** <note>original Greek: πρεσβύτου (presbytou)</note>—so that the rays become parallel, or even further, a divergence must be introduced for the eye of a **nearsighted person** <note>original Greek: μύωπος (myopos)</note>. Yet, according to Proposition 104, both can be achieved by a concave lens placed at some point before the points of convergence. It is demonstrated (as in Proposition 107) that this lens must be "hollowed from a smaller circle" <note>Meaning a lens with a tighter radius of curvature, making it more powerful or "stronger."</note> than the one used for the convex lens. Furthermore, a concave lens applied alone to the eye must produce blurred vision. This is because whatever cures the blur caused by a convex lens must also be capable of producing blur from the opposite cause.

Second, let this be the arrangement: that the eye is placed *outside* the points of convergence, as if in the next diagram (repeated from Propositions 70 and 75) it were at **OP**, beyond **D** and **F**. In that case, a concave lens applied within the point of convergence **D** or **F** could, according to Proposition 104, ensure that no convergence occurs, but rather that the rays diverge again, thus reaching the eye at **OP**. Nevertheless, many conditions are required in this case. First, the concave lens must be of a "small circle" <note>Highly curved.</note>. For if it were a "large circle" <note>Flatter, with less curvature.</note> concave lens, all the rays between **AD** and **BD** would only strike a small portion of it near the center; therefore, the effect of the **refraction** <note>The bending of light as it passes through the lens.</note> would be slight—not enough to cancel out the convergence. This requirement is common to both this case and the previous one. Furthermore, if the lens is of a small circle so that it can induce divergence, it still does not send all those diverging rays to the eye...

<meta>catchword: far.</meta>

<summary>Kepler explains how concave and convex lenses must be paired to correct vision, noting that the concave lens must be more strongly curved than the convex one to properly direct light to the eye.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Proposition 108, specifically examining how the distance of the eye from the focal points (points of convergence) affects what is seen through a combination of convex and concave lenses. ...placed far outside the **points of convergence** <note>Also known as focal points; the locations where light rays meet after passing through a lens.</note> **D** and **F**. For if the rays diverge, they scatter in every direction away from an eye placed at a distance. Therefore, only a very few rays are left to be transmitted through the very **center** <note>original: *umbilicum*, literally "navel," referring to the optical center of the lens</note> of the convex lens (or some other point relative to the position of the concave lens). These rays are transmitted through a very narrow portion to the very bottom **D** of the concave lens almost perpendicularly; because they have almost no divergence, they can be regarded as parallel rays. For this reason, they serve only **the farsighted** <note>original Greek: τοῖς πρεσβύταις (tois presbytais). Kepler refers to elderly individuals whose eyes require parallel or converging light to focus.</note>. Thirdly, this arrangement transmits only a tiny particle of the visible object to the eye, because of the distance of the eye **O P** both from the convex lens **A B** (as stated) and from the concave lens to be placed above **D** or **F** according to Proposition 97, and furthermore because whatever is seen is viewed at a very small angle according to Proposition 98.

### CIX. PROPOSITION.

In instruments that exhibit visible objects as larger and more distinct, no concave lens is very far from the points of convergence situated after the convex lens.

For if the lenses are to represent objects as large as possible, the concave lens must be very near the eye, according to Proposition 98. The convex lens, however, must be far from the eye, according to Proposition 22. Consequently, it must also be far from the concave lens; and yet, the location of the concave lens, according to Proposition 104, is between the convex lens and its point of convergence. Therefore, if the convex lens is far removed from the concave lens, the point of convergence will be very close to the concave lens.

### CX. PROPOSITION.

Given a convex lens, of those concave lenses applied very close to the eye, the one that is **hollowed from a smaller circle** <note>A lens with a smaller radius of curvature, making it more "powerful" or more deeply curved.</note> is located further from the convex lens and must be applied closer to the point of convergence.

Let portion **D** of the convex lens **AB** transmit rays flowing from the same point, and let the convergence be at **C**.

<summary>Kepler explains that placing the eye too far from the focal points results in a tiny, dim field of view suitable only for the farsighted. He then establishes the geometry of the telescope: for maximum magnification, the concave eyepiece must be close to the eye and the focal point of the objective lens.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof regarding the placement of lenses in an early telescope system. It follows from the previous discussion on how rays from a convex lens must be intercepted by a concave lens to provide a clear image to the eye.</meta>

Since, therefore, the **convergence of rays** <note>The way light rays move toward each other to meet at a single point.</note> **D C** and **E C** remains the same through a single **convex lens** **A B** (or through its single and identical portion **D E**), one and the same remedy must be applied—specifically, divergence provided by **concave lenses**. But **refraction** <note>The bending of light as it passes through a lens.</note> causes this divergence; indeed, the refraction of the same ray—such as **D C**—can only be identical in similar portions of unequal concave lenses. Let there be, therefore, concave lenses **F G** and **H I**. And since the portions **F G** and **H I** are similar (each according to the concavity of its own lens), they must be intercepted by the same rays **D C** and **E C**. Therefore, just as the portion **F G** of the larger concavity relates to the similar portion **H I** of the smaller concavity, so also the distance **F C** of the former from the **point of convergence** <note>original: *concursu*; the location where rays meet</note> is greater than the smaller distance **H C**. If, then, **H I** is less distant from **C** than **F G**, the same lens **H I** will be farther from **D E** than the lens **F G**, which is hollowed out from a larger circle.

### CXI. PROPOSITION.

One and the same concave lens, when applied closely to the eye, must be placed at an equal distance from the **points of convergence** of all those convex lenses if it is to show distinct images through them.

*For a single concave lens provides only one remedy; therefore, it corrects only a single convergence of rays. However, at a fixed distance of the concave lens from the points of convergence of any convex lenses, the convergence of the rays received by that same concave lens remains the same. For even if one of the convex lenses were much wider and its outermost rays converged more sharply, those rays would either miss the concave lens entirely or miss that specific portion of the lens from which refracted rays are able to reach the **pupil of the eye**.*

### CXII. PROPOSITION.

**convex lens**, **convergence of rays**, **concave lenses**, **refraction**, **point of convergence**, **pupil of the eye**

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof regarding the placement and effects of concave and convex lenses in an early telescope system. It builds on the idea that the eye and the concave lens (the eyepiece) must be positioned relative to the point where light rays from the convex lens (the objective) would normally meet.</meta>

When a **concave lens** <note>A lens that curves inward, like a bowl, causing light rays to spread out.</note> is placed near the eye, **convex lenses** <note>Lenses that curve outward, like a magnifying glass, causing light rays to come together.</note> ground from a large circle require a long distance from the concave lens and the eye, while those from a small circle require a short distance.

*For according to Proposition 109, the eye is near the **point of convergence** <note>original: *punctum concursus*; the spot where light rays meet after passing through a lens.</note>, and according to Proposition 111, a concave lens of the same kind remains at the same distance from the points of convergence of all convex lenses. However, the points of convergence are at unequal distances from their respective convex lenses: they are far from lenses ground from a large circle, and less far from those ground from a small circle, according to Proposition 39 <note>A larger circle implies a flatter curve and a longer focal length; a smaller circle implies a steeper curve and a shorter focal length.</note>. Since equals subtracted from unequals leave unequal remainders, and the interval between the concave and convex lenses—given the distance from the point of convergence to the concave lens is the same in every case—is less than the interval between the convex lens and the point of convergence: the concave lens (along with the eye) will therefore be further from a convex lens of a larger circle than from one of a smaller circle.*

### CXIII. PROPOSITION.

Given a specific convex lens, a concave lens of a smaller circle represents visible objects as larger, while one of a larger circle represents them as smaller.

<image-desc>An optical ray diagram on the right side of the page. It features two vertical diagrams of light rays passing through lenses. The right diagram shows rays passing through a convex lens (A B) and then through a concave lens (G H) toward a point (C). The labels A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I, K are used to designate specific points and lens boundaries.</image-desc>

*For according to Proposition 110, concave lenses of a small circle, along with the eye to which they are closely joined, must move further back from the convex lens to achieve **distinct vision** <note>A clear, focused image.</note>. But according to Proposition 22, the further the eye moves back from the convex lens toward the point of convergence, the larger the objects it sees. Therefore, the eye, using a concave lens of a smaller circle, sees objects clearly and larger than it does with a concave lens of a larger circle.*

A concave lens, even when moved only a very short distance further away from the convex lens, greatly increases the size of visible objects.

*For according to Proposition 109, in the following diagram, **G H** and **I K** are very close to the points **C** and **F**. And indeed, in crossing such a short distance as **I F**, with the eye **I K** positioned at **F**, the quantity of a single point, from which all rays in **D E**...*

*...flow in, it increases to a certain magnitude, indeed equal to the entire lens D E, so that a point appears at the angle D F E, which is an infinite multiplication. Thus, by a slight movement, a great result is achieved.*

## CXV. PROPOSITION.

*If a concave lens <note>A lens that curves inward, like a bowl, used here as the eyepiece.</note> is placed near the eye, then among convex lenses <note>Lenses that curve outward, like a magnifying glass, used as the objective lens.</note>, the one formed from a smaller circle represents visible objects as smaller, while the one from a larger circle represents them as larger.*

*Let the convex lens be A B, with a larger radius <note>original: *semidiametro*; literally half-diameter.</note> A C, and lens D E with a smaller radius D F. Therefore, C and F will be the points of convergence <note>original: *puncta concursus*; the focal points where the light rays would meet.</note> according to Proposition 39. Let there be one concave lens, either G H or I K, provided for both cases, which, when placed very near the eye, causes a certain divergence of the parallel rays by its specific shape. Since the concave lens G H and I K is one and the same, it will be at the same position relative to the points of convergence C and F according to Proposition 111. The eye is indeed very near the lens in both cases, as assumed. Therefore, when the equal segments G C and I F are subtracted from the unequal segments A C and D F, the remainders A G and D I will exist in a larger proportion. Thus, the convex lens A B is removed further in its proportion from the concave lens G H and the eye than the convex lens D E is from the concave lens I K and the eye in its proportion. And G H (with the eye) is closer to point C in the proportion of A B C than I K (with the eye) is to point F in the proportion of D E F. Therefore, larger visible objects are represented by the combination of A B and G H than by D E and I K, according to Proposition 93. And indeed, by the slightest alteration of the proportion, they become much larger, according to Proposition 114.*

*This primary Proposition was most obscure, and for this reason: if the proportion of A C to C G had been the same as that of D F to F I, then the longer distance A G would have done nothing to increase the size of visible objects. For all things would have been equal on both sides according to Proposition 83.*

## CXVI. PROBLEM.

**To represent visible objects as large as one wishes.**

*For it is clear through Proposition 113 and 115 that when the proportion of the circles of the cavity <note>original: *cavitatis*; referring to the curvature of the concave lens eyepiece.</note> and the convexity <note>original: *convexitatis*; referring to the curvature of the convex objective lens.</note> is increased, the visible objects are increased in size.*

## CXVII. PROBLEM.

To represent visible objects with an equal increase in magnitude using unequal distances between lenses—that is, using tubes <note>original: *tubis*; the housings for the lenses in an early telescope.</note> of unequal lengths.

According to Propositions 113 and 115, ensure that there is the same proportion both between the cavities themselves and between the convexities, and also between the distances of the lenses, even if the convex lenses themselves are dissimilar to one another.

## CXIIX. PROBLEM.

To represent larger images with shorter tubes.

*If, while the convex lens is smaller, there is a greater proportion between the convexity and the cavity than in the longer instrument, larger objects will be represented by the shorter instrument, according to Propositions 113 and 115.*

## CXIX. PROPOSITION.

Given a concave lens, visible objects are represented more clearly or strongly <note>original: *fortius*; referring to the intensity or brightness of the light.</note> with a larger or wider convex lens than with a smaller one.

*For more light is scattered from a single point (in the nearby diagram) through the larger aperture <note>original: *amplitudinem*</note> A B than through the smaller aperture D E. Indeed, all that light is collected into a single point, C or F. Therefore, the image <note>original: *pictura*; literally "the picture" formed by the light.</note> is stronger in C than in F, and the eye at G H receives more dense <note>original: *stipatiores*; literally "more crowded."</note> rays than at I K.*

*Understand the "size" of the convex lens here according to Proposition 30 as referring to the physical body of the lens, not to its geometric figure <note>original: *figurâ*; i.e., its diameter/width rather than its curvature.</note>.*

## CXX. PROPOSITION.

Given a convex lens, visible objects are represented more clearly or strongly through a concave lens of a larger circle <note>A larger circle implies a flatter curve or a larger radius of curvature.</note> than through a concave lens of a smaller circle.
