<meta>This page continues the discussion from Proposition 119, which argued that larger convex lenses provide more light. This page (Proposition 120-122) qualifies that rule, explaining how the size of the lens aperture affects image clarity and why the center of the view is sharper than the edges.</meta>

For a small lens placed very close to the eye causes a proper divergence of rays through only a small portion of itself. Even if many rays from a single point fall upon it—having radiated from a large portion of the convex lens—most of them stray away from the eye because of the excessive refraction caused by the sides or the rim of the concave lens (as shown in the diagram for Proposition 100, where FG represents the width of the pupil <note>original: *pupillæ*</note> relative to HI). Indeed, only a few radiations enter the eye, and only those very close to the perpendicular line; thus, they arrive from only a small portion of the convex lens. Therefore, according to Proposition 119, vision through a lens with a small circular cavity is weak. The same result occurs if the portion of the lens being used is narrower than the pupil, even if that lens belongs to a large circle.

## PROPOSITION 121.

> When viewing a portion of a hemisphere through lenses, the central part closest to the perpendicular line is seen more clearly and strongly than the surrounding edge <note>original: *limbus*</note>.

The reason for this regarding the eye is clear in the diagram for Proposition 70, in which the width of the pupil is marked Q G. For the eye placed at Q G—whether naked or with a concave lens held before it—intercepts all the radiations of the central point E between lines E A Q and E B G. However, it does not receive all the rays from point C, but only a part of the pencil <note>original: *penicilli*; a "pencil" of light refers to a bundle of rays originating from a single point.</note> of rays C A F B C; specifically, only that which is between C A and I C H G. The light that is currently between C H G and C B F misses the pupil Q G. Therefore, since E is seen through the span of A B, but C is seen only through A H, then by Proposition 119, E will be seen more strongly and clearly than C.

## PROPOSITION 122.

> When using a narrow portion of a convex lens, all other things being equal, visible objects are represented more distinctly; when using a wide portion, they appear more blurred <note>original: *confusiora*</note>.

For those rays which radiate into the eye through a large portion of the convexity do so more strongly, according to Proposition 119. By this very strength, the colors of the Rainbow <note>original: *Iridis*; Kepler is describing chromatic aberration, where light breaking through the edges of a lens creates a "rainbow" fringe that blurs the image.</note> are first provoked, followed by a mistiness. For the hollow and net-like tunic <term>tunica retiformis: the retina, described here as "net-like" based on its anatomical appearance</term> of the eye is full of spirit <note>original: *spiritu*; in 17th-century physiology, "animal spirits" were believed to be the fluid medium that transmitted sensory data from the nerves to the brain.</note>, and although it is touched only at a single point, if that point is excessively bright due to the convergence of too many rays, the spirits in...

<summary>Kepler explains that while larger lenses collect more light, they also cause blurring and color fringes (chromatic aberration) at the edges. He notes that the center of the visual field is always sharper because the eye intercepts a more complete bundle of rays from the center than from the periphery.</summary>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of how lens apertures and instrument design affect the quality of vision. It concludes Proposition 121 and addresses Proposition 122 regarding the use of diaphragms to reduce glare, then moves to Problem 123 concerning the field of view.</meta>

...over some width of the retina <note>original: *retiformis*; the "net-like" tunic of the eye</note>, the area around this point is imbued with the contagion of the penetrating sensation <note>original: *passionis penetrantis*; referring here to the sensory impression of light</note>; see Proposition 61. Therefore, for the convenience of the eye, the instrument, and the light—whether by day or night—the convex lens is either widened and uncovered, or narrowed and covered. This is done either directly, or at an intermediate location between the lenses, by using a pierced diaphragm <note>original: *diaphragmate*; a physical divider with a hole to limit light</note>, or by bending and narrowing the neck of the instrument inward, or by extending the tube beyond the convex lens so that its cylindrical opening, being further away, is seen at a smaller angle (per Proposition 67) and acts just as effectively as something narrower. Nature has already demonstrated this through the widening of the opening of the uvea <note>original: *uveæ*; here referring to the pupil within the iris</note> for night light and its contraction for daylight.

The diaphragm also serves this purpose: to create darkness within. This is also aided by a black color applied to the interior, and by the shape of a curved horn <note>original: *litui*; a "lituus" is a staff or trumpet shape that flares outward</note>, which curves outward in its progression but has sides curving inward in the middle, lest the rays entering near the convex lens should be reflected back and forth and create a glare.

The extension of the tube far beyond the convex lens serves the same purpose: to prevent the convex lens from being struck by light from the side parts of the hemisphere <note>the "hemisphere" refers to the entire field of view in front of the lens</note>.

## PROBLEM 123.

->To see a visible object on high, in the depths,<-
->to the right, to the left, or wherever you wish.<-

This is achieved if the diameter of the concave lens <note>the eyepiece</note> is wider than the pupil of the eye and large enough so that the eye can move a sufficient distance from the center of the lens toward the sides.

For the pencils of light <term>penicelli; bundles of light rays radiating from a single point</term> at the sides of the lens are refracted entirely and obliquely: to the left on the left side, and to the right on the right side. For example, in the diagram for Proposition 100 <note>original: "prop. C."; Kepler uses Roman numerals here</note>, let ABKF be the median line of a single pencil of light coming into the center of the pupil; it is refracted outward to the left through points BK, because BK is the left part of the lens. Therefore, when the eye is moved from the center of the cavity to the left side K, the point A, seen through the straight line FKM, will be perceived to be in position M, which is further to the right, according to Proposition 19.

## CXXIV. PROBLEM.

### To skillfully estimate the size of an enlarged image.

Let the left eye be directed freely at the visible object without an instrument; but let the right eye look at the same object through the lenses. Since the left eye is directed at the object, the right eye will always naturally remain parallel to the left—even if it is covered or guided by the instrument—because the parallel direction of the eyes is natural according to Proposition 57. Therefore, the right eye will be directed as if toward the object itself, whether the image <term>species; the visual representation or "form" of the object perceived by the eye</term> produced by the instrument appears lower or higher than the actual thing seen through the left eye. For according to Proposition 62, the right eye will indeed see the enlarged image of that thing toward which it is directed through the natural association with the left eye, but it will not necessarily see it with the same part of its surface <note>meaning the specific area of the retina</note> as that with which the left eye sees it.

When, therefore, the images appear to differ in position, by moving the convex lens of the instrument back and forth, and sometimes by applying the concave lens to the eye in a slightly different way, it will easily be brought about that both visible images of the same thing coincide in position. Then, the excess of one over the other will become apparent when the two images are superimposed upon one another <note>This is a classic method for measuring telescope magnification: looking through the telescope with one eye while the other eye looks at the object directly, then comparing the two "layers" of vision</note>.

->Thus far regarding the simple instrument; the *krypsis* <note>original: "κρύψις"; a Greek term meaning concealment or a hidden conclusion, here likely referring to the internal logic or summary of the preceding section</note> follows.<-

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of lens placement in a telescope. Having previously discussed how to estimate image size, Kepler now transitions to the geometry of combining multiple convex lenses to shorten the instrument's focal length.</meta>

When a concave lens is used, two similar convex lenses placed very close together act as one and almost halve the length of the instrument compared to one having only one of those convex lenses; and at the same time, they reduce the size of the image <term>species; the visual representation or "form" of the object perceived by the eye</term>.

<image-desc>A technical diagram on the right side of the page illustrates optical principles with two vertical axes. On the left axis, two convex lenses (labeled AB and CD) are shown in profile as thin ovals, with light rays passing through them and converging at points labeled I, N, and H along the central axis. On the right axis, a single thicker lens (EF) is shown with rays converging at points L, M, G, P, and K. Geometric labels correspond to variables in the text.</image-desc>

*Let there be two similar convex lenses, AB and CD, and let the center of the circle of [lens] ARB be H. Let the radius <term>semidiameter; the distance from the center of a circle to its edge</term> HR be bisected at I. Therefore, if only the convex lens AB is positioned, the point of convergence <term>concursus; the point where light rays meet after being refracted</term> will be near H, according to Proposition 39. And for that reason, the concave lens must be placed not far inside H, according to Proposition 109.*

*I say that when CD is applied very close to AB, the concave lens must be applied within point I. I shall first prove this by a "rougher wisdom" <note>original: "rudi Minerva"; a Latin idiom meaning a simple, practical, or unrefined explanation rather than a rigorous mathematical proof</note>.*

*For because the parallel rays refracted in AB converge at H due to refraction; when they are intercepted by CD, and thus having undergone refraction in CD a second time, they will converge closer. For in CD they undergo a greater refraction than in AB, because they strike it more obliquely—indeed, they strike AB as parallel rays, but they strike CD while already converging. It is clear from this that the convergence of the rays will occur much closer, and therefore the concave lens must move closer to the convex lenses AB and CD, according to Proposition 109. Moreover, it is clear that the concave lens must be placed within point I (which halves the radius HR of the convexity AB) for this reason: Let GL be equal to the half-length HI, and let a lens EF be made with this circular radius, having convexities ELF and EMF, and let GK be equal to LG. Therefore, by Proposition 79, if there were only a single surface EMF, it would be as effective as the two surfaces of AB, making parallel rays converge at K, which is the same distance from EF as the convergence point H is from AB. Yet the lens EF has two such surfaces. And just as the surface EMF encompasses both convexities of AB, so the other surface ELF encompasses both convexities in CD...*

<meta>catchword: because</meta>

<summary>Kepler demonstrates through geometry that doubling the convex lenses in a telescope's objective significantly shortens the focal length, requiring the concave eyepiece to be moved closer to the objective. <meta>This page continues the geometric proof regarding the combination of lenses. Kepler is demonstrating how two convex lenses placed together shorten the focal length and affect the magnification of the image, continuing from the "rough wisdom" explanation started on the previous page.</meta>

Indeed, AB and CD are similar, just as ELF and EMF are. But lens EF, being convex on both sides, causes parallel rays to converge at G. This occurs from the center according to Proposition 39 <note>Kepler refers to his own previously established optical laws</note>. This is at the distance LG, which is half of the radius <term>semidiameter; the distance from the center of a circle to its edge</term> of AB. Therefore, the lenses AB and CD, when joined and touching, force parallel rays to meet at half the distance of this radius—that is, around point I. However, a concave lens, according to Proposition 109, must be placed inside the point of convergence <term>concursus; the point where light rays meet after being refracted</term>, and therefore it must be placed inside point I.

I also state that the image <term>species; the optical form or appearance of the object</term> becomes smaller when using two contiguous convex lenses AB and CD than when using only one, AB. 

For because a single concave lens is the same on both sides, it will therefore cause the same divergence of rays. It will therefore be separated by the same interval from the convergence point H (produced by the single lens AB) as from the convergence point I (produced by both AB and CD combined): according to Proposition 111. Let this distance be HN, IO, or GP. Yet this same segment has a greater proportion to the half-length IR than it does to the double-length HR. Therefore, the joined lenses AB and CD are closer to point O (just as the equivalent lens EF is closer to point P in proportion to its radius LG) than the single lens AB is to point N in proportion to its radius RH. Consequently, EF represents smaller visible objects through the concave lens P than AB alone does through the same concave lens N, according to Proposition 83; therefore, the two joined lenses AB and CD likewise produce a smaller image than a single lens AB.

### PROPOSITION 126.

A single concave surface of a small circle is almost equivalent in dissipating or scattering rays to two concave surfaces taken from a circle twice as large. 
   This is proven from Propositions 79 and 3.

### PROPOSITION 127.

Two concave lenses placed against one another must be situated slightly further from the convex lens than a single one would be to produce clear vision; however, they significantly increase the size of the visible image, nearly doubling it. 
   For the parallel rays, which the convex lens has caused to converge, in the process of converging...

<meta>This page continues the geometric discussion of lens combinations, specifically focusing on how adding a second concave lens near the eye requires a greater distance from the convex objective lens and how this affects the magnification and clarity of the image.</meta>

*...rays falling into the concave lens, having passed through it and avoiding a point of convergence, diverge again toward the eye according to Proposition 107. For the telescope instrument is assumed, and within it, a useful placement for the concave lens. Now, however, if a second concave lens is placed between the eye and the first concave lens to intercept the diverging rays, it causes them, once they have passed through, to diverge even further according to Proposition 94. These rays therefore fail by an excess of divergence and produce a confused image according to Propositions 95 and 99.*

*It will therefore be necessary to increase the opposing convergence from the convex lens so that these defects balance out and cancel one another, according to Proposition 104. This convergence—and the resulting confusion—is increased if the convex lens is moved further away from the eye when positioned within the point of convergence <term>concursus; the focal point where light rays meet</term>, according to Proposition 71. Therefore, two concave lenses, with the eye held very close to them, must be further away from the convex lens than a single concave lens would be. Or, according to Proposition 126, two concave lenses are equivalent to a single lens of a larger circle <note>Kepler means a lens with a shallower curve or larger radius of curvature</note>, while a single lens corresponds to a smaller circle. But according to Proposition 110, a concave lens of a small circle is further from the [convex] lens than a single concave lens of a large circle. Therefore, two concave lenses of a large circle are further apart than just one of them alone.*

*I also state that visible objects are represented as larger through two concave lenses than through one held close to the eye. This is demonstrated (as with the previous points) from Propositions 113 and 126.*

*Indeed, even a very small increase in distance creates a large addition to the size of the image <term>species; the optical appearance or form of the object</term> according to Proposition 114.*

### PROPOSITION 128.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram of light rays passing through a meniscus lens (convex-concave). A vertical central axis is labeled with points G (top), C and F (on the lens surfaces), and A and D (bottom). A light ray originates at point G, enters the first curved surface at point B, passes through the lens body, exits at point E on the second curved surface, and continues downward to point H. The lens is depicted with two parallel curved arcs.</image-desc>

*In a lens that is convex on one side and concave on the other with equal circles <note>meaning the radii of curvature for both sides are the same, creating a "meniscus" lens of uniform thickness</note>, all rays that travel parallel to the perpendicular axis within the body of the lens are refracted at equal angles on both surfaces. Once refracted, they retain the same divergence or parallelism that they had before.*

<summary>Kepler explains that adding a second concave lens to a telescope system increases ray divergence, requiring the convex lens to be moved further away to maintain focus, which in turn increases magnification. He also introduces the properties of a meniscus lens with equal curvatures.</summary>

<keywords>refraction, concave lens, convex lens, magnification, optical divergence, focal point, meniscus lens, Kepler's laws of optics</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues Johannes Kepler’s mathematical exploration of dioptrics, specifically analyzing how light behaves when passing through a "meniscus" lens—one that is convex on one side and concave on the other—and how the distance of the light source changes whether the light rays eventually meet or spread apart.</meta>

Let there be a lens formed by a circle $B C$, whose center is $A$, which is convex; and by a circle $E F$, whose center is $D$, which is concave. Let the straight line $D A$ pass through these centers, cutting the surfaces perpendicularly at $F$ and $C$ <note>This line represents the optical axis of the lens</note>. Let any line $B E$ be drawn parallel to it, cutting the surfaces perpendicularly. It is demonstrated geometrically, especially by Ptolemy and other astronomers, that just as $C F$ and $B E$ are equal, so too are $C B$ and $F E$. Consequently, the inclination of $B E$ to each surface is the same—that is, it is the same relative to the tangents of the surface at the points of incidence $B$ and $E$. For these tangents are parallel. Because of this, the refraction will also be the same, and the rays refracted out of the dense body <note>meaning the glass of the lens</note> into the air on either side will be parallel, such as $B G$ and $E H$. Therefore, the exiting rays $E H$ have the same divergence or convergence as the entering rays $G B$, provided that $B E$ and $C F$ remain parallel within the body of the lens.

## PROPOSITION CXXIX.

If the rays from a single point fall upon a lens that is simultaneously convex and concave and formed by the same circle: if the point is far away, the rays will converge after passing through the lens; if the point is closer than the diameter of the circle, the rays will diverge more than they did at their origin.

For according to Proposition 23, the rays from a distant point are parallel. According to Proposition 34, parallel rays falling upon a denser convex surface converge within that dense body.

Let $G$ be a distant point, and let $G B$ and $G C$ be parallel rays, and let $B E$ and $C F$ converge. Therefore, the distance $E F$ will be shorter than $B C$. Because of this, the incidence of the ray $B E$ at point $E$ is more direct <note>closer to the perpendicular</note> than it was at point $B$. Therefore, the refraction at $E$ is less than the refraction at $B$. Consequently, the angle $G B E$ is smaller than the angle $B E H$. Thus, $G B$ and $E H$ are not parallel. But $G B$ and $G C$ are assumed to be parallel. Therefore, the refracted rays $E H$ and $F A$ converge and will eventually meet.

Conversely, let $G$ be a radiating point located closer than the diameter of the circle. The rays $G B$ and $G C$ will therefore be diverging. Having entered the denser convex medium in this manner, they will diverge less, but they will still diverge, according to Proposition 37.

<meta>The catchword "Cum" indicates the start of the next page's text.</meta>

<summary>Kepler explains the geometric properties of a meniscus lens, demonstrating how the distance of a light source determines whether the light rays will converge to a point or diverge further after passing through the glass.</summary>
<keywords>meniscus lens, refraction, convergence, divergence, Ptolemy, optical axis, dense medium, geometry of light</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric proof regarding how light rays behave when passing through a "meniscus" lens—a lens with one convex side and one concave side. The previous page established that when the two surfaces are part of the same circle, the rays eventually exit at the same angle they entered. Here, Kepler explores what happens when the circles forming the two sides have different diameters.</meta>

*Since, therefore, the rays $B E$ and $C F$ diverge toward the concave boundary $E F$ of the dense body <note>original: "densiterminum," the surface of the glass</note>, $E F$ will be larger than $B C$. The angle of incidence of $B E$ at point $E$ is more oblique than it was at point $B$; therefore, the refraction is greater at the exit than it was at the entry. Thus, the angle $G B E$ is larger, and $B E H$ is smaller. Consequently, $G B$ and $E H$ are not parallel, but would eventually meet if they were extended toward $H$. The refracted rays $E H$ and $F A$ therefore diverge from each other more than the original rays $G B$ and $G C$.*

## PROPOSITION CXXX.

If the concavity is formed from a larger circle than the convexity, the rays from a distant point will converge after passing through the lens. Indeed, they converge more (or after a shorter distance than if there were only a convex surface) if the circle of the concavity is more than three times the size of the circle of the convexity; they converge less, however (and after a greater distance), if the circle is less than three times the size.

->Or<-

A concavity of a larger circle, though it detracts from the convexity of a smaller circle, still produces the effect of a very large convex lens. Let this be called a **Meniscus** <note>From the Greek 'meniskos' meaning 'crescent'</note>. It is equivalent to a purely convex lens.

<image-desc>A vertical geometric diagram on the right side of the page illustrating the refraction of light rays through a meniscus lens. A point source G at the top emits two rays that enter the lens at points B and C. The rays pass through the body of the lens to points E and F on the second surface. Upon exiting, the rays converge towards a central axis, passing through or towards points labeled A, P, H, and R. The lens itself is depicted with a concave top surface and a convex bottom surface.</image-desc>

*Let $C F$ and $B E$ be the rays refracted within the body, having arrived from a distant point $G$. They will therefore converge toward $E F$ according to Proposition XXXIV; thus $E F$ will be smaller than $B C$. But at the same time, the circle of that surface is larger. Therefore, $B E$ will strike the surface more directly <note>original: "rectius," meaning closer to the perpendicular</note> at $E$ than it did at $B$. Consequently, there is less refraction at $E$ than at $B$. The angle $B E H$ is therefore larger than $E B G$. Thus, $H E$ and $B G$ are not parallel to each other, but if they were extended toward $G$, they would meet; and so $E H$ and $F H$ converge toward one another, toward point $H$.*

Sit <note>Catchword: Let [there be]</note>

<summary>Kepler defines the 'meniscus' lens (convex-concave) and proves that if the concave side has a larger radius than the convex side, the lens still acts like a convex lens, causing light rays to converge.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof from Proposition 130, detailing how the specific curvature of a meniscus lens (one convex side, one concave side) shifts the point where light rays meet, known as the focal point. It establishes the mathematical relationship between the radii of the two surfaces and the resulting focus.</meta>

*Let A now be the center of Circle B C, and let the distance C H be triple the length of the radius C A. And let point R be located below H. If the convex surface B C were acting alone, the rays B E and C F would converge at point H, according to Proposition 34 <note>Kepler refers to an earlier rule in this work establishing that for a single convex glass surface, the focal point is three radii distant</note>. Now, let R be the center of the second, concave circle E F. When the perpendicular line E R is drawn, the ray B E will be refracted at point E away from that perpendicular E R, according to Proposition 2; it will then meet the central axis C H above H—let us call this meeting point P. Therefore, the rays E P and F P converge more sharply than B E and C F did: and the distance to this meeting point, C P, is shorter than the distance C H.*

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating a cross-section of a lens and its vertical optical axis. The lens is labeled with points A (left), C (right), B (top), and H (bottom). Internal points D and G are marked on the axis within the lens. The vertical axis extends downwards through points E, S, F, ending in a mark labeled T.</image-desc>

*Conversely, let the center of the circle E F be located above H—for instance, at point P. When the perpendicular line E P is drawn, the ray B E will be refracted at point E further away from the perpendicular than the path E H would have taken. According to Proposition 2, this refracted ray will meet the other ray F H at a point below H—let us say they meet at R. Therefore, the convergence of E R and F R will be less than that of B E and C F. Because the distance C R is greater than C H, the meeting point R will be moved further away. But if the center of circle E F is placed exactly at H (which is one and a half diameters <note>original: "sesquidiametro"</note> below C), then the meeting point also occurs at H; in this specific case, the second surface E F neither helps nor hinders the effect of the first surface B C.*

### PROPOSITION CXXXI. PROBLEM.

#### To find the meeting point for a Meniscus lens. Or: the meeting point is lengthened by the same amount that the lens is thinned.

*Let A B C D be a **Meniscus** <note>A lens with one convex and one concave side, shaped like a crescent moon</note>, and let E and F be the centers of its two surfaces. If the convex side A B C were the only surface causing refraction, the rays would meet at a distance of three semidiameters <term>semidiameter: the radius of the circle forming the lens surface</term> of B E, according to Proposition 34. However, the lens acts as if only the first surface is present if the circle of the concavity A D C is three times larger than the circle of the convexity A B C; that is, if the radius B F is triple the length of B E. Since the meeting point occurs after three semidiameters of B E, the meeting point would therefore be—*

<meta>This page continues the geometric demonstration from the previous page, specifically focusing on how the thickness of a lens and the specific radii of its surfaces determine the focal point (concursus). The previous discussion established that changing the center of the lens's second surface shifts the point where light rays meet. Here, Kepler compares four specific lens configurations to derive a general rule of proportion.</meta>

...given that F is the center of the AD Circle: since all rays passing through the body ABC would fall perpendicularly upon the surface ADC, they would therefore not be refracted <note>original: "refringerentur"; light rays do not bend when they enter a surface at a perfect 90-degree angle</note>. Thus, for the lens ABCD, the meeting point <note>Kepler uses "concursus" for what we now call the focal point</note> is at a distance of three radii <note>original: "semidiametros"</note>.

Secondly, when the lens is equally convex on both sides, such as ABC and AHC, the meeting point is one radius BE away from B, at point E, according to Proposition 39.

Thirdly, when the lens is AGCH—being flat on the side AGC—parallel rays in AGC are not refracted at all and meet after two radii, as shown in Proposition 35, at point S.

Fourthly, by Proposition 125, if two such lenses were joined together, the meeting point would be at a distance of half of that same EB.

From these clues <note>original: "vestigijs"</note>, it therefore appears that the distance of the meeting point from the lens increases in almost the same proportion as the thickness of the lens BD is diminished. For when the thickness was twice BH, the distance was half of BE. When the thickness was exactly BH, the distance was exactly BE. When the thickness was half (namely GH), the distance was twice BE (namely ES). Now, since a little less than a third part is removed from GH or BG, a third radius SF is added to the two radii BE and ES.

That DG is less than a third part of GB (or GH) is proved as follows. Let AB be either 30° or 0° 30'. By Proposition 7, with a small margin of error, it will be:

| *there remains* | *919,629* | *or* | *258.* |
| *with a fourth of this* | *229,907* | *or* | *64.* |
| *subtracted, there remains* | *689,722.* | *or* | *192.* |
| *which is nearly half of BG itself.* | | | |

*Therefore, in nearly the same proportion as the lens is made thinner, the meeting point is lengthened.*

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating optical refraction through a concavo-convex lens. A vertical axis contains points G (top), A, and B. A lens is depicted with a convex upper surface C-D and a concave lower surface E-F. Two rays originate from point G, strike the first surface at C and D, pass through the lens body to points E and F on the second surface, and then refract outwards towards labels H at the bottom. The labels C, E, and D, F mark the points of entry and exit for the rays.</image-desc>

## PROPOSITION CXXXII.

If the concavity <note>original: "cavitas"; the hollow or inward-curving side of a lens</note> is formed from a smaller circle than the convexity; the rays from a single point placed at the distance of one diameter behind the convex surface will diverge <note>spread apart</note> further after passing through the lens. Or: a convexity of a larger circle, when detracting from a concavity of a smaller circle, produces the effect of a very large concave circle.

*For the rays CE and DF coming from point G inside the body [of the lens], if that point is at a diameter's distance from the convex side, they are parallel according to Proposition 35. Therefore, they strike the concave surface EF more obliquely than they did the convex surface CD. The rest follows as in Proposition 129. If, however, G were closer: then CE and DF would diverge inside the body toward EF according to Proposition 37; and they would be refracted even more into the air at EH and FB according to Proposition 92.*

<sig>HH</sig>

<summary>Kepler concludes that thinning a lens lengthens its focal point and proves that a concavo-convex lens acts as a diverging lens if its concave side has a sharper curve (smaller radius) than its convex side.</summary>

# PROPOSITION CXXXIII.

If the cavity <note>original: "cavitas"; the concave side</note> of a lens that is convex on one surface has its center located further inward <note>meaning the center of curvature is closer to the lens itself</note> than the center of the convex surface: the rays from even a distant point are made to diverge by the lens. This lens is equivalent to a purely concave lens of a very large circle.

For let **G** be a distant point; therefore, its rays **GC** and **GD** are parallel according to Proposition XXIII. Therefore, they converge inside the body as **CE** and **DF** according to Proposition XXXIV, as if they were going to meet at one and a half radii <note>original: "sesquidiametro"</note> of the convexity at **HH**. But if a smaller circle were drawn through **E** with center **B**, then **EB** and **FB** would intercept a larger portion of that circle than **CD** does in respect to its own circle. This is evident: for since **CE** tends toward **HH**, point **E** is lower than the line **CB**. Yet it is **CB** (and not **CHH**) that would ultimately cut off similar portions. Therefore, **EF** will be an even larger portion of its circle when its center is above **B**, such as at **A**. Because the portion **EF** is larger than **CD**, the inclination of **CE** to **EF** is also greater than its inclination to **CD**. Thus, there is a greater refraction outward at **E**, according to Proposition II, than there was inward at **C** toward **BDG**. Therefore, **GC** and **EH** are not parallel. And since **GC** and **GD** are posited as parallel, their refracted rays **DB** and **EH** diverge at the concave boundary **EF** of the dense body.

# PROPOSITION CXXXIV.

Pure lenses of different types, when associated and touching one another, are equivalent to a lens of a mixed type, and ultimately to a pure lens.

This is demonstrated much like Proposition CXXV. For let there be a convex lens **OP** and a concave lens **QR**, and let both convex surfaces of **OP** be reduced into a single convex surface **ST** according to Proposition LXXIX.

By Proposition CXXVI, let the cavities of **QR** also be reduced into a single one, **VX**, and let a lens of mixed type <note>original: "mixti generis"; a meniscus lens</note> be formed, **STXV**. If the cavity **VX** prevails—that is, if its circle is smaller—the mixed lens is equivalent to a purely concave lens according to Proposition CXXXIII. And accordingly, **OP** and **QR**, being of different types joined together, are equivalent to a purely <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "cava" suggests "purely concave."</note>

<vocab>lenses, convex lens, concave lens, refraction, is equivalent, cavity, rays, convexity, mixed-type lens</vocab>

...of the cavity <note>original: "cavæ"; the concave side</note> of a very large circle. But if the convexity **ST** should prevail, because of its smaller circle <note>In historical optics, a "smaller circle" refers to a sharper curvature with a shorter radius, while a "larger circle" is a flatter, shallower curve</note>, as in the diagram for proposition 131: in a meniscus, **ABC** being the greater convexity and **ADC** the lesser cavity, then the mixed lens <term>mixed lens: a meniscus lens combining convex and concave surfaces</term> **SX**, and likewise even two lenses joined together, **OP** and **QR**, are equivalent to a purely convex lens according to proposition 130.

To prepare an instrument with a convex lens of a large circle <note>a lens with a shallow curve and long focal length</note> that is shorter than the expectation of those who manufacture common ones.

## PROBLEM CXXXVI.

To prepare an instrument with a concave lens of a large circle (and one that even exceeds the circle of the convex lens) which represents visible objects as larger than the expectation of those who manufacture common instruments.

*This is done by using a doubled concave lens in place of one, which the observer is unaware of, according to Proposition 127.*

## PROBLEM CXXXVII.

Using a convex lens of a small circle <note>a sharply curved lens</note>, even smaller than the circle of the concave lens at the eye (which seems absurd, according to Proposition 107), to create a very long instrument and present huge visible objects.

*Either combine, with a certain precise adjustment, a convex lens of a smaller circle with a concave one of a larger circle hidden and inconspicuous inside, and the effect will follow according to Proposition 134. Or use a mixed lens, convex on the outside with a smaller circle and concave on the inside with a larger one, according to Proposition 130. And seek the placement for the second concave lens, which is to be applied to the eye, according to Proposition 131. Also try something according to Proposition 128.*

### PROPOSITION 138.

While maintaining the same distance of the lens from the eye, and with the line from the eye to the optical center <note>original: "umbilicum"; literally 'navel', referring here to the lens's focal or geometric center</note> passing through the centers of the curvatures—whether they be convexities or cavities—the refractions remain nearly the same regardless of which of the two different surfaces of the lens you choose to turn toward the eye.

*This seems absurd and contrary to Propositions 34 and 35. For in the diagram for Proposition 34, the convex surface of a dense medium, **BCD**, when turned toward parallel rays, brings them together at point **F** at an interval of one and a half diameters <term>sesquidiametri: a distance 1.5 times the diameter of the circle forming the lens's curve</term>. But in Proposition 35, the dense surface **PQR**, when turned away from the parallel rays, brings them together at point **S** at an interval of one diameter. However, you must remember that the discussion there concerned a single surface, whereas any lens necessarily has two. Likewise, according to Proposition 34, the parallel rays are considered as being in the air, while in Proposition 35, they are considered as being inside the dense body; therefore, they cannot be compared. But if each of those lenses were bounded by a second surface in such a way that the points of convergence **F** and **S** remained, the difference would become apparent. For with center **F**, at the distance **FB**, let a portion of a circle **BKD** be drawn for the second surface, intersecting the axis **IAF** at **K**, and the first surface **BCD** at points **B** and **D**; in this way, the rays converging at **F** are all perpendicular to **BKD** itself...*

On the right, a smaller, narrower ray diagram shows light passing through a surface labeled P, Q, R, V and converging to a focal point S. <meta>This page continues the technical geometric proof regarding the focal points of lenses with different thicknesses and surface orientations, specifically resolving the apparent contradictions between the single-surface calculations of Propositions 34 and 35 and the behavior of two-surfaced lenses.</meta>

...to the surface, and therefore they are not refracted at all at *BKD*. In the other diagram for Proposition 35, let the arc *PQR* be equal and similar to the arc *BCD*, with *Q* as its midpoint; let the endpoints *PR* be connected by a straight line cutting the perpendicular *OQS* at *V*. This line represents the other flat surface of the lens, upon which all rays parallel to *OP* fall perpendicularly. Therefore, at *PVR*, they will not be refracted at all, and the point of meeting will remain at *S*. 

Now it is clear that lenses—where one causes parallel rays to meet at an interval of one and a half diameters <term>sesquidiametro: a distance 1.5 times the diameter of the circle defining the lens's curve</term> *CF*, and the other at a single diameter *QS*—are of different thicknesses, even though they are similar and equal in their convexity. The former has a smaller thickness *CK*, while the latter has a greater thickness *QV*. The difference between the two is the versed sine <term>sinus versus: the vertical distance between the center of an arc and the center of its chord; essentially the "height" of the lens's curve</term> of the arc *BK*. Therefore, it is no wonder that in the former, the interval of the meeting point consists of three semidiameters <note>original: "semidiametros"; radii or half-diameters</note>, while in the latter it has only two—following Proposition 131. 

The truth of the proposition will also shine light in this way. In the diagram for Proposition 34, let the circles *BCD* and *BKD* be turned away from the parallel rays, while points *B* and *D* remain fixed. In this way, the parallel rays first fall into the dense cavity *BKD*; they will then diverge through the body of the lens toward the convex surface *BCD* through *XC*. However, if the rays had remained parallel through the body, as in the diagram for Proposition 35, they would have met after the convex surface at a distance of two semidiameters, according to Proposition 35. But because they diverge toward *BCD* (just as if they were converging in the other diagram toward *PQR*), it is therefore right that they should meet further away than *S*, following the rule of Proposition 11—specifically at point *F*. 

The same is also easy to prove in the diagram for Proposition 35. For if *PQR* is turned toward the parallel rays, they will converge inside the body, as if they intended to meet after three semidiameters, as in the case of *BCD* toward *F*. Therefore, by converging as they pass through the body and falling onto its flat boundary, they are inclined toward it; thus, they are now also refracted at the flat surface, each moving away from the perpendicular of its point of entry. And since, with respect to the whole lens, they turn away both from each other within the body and from their own perpendiculars, once refracted outside in the air, they come together all the more, each one fleeing from its own perpendicular. And so, it is no wonder that they come together sooner,

### CXXXIX. PROBLEM.

*How to construct a telescope so that both lenses are concave—both the one toward the eye and the one toward the visible object—and yet the telescopic effect still follows.*

*Either place on the outside, toward the visible object, instead of a single convex lens, a visible concave space into which a convex lens of a much smaller curvature* <note>original: "angustioris circuli"; literally a narrower circle, meaning a smaller radius of curvature</note> *is secretly attached on the inside, as described in Proposition 137. Or use a "mixed" lens* <term>mixto: a meniscus lens, which has one convex side and one concave side</term> *there, as in Proposition 137, with its concave side turned outward. For by Proposition 138, the result is the same however you turn it.*

### CXL.

*How to prepare a telescope tube in which both lenses are convex—both the one toward the eye and the one toward the visible object—so that the telescopic effect nevertheless follows.*

*At the eye-piece, instead of a single concave lens, combine a convex lens with a concave lens of a smaller curvature. Arrange it so that the convex lens of the larger curvature* <note>original: "magni circuli"; a "large circle" implies a flatter, less powerful curve</note> *is seen on the outside by the eye, while the concave lens remains hidden inside, according to Proposition 134. Or, use a mixed lens at the eye-piece, whose convexity of a large circle projects outward, and whose cavity of a small—and much smaller—circle projects inward, according to Proposition 133.*

## CVI. NOTE.

Regarding what Giambattista della Porta <note>Giambattista della Porta (1535–1615) was a famous Italian polymath and author of "Natural Magic." He was known for his secretive and sometimes hyperbolic writing on optics.</note> claims—that he first collects the sun's rays and, once collected, sends them out to an infinite distance to cause burning—even though he speaks of mirrors, it seems it should be understood as referring to lenses <term>perspicillis: a term used in the 17th century for lenses or early telescopes</term>, because he deliberately hid his true meaning. 

But if this must be understood as referring to lenses, the technique would be nothing else than first collecting many rays with a convex lens, and then, just before the point of convergence, intercepting them with a concave lens, which makes the converging rays parallel, as stated in Proposition 105. Therefore, see those things that were said to the contrary in Proposition 56. To which I now add this further point: even if you were to correct Porta’s words regarding an "infinite burning line" to mean the same thing as a burning cone <term>conus ustorius: the cone-shaped path of light that tapers to a focal point for the purpose of igniting a material</term>, extended as far as you wish, so that still through the intersection of the rays burn-

<meta>This page transitions from the general physics of lenses to the specific anatomy of the human eye, applying the previously discussed optical propositions to biological structures to explain how the eye might focus.</meta>

## Regarding Proposition LXIV, page 27.

In **Anatomical Plate 49** of the distinguished Felix Plater <note>Felix Plater (1536–1614) was a pioneering Swiss physician whose anatomical drawings were highly influential in the early 17th century</note>, which is inserted on page 177 of my own *Supplements to Witelo* <note>original: "Vitellionem Paralipomenon," referring to Kepler's 1604 work *Ad Vitellionem Paralipomena*, which founded the modern science of optics</note>, or the *Optical Part of Astronomy*, the structure of the **spider’s web** <term>Tela aranea: a historical term for the capsule of the lens or the suspensory ligaments (ciliary zonules) that hold the lens in place, so named because of their delicate, web-like appearance</term> appears at figure X. In the middle of this structure is suspended the **crystalline humor** <term>cryſtallinus humor: the lens of the eye</term>, the true shape of which is shown in figure XIII, and its position in the eye in figure I at the letter **a**: where the rays of the "spider's web" are represented by **K K**. The ends of these rays, which are surrounded by a circle in figure X, should be imagined as being continuous with the interior of the **uveal tunic** <term>tunica uvea: the middle, pigmented layer of the eye, which includes the iris and the choroid</term>.

Thus, in figure VII, you see that tunic turned inside out, and at the letters **o. o.**, the traces of those rays that have been torn away from the uveal tunic. In the same place, the letter **n** indicates the **pupil**. Since, therefore, both this tunic and the aforementioned rays of the spider’s web are made of the same substance and are bodies continuous with one another—and are also of the same black color—it is highly probable that the nature of their movement is the same for both. There is, however, a natural contraction <note>original: "conniventia"</note> of the parts around **n**, or a contrary expansion.

Therefore, it also seems natural that the rays in figure X, by sometimes retreating into themselves, are shortened; and thus the circle which surrounds them—and its traces at **o. o.** in figure VII—is narrowed. At the same time, the crystalline lens at **o. o.** is moved further away from the **back** <term>fundus: the back of the eye, specifically the retina where the image is formed</term> at **p**. On the contrary, by the stretching out of the rays in figure X (which happens by the thinning of each individual ray), the circle enclosing their ends is expanded, representing the traces of the torn rays above **o. o.** in figure VII. By this reasoning, it happens that as the circle **o. o.** is expanded, the back **p** is drawn closer to the crystalline lens suspended in the middle of the circle **o. o.**

An explanation of the remaining parts of the eye, which is useful for Propositions 60, 61, 62, 63, and 64, should be sought from Plater and my *Optical Part of Astronomy*.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of lens combinations, specifically addressing how a convex lens (the objective) and a concave lens (the eyepiece) work together to correct vision and magnify images, which is the basis for the Galilean telescope. The text begins mid-sentence from page 56.</meta>

...to the concavity of this one, then the lenses joined immediately would almost neutralize each other and one would abolish the action of the other. Thus, the eye struggling with <term>confusion</term><note>In this context, "confusion" refers to blurred vision or the inability to focus on distant objects.</note> in seeing distant things would have no remedy, or a very poor one, placed in the excess of the other lens. But if this continuous lens were separated from the concave one, the rays incident on the concave lens would converge even more; thus, they could no longer even be made parallel by the concave lens, let alone divergent. The same applies much more to a convex lens of a smaller <term>circle</term><note>The "circle" refers to the radius of curvature of the lens surface; a smaller circle means a more steeply curved, powerful lens.</note>. Therefore, the convexity of a larger circle remains suitable.

Finally, I say that the <term>appearance of visible things</term><note>original: "speciem visibilium"</note> is increased if the circle of convexity is larger. For, according to Proposition 80, a solitary <term>convex lens</term> increases visible things. And although, according to Proposition 96, a <term>concave lens</term> even by itself diminishes visible things—and it is true that both the convex lens and what is seen through it are larger if the convex lens is alone than if a concave one is interposed—nevertheless, according to Propositions 22 and 98, this increase and this decrease are greater at farther distances. Since, therefore, the concave lens is near the eye, its diminution will be almost nothing; and since the convex lens is removed further from the eye, its increase will be greater.

### PROPOSITION 108.
**With a convex lens placed at any distance from the eye, any concave lens which, when applied alone to the eye, provides confused visible things, and which is of a smaller circle than that used by the convex lens, will—at a certain distance and position between the eye and the convex lens—exhibit distinct visible things.**

This is almost the converse of the previous proposition, but more free. For there, the position of the concave lens was given as being very close to the eye, and therefore...

<summary>This page explains why a telescope magnifies: the objective lens (far from the eye) provides significant magnification, while the eyepiece (close to the eye) provides minimal shrinking, resulting in a net increase in size. It also introduces Proposition 108 regarding the positioning of lenses to achieve focus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of lens placement in a telescope-like system, specifically focusing on how the positioning of a concave lens relative to the focal point of a convex lens affects vision for different types of eyes.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>

...only one, whereas on the contrary, the position of the convex lens could be chosen. Here now, the position of the convex lens is given, but not just one; rather, it is manifold in both quantity and quality, and in turn, the position of the concave lens can be chosen.

First, let this be the quality of the convex lens's position: that the eye is located *within* the point of convergence <note>The "point of convergence" (*punctum concursus*) refers to the focal point where light rays meet after passing through a lens.</note>. In this case, there is a closer relationship between this proposition and the previous one, and it is specific to the investigation of the eyepiece tube.

Therefore, in the next diagram (repeated from Proposition 70), the position of the concave lens and the eye will be between the convex lens **AB** and the points of convergence **D** and **F**. Let it be at **IG**. There will then be a certain measure of convergence for rays **AI** and **HG** at the angle **IFG**. To prevent this convergence from hindering distant vision, it must either be removed entirely for a <term>presbyopic eye</term> <note>original Greek: *presbytou*; literally "of an old man," referring to an eye that requires parallel rays to see clearly at a distance</note>, so that the rays become parallel, or a degree of divergence must additionally be introduced for a <term>nearsighted eye</term> <note>original Greek: *myops*; an eye that requires diverging rays to focus correctly</note>. And yet, according to Proposition 104, both can be achieved by a concave lens placed at some point before the points of convergence. It is demonstrated in Proposition 107 that this lens must be "hollowed" <note>concave</note> from a smaller circle than that used by the convex lens <note>In 17th-century optics, a "smaller circle" refers to a tighter radius of curvature, making the lens more powerful.</note>. Furthermore, a concave lens applied by itself close to the eye must produce blurred vision. This is because whatever remedies the blurriness caused by a convex lens must also produce blurriness from the opposite cause.

Secondly, let the quality of the eye's position be such that it is placed *outside* the points of convergence—for instance, if in the next diagram (repeated from Propositions 70 and 75) it were at **OP**, beyond **D** and **F**. In that case, a concave lens applied before the point of convergence **D** or **F** can, according to Proposition 104, ensure that no convergence occurs, but rather that the rays diverge again, and thus reach the eye at **OP**. However, in this case, many circumstances are required. First, the concave lens must be made from a small circle <note>i.e., it must have a sharp curvature</note>. For if it were from a large circle, all the rays between **AD** and **BD** would only strike a small portion of it near the perpendicular line; therefore, the refraction would have little effect, and certainly not enough to cancel out the convergence. This requirement is common to this case and the previous one. Secondly, if the lens is concave from a small circle so that it can induce divergence, it still does not send all those diverging rays to the eye...

<summary>This page explores the mathematical and physical requirements for combining convex and concave lenses to correct vision, distinguishing between the needs of presbyopic and nearsighted eyes. It explains how the curvature and placement of a concave lens can stop light rays from converging too early or redirect them to suit the observer's eye.</summary>
